abstract_pt: A autorregulação da aprendizagem é um arcabouço conceptual da psicologia
  cognitiva que se relaciona com a capacidade de reconhecimento e controlo sobre os
  múltiplos recursos mentais envolvidos na aprendizagem. Esta habilidade é importante
  em múltiplos domínios da educação, o que também inclui a programação. Estudantes
  que apresentam menor capacidade de autorregulação podem ter maior dificuldade na
  criação de código, correção de erros de programação e manutenção da motivação perante
  dificuldades.Os investigadores têm desenvolvido ambientes computacionais para estimular
  processos regulatórios como forma de apoiar a aprendizagem. Apesar de ser um campo
  de investigação consolidado em outras áreas, ainda não é amplamente explorado na
  educação em programação. Como consequência, existem lacunas sobre como introduzir
  o suporte regulatório em consonância com as especificidades deste tipo de aprendizagem.
  Outra limitação identificada no estado atual da arte é o foco na autorregulação
  como um processo individual, isolado de um contexto social. Esta perspetiva minimiza
  a influência que as interações sociais podem incidir na autorregulação, bem como
  desconsidera cenários de regulação coletiva em atividades colaborativas.Esta pesquisa
  de doutoramento centra-se nas lacunas apresentadas, a partir de uma investigação
  sobre como promover estímulos regulatórios em práticas individuais e coletivas de
  aprendizagem de programação. Este processo investigativo culmina com a proposição
  de um ambiente de aprendizagem colaborativa mediada pelo computador que integra
  construtos teóricos da psicologia cognitiva e da educação em programação. As evidências
  obtidas reforçam a eficácia dos estímulos regulatórios como uma estratégia pedagógica
  e destaca o seu potencial para auxiliar os estudantes nas suas jornadas de aprendizagem.
  Esta contribuição não só amplia o entendimento sobre como é possível integrar construtos
  teóricos da autorregulação da aprendizagem e adaptá-los às especificidades da área
  de programação, como também oferece orientações para pesquisas futuras em educação
  em programação interessadas em utilizar a autorregulação como base teórica.Esta
  tese não se limitou ao desenvolvimento do ambiente descrito, como também adotou
  métodos qualitativos para compreender como o processo de autorregulação ocorre na
  área de programação. A análise das estratégias de regulação utilizadas por estudantes
  de programação amplia o entendimento sobre os recursos mentais por eles utilizados
  e serve como base para compreender como ocorre a aprendizagem. Também se procurou
  investigar a perceção dos alunos sobre o uso do ambiente de programação proposto,
  sendo os seus relatos valiosos para compreender os fatores que favorecem ou dificultam
  o seu uso. Este conhecimento oferece diretrizes para o desenho futuro de intervenções,
  a partir de elementos que precisam ser ponderados neste processo a fim de não comprometer
  os estímulos regulatórios. Por fim, as revisões da literatura realizadas contribuem
  para uma sumarização do estado atual da arte, consolidam descobertas e apontam lacunas
  que merecem mais investigação.

  that relates to the ability to recognize and control the multiple mental resources
  involved in learning. This skill is important in multiple domains of education,
  including programming. Students who have lower self-regulation abilities may have
  greater difficulty in coding, correcting programming errors, and maintaining motivation
  in the face of challenges.Researchers have developed computational environments
  to stimulate regulatory processes as a resource to support learning. Despite being
  a consolidated research field in other areas, it is still underexplored in programming
  education. As a result, there are gaps in how to introduce regulatory support in
  accordance with the specificities of programming learning. Another limitation identified
  in the current state of the art is the focus on self-regulation as an individual
  process, isolated from social context. This perspective minimizes the social interaction
  influence on the regulatory processes and disregards scenarios of socially shared
  regulation of learning during collaborative learning.This doctoral research focuses
  on the identified gaps, through an investigation into how to promote regulatory
  stimuli in individual and collective programming learning practices. This investigative
  process culminates in the proposition of a computer-supported collaborative learning
  environment that integrates theoretical constructs from cognitive psychology and
  programming education. The evidence gathered reinforces the effectiveness of regulatory
  stimuli as a pedagogical strategy and highlights their potential to assist students
  in their learning journeys. This contribution not only expands the understanding
  of how to integrate theoretical constructs of self-regulated learning and adapt
  them to the specificities of the programming area, but also provides guidance for
  future research in programming education interested in using self-regulation as
  a theoretical basis.This thesis went beyond the development of the described environment,
  also adopting qualitative methods to understand how the self-regulation process
  occurs in programming education. The analysis of the regulation strategies used
  by programming students enhances the understanding of the mental resources they
  utilize and serves as a basis to comprehend how learning occurs. Additionally, efforts
  were made to investigate students' perception of the proposed programming environment
  use, with their feedback being valuable for understanding the factors that favor
  or hinder its use. This knowledge provides guidelines for the future design of interventions,
  based on elements that need to be considered in this process to avoid compromising
  regulatory stimuli. Finally, the literature reviews conducted summarize the current
  state of the art, consolidating findings and pointing out gaps that deserve further
  investigation.

# A Computer-Supported Collaborative Learning Environment to Foster the Regulation of Learning Skills in Programming Education

Leonardo Soares e Silva

LEARNING ENVIRONMENT TO FOSTER THE

PROGRAMMING EDUCATION

Professor António José Mendes and Professor Anabela Jesus Gomes, and presented
at Department of Informatics in Engineering of the Faculty of Sciences and
Technology from the University of Coimbra.

ix

Abstract
Self-regulated learning is a conceptual framework in cognitive psychology that
relates to the ability to recognize and control the multiple mental resources in-
volved in learning. This skill is important in multiple domains of education, in-
cluding programming. Students who have lower self-regulation abilities may
have greater difficulty in coding, correcting programming errors, and maintain-
ing motivation in the face of challenges.
Researchers have developed computational environments to stimulate regulatory
processes as a resource to support learning. Despite being a consolidated research
field in other areas, it is still underexplored in programming education. As a
result, there are gaps in how to introduce regulatory support in accordance with
the specificities of programming learning. Another limitation identified in the
current state of the art is the focus on self-regulation as an individual process,
isolated from social context. This perspective minimizes the social interaction
influence on the regulatory processes and disregards scenarios of socially shared
regulation of learning during collaborative learning.
This doctoral research focuses on the identified gaps, through an investigation
into how to promote regulatory stimuli in individual and collective program-
ming learning practices. This investigative process culminates in the proposition
of a computer-supported collaborative learning environment that integrates the-
oretical constructs from cognitive psychology and programming education. The
evidence gathered reinforces the effectiveness of regulatory stimuli as a pedagog-
ical strategy and highlights their potential to assist students in their learning jour-
neys. This contribution not only expands the understanding of how to integrate
theoretical constructs of self-regulated learning and adapt them to the specifici-
ties of the programming area, but also provides guidance for future research in
programming education interested in using self-regulation as a theoretical basis.
This thesis went beyond the development of the described environment, also
adopting qualitative methods to understand how the self-regulation process oc-
curs in programming education. The analysis of the regulation strategies used by
programming students enhances the understanding of the mental resources they
utilize and serves as a basis to comprehend how learning occurs. Additionally,
efforts were made to investigate students’ perception of the proposed program-
ming environment use, with their feedback being valuable for understanding the
factors that favor or hinder its use. This knowledge provides guidelines for the
future design of interventions, based on elements that need to be considered in
this process to avoid compromising regulatory stimuli. Finally, the literature re-
views conducted, summarize the current state of the art, consolidating findings
and pointing out gaps that deserve further investigation.
xi

Keywords
Computer-based learning environment; co-regulation; programming learning;
self-regulation of learning; socially shared regulation of learning.
xii

Resumo
A autorregulação da aprendizagem é um arcabouço conceptual da psicologia
cognitiva que se relaciona com a capacidade de reconhecimento e controlo so-
bre os múltiplos recursos mentais envolvidos na aprendizagem. Esta habilidade
é importante em múltiplos domínios da educação, o que também inclui a progra-
mação. Estudantes que apresentam menor capacidade de autorregulação podem
ter maior dificuldade na criação de código, correção de erros de programação e
manutenção da motivação perante dificuldades.
Os investigadores têm desenvolvido ambientes computacionais para estimular
processos regulatórios como forma de apoiar a aprendizagem. Apesar de ser um
campo de investigação consolidado em outras áreas, ainda não é amplamente
explorado na educação em programação. Como consequência, existem lacunas
sobre como introduzir o suporte regulatório em consonância com as especifici-
dades deste tipo de aprendizagem. Outra limitação identificada no estado atual
da arte é o foco na autorregulação como um processo individual, isolado de um
contexto social. Esta perspetiva minimiza a influência que as interações sociais
podem incidir na autorregulação, bem como desconsidera cenários de regulação
coletiva em atividades colaborativas.
Esta pesquisa de doutoramento centra-se nas lacunas apresentadas, a partir de
uma investigação sobre como promover estímulos regulatórios em práticas indi-
viduais e coletivas de aprendizagem de programação. Este processo investigativo
culmina com a proposição de um ambiente de aprendizagem colaborativa medi-
ada pelo computador que integra construtos teóricos da psicologia cognitiva e da
educação em programação. As evidências obtidas reforçam a eficácia dos estímu-
los regulatórios como uma estratégia pedagógica e destaca o seu potencial para
auxiliar os estudantes nas suas jornadas de aprendizagem. Esta contribuição não
só amplia o entendimento sobre como é possível integrar construtos teóricos da
autorregulação da aprendizagem e adaptá-los às especificidades da área de pro-
gramação, como também oferece orientações para pesquisas futuras em educação
em programação interessadas em utilizar a autorregulação como base teórica.
Esta tese não se limitou ao desenvolvimento do ambiente descrito, como também
adotou métodos qualitativos para compreender como o processo de autorregu-
lação ocorre na área de programação. A análise das estratégias de regulação uti-
lizadas por estudantes de programação amplia o entendimento sobre os recursos
mentais por eles utilizados e serve como base para compreender como ocorre a
aprendizagem. Também se procurou investigar a perceção dos alunos sobre o uso
do ambiente de programação proposto, sendo os seus relatos valiosos para com-
preender os fatores que favorecem ou dificultam o seu uso. Este conhecimento
oferece diretrizes para o desenho futuro de intervenções, a partir de elementos
que precisam ser ponderados neste processo a fim de não comprometer os estí-
mulos regulatórios. Por fim, as revisões da literatura realizadas contribuem para
uma sumarização do estado atual da arte, consolidam descobertas e apontam la-
cunas que merecem mais investigação.

Palavras-Chave
Ambientes computacionais de aprendizagem; aprendizagem de programação;
autorregulação da aprendizagem; co-regulação; regulação social da aprendiza-
gem.
xiv

Publications
The contributions of this thesis resulted in the following publications in interna-
tional peer-reviewed conferences and journals, alongside with the participation
in doctoral consortiums. Moreover, the insights gained throughout the doctoral
journey have also played a pivotal role in authoring an introductory program-
ming book. This book integrates concepts of self-regulation with innovative ped-
agogical approaches to support programming learning.

Chapter 1
Introduction
The demand for software developers is rising in various countries. It reflects an
increased dependence of individuals and companies on technological resources,
including software [Anand, 2023]. As a result, there has been a significant in-
crease in interest in computing courses, particularly those focused on software
development [Herzog, 2021].
To address the higher demand for software developers, educational institutions
must mitigate the challenges commonly observed in introductory programming
courses. This is necessary to reduce dropouts and failure rates, which can be high
in this educational domain [Qian and Lehman, 2017]. This problem was observed
in the educational institution where the author of this doctoral thesis works as a
professor, with dropout and failure rates extremely alarming (above 50%) [Silva,
2018]. This observation motivated the development of this doctoral work, which
sought to investigate and develop strategies to support students’ programming
learning processes.
There are many factors associated with the difficulties in learning programming
[Gomes and Mendes, 2007a]. One of the factors refers to students’ difficulties in
taking responsibility and controlling their learning process, characteristics that
indicate a lack of self-regulation of learning (SRL) skills [Zimmerman, 1990]. SRL
is a psychological construct identified to be a crucial element for successful learn-
ing in diverse educational contexts, including programming education [Loksa
et al., 2022].
SRL represents a set of multifaceted skills that involves the students’ control and
management of cognitive, metacognitive, motivational, emotional, and behav-
ioral processes for achieving educational objectives [Zimmerman and Schunk,
2011]. Self-regulatory behavior manifests through the combination of these psy-
chological processes, whether by defining how a task will be performed (cog-
nition), reviewing and recognizing limitations on a subject (metacognition), re-
maining persistent in achieving educational goals (motivation), and avoiding dis-
tractions that could hinder learning (behavioral). These actions are some exam-
ples of learning strategies that students can adopt to improve their performance
and are termed self-regulatory when the student has an interest in controlling
their learning process. [Dignath and Büttner, 2008].
1

Chapter 1
Students who consistently exhibit regulatory abilities are more likely to achieve
academic success [Winne et al., 2013]. Researchers have highlighted the connec-
tion between SRL skills and programming success [Kumar et al., 2005]. For ex-
ample, Loksa and Ko [2016] found that students exhibiting regulatory abilities
managed programming exercises with higher success. Conversely, the absence of
these abilities was associated with difficulties in completing programming exer-
cises and lower programming learning performance [Falkner et al., 2015]. Due to
the association between SRL and learning, this theory has attracted the attention
of researchers across various educational fields, who have utilized SRL as a foun-
dational framework to study learning processes and devise interventions aimed
at enhancing learning [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023].
Students may have limitations in self-regulating their learning process, which
can negatively impact their educational achievements [Zimmerman and Schunk,
2011]. These difficulties can impact the entire learning process, leading to stu-
dents not employing resources that could aid them in a specific subject (e.g., tak-
ing notes, highlighting important parts of a text) or even being able to recognize
their own limitations and areas needing improvement. These issues can make
the student oblivious to their own learning, losing autonomy by not being able to
adopt corrective measures that could assist them [Winne et al., 2013].
Novice programming students often lack self-regulatory skills that could impact
their code development process [Falkner et al., 2014; Ferreira and Campos, 2023].
For example, students often neglect to use planning actions before and during
constructing programming codes, which in many situations leads to a misinter-
pretation of the problem or incorrect choices of the programming resources that
would be used in the construction of the computational solution [Loksa and Ko,
2016]. The absence of these cognitive strategies may suggest that the student has
difficulties using these resources, but it could also indicate that they do not rec-
ognize their importance for learning. In both cases, some form of intervention is
needed to support the student [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013].
A significant part of the research based on the self-regulation of learning seeks
to design resources to support students in regulating their learning effectively
[Zheng, 2016a]. Regulatory stimuli are known as scaffolding and proved effec-
tive in supporting learning across numerous educational contexts [Zheng, 2016a].
Scaffoldings have been designed for Computer-based Learning Environments
(CBLEs) in various learning domains [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013], and use vari-
ous computational resources (e.g., graphics, connectivity, databases) to stimulate
the execution of regulatory strategies that may facilitate knowledge acquisition
[Theobald, 2021].
The concept of scaffolding originates from Vygotsky’s theory of the zone of prox-
imal development [Azevedo and Hadwin, 2005]. Scaffolding refers to the assis-
tance provided to students, enabling them to complete tasks that would be chal-
lenging to achieve independently [Wang et al., 2023]. To illustrate, Loksa et al.
[2016] developed a problem-solving guideline illustrating how students could
navigate through the phases of the problem-solving process during code devel-
opment. In this scenario, the guideline serves as a scaffold, guiding students to
engage in an action that is acknowledged to be effective for learning. The us-
2

Introduction
age of scaffoldings to stimulate regulatory processes is an area of interest for this
thesis, as one of our goals was to create scaffolding adapted to the context of
programming learning.
There is an emerging interest in the use of regulatory scaffoldings to support pro-
gramming learning. Examples include providing learning metrics to aid students
in comprehending their progress during a programming course [Law et al., 2017],
and implementing learning journals where students can document their study
plans, evaluate their progress, and reflect on their learning processes [Alhazbi,
2014]. The findings from a systematic literature review highlight the potential
of regulatory scaffolding in supporting students’ programming learning [Silva
et al., 2021b].
Despite the advancements, the research on the use of regulatory scaffoldings in
programming education is still underexplored [Loksa et al., 2022]. To understand
the limitations of the current literature, a systematic literature review was con-
ducted and published in [Silva et al., 2021b]. The findings indicated a limited
number of studies that used SRL as a foundational theory for the design of their
educational software. In addition, the studies mostly used a specific format of
regulatory scaffolding that supports general-purpose regulatory processes. These
processes receive that name because their occurrence is externalized across vari-
ous educational areas. Despite their relevance for programming learning, there is
emerging evidence suggesting that regulatory strategies specialized to program-
ming have a greater contribution to learning [Falkner et al., 2015]. Thus, there
is a research opportunity to investigate how to design scaffolding to stimulate
programming-specific processes and assess their effectiveness to support learn-
ing.
The literature review also revealed that most studies conceptualized the regula-
tion of learning as an individual process, technically defined as self-regulation
of learning, and the focus is on the students solely regulating their own mental
processes [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. However, most recent literature in-
dicates that social interactions, commonly present in educational environments,
have a significant influence on the modulation and execution of individuals’ reg-
ulatory processes [Järvelä et al., 2019]. Additionally, collective goal achievement
seems also influenced by the joint regulatory capabilities of individuals working
in groups [Malmberg et al., 2017]. Thus, another research opportunity emerges to
investigate the design of regulatory scaffoldings to support regulation of learning
during collaborative programming learning.
The influence of the social context on the regulatory process is examined through
the psychological constructs of Socially Shared Regulation of Learning (SSRL)
and co-regulation [Järvelä et al., 2016]. SSRL pertains to regulatory episodes
within groups of individuals who collaboratively negotiate and deliberate to at-
tain a shared goal. On the other hand, co-regulation refers to the regulatory sup-
port that arises from interpersonal interactions, often when one individual re-
ceives assistance from another (such as a professor or fellow student) to regulate
themselves.
In contrast to SRL, which has a well-established body of knowledge, research
3

Chapter 1
on SSRL and co-regulation has gained momentum in recent years [Järvelä et al.,
2019]. As a result, there is limited understanding of how to design scaffolding to
support the regulatory processes in social contexts and their influence on learn-
ing [Panadero and Järvelä, 2015]. Research on this theme in the programming
education context is even more scarce [Silva et al., 2021b].
For this study, no distinction was made between co-regulation and SSRL, as both
forms of regulation of learning stem from social interactions. Thus, through this
thesis, they are referred to as "social regulation", a framework other researchers
have also adopted [Hadwin et al., 2017]. Additionally, the term "regulation of
learning" will be used to represent both SRL and social regulation.
The theoretical basis presented thus far is central to this work. This thesis posi-
tions within a social-cognitive framework that acknowledges the importance of
mental processes, such as cognition and metacognition, in the learning process.
It also recognizes the role of social interactions in shaping the mental and behav-
ioral processes employed in learning.
In summary, the identified gaps point to the opportunity to investigate how to
design regulatory scaffoldings to support programming learning. In particular,
aiming to support programming-specific regulatory processes in individual and
collaborative contexts. This motivated the research conducted in this doctorate
that resulted in developing and evaluating a Computer-Support Collaborative
Learning (CSCL) system named Regulatory Support for Programming Education
(RESPE).
This doctorate work also seeks to provide contributions regarding the study of
the regulatory process in programming education. In this sense, statistical ev-
idence is presented on the association between the regulation of learning pro-
cesses (individually and collectively) and programming learning. This data is
necessary to increase the available evidence that helps researchers assess the im-
portance of this theory to programming education. Additionally, part of this doc-
torate’ scope involved investigating how programming students regulate them-
selves. This knowledge is crucial to understanding how regulatory processes
occur within this domain but remained little explored [Loksa et al., 2020].
The following research questions were developed to achieve this doctorate objec-
tives: RQ1) Which regulatory scaffoldings are used in programming education?
RQ2) How to structure collaborative activities in programming? RQ3) How can a
CSCL be designed to foster regulation of learning among programming students?
RQ4) How do students use and evaluate the RESPE’s regulatory scaffoldings?
RQ5) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit higher performance in individual
programming exercises than students who did not? RQ6) Do students who used
the RESPE exhibit higher performance in collaborative programming exercises
than students who did not? RQ7) Do students who used the RESPE demonstrate
higher regulatory skills development than students who did not? RQ8) What is
the association between SRL and programming learning? RQ9) What regulatory
strategies are used by programming students? RQ10) What is the psychometric
validity of self-regulation measurement instruments in the context of program-
ming education?
4

Introduction
These research questions position this study within an exploratory and explana-
tory framework. Multiple methods were used to answer the above research ques-
tions, including literature reviews, questionnaires, and a controlled experiment.
This doctorate yielded the following contributions:
i) A computer-supported collaborative learning environment was designed to
foster regulatory processes in programming education. The scaffoldings intro-
duced are designed to address gaps identified in the current literature, specif-
ically focusing on stimulating regulatory processes in programming education,
particularly during programming activities and within an individual and social
context;
ii) Provision of statistical evidence on the feasibility of supporting learning pro-
gramming through regulatory scaffolding;
iii) A model describing the resources that can be used to design regulatory scaf-
folding in programming education. This resource can support the design of fu-
ture scaffoldings;
iv) A conceptual framework describing how programming students regulate them-
selves. This framework aids in understanding the differences in learning strate-
gies between high and low-performing students and sheds light on the resources
students use to learn how to program.
v) Three systematic literature reviews of the existing literature related to: a) evi-
dence on the association between SRL and programming education; b) summary
of the interventions grounded on the SRL theory to support programming learn-
ing; and c) identification of how collaborative learning wa stimulated in program-
ming education. These reviews provide an in-depth overview of the research in
these fields, highlighting its advancements and identifying gaps.
This multifaceted set of contributions unfolds in different directions and points to
the complexities of studying self-regulation. It goes beyond the mere construction
of an educational technology, also seeking to understand the regulatory mecha-
nisms that influence the learning of programming. The challenges encountered
during the research are outlined, which can serve as a reference for designing
future educational software based on the principles of SRL theory.
This thesis is structured as follows: Section 2 presents the literature review on the
regulation of learning and programming education. The methodology (Section
3), describes the pedagogical context in which this research is situated, the re-
search questions, and methods, ending with a presentation on how the data was
collected and analyzed. The RESPE’s development process and its evaluation are
presented in Section 4. The complementary research that was performed and re-
lated to the SRL theory in programming education is presented in Section 5. A
discussion of the findings is presented in Section 6. Discoveries and implications
for teaching practice are also presented. Lastly, the main conclusions and venues
for future research are presented in Section 7.
5

Chapter 2
Literature review
This thesis resides at the intersection of programming education, regulation of
learning, and computer-supported collaborative learning. As a result, studies
within these domains, whether directly or indirectly related, are of great interest
to this work.
2.1 Programming education
Introductory programming courses are fundamental to many computing courses,
particularly software development-related ones. According to the Association for
Computing Machinery (ACM) curriculum propositions1, this subject usually en-
compasses programming fundamentals concepts, such as variables, input/out-
put, conditionals, loop structures, and functions. These topics provide a basis for
the more advanced software development subjects.
Many educators and students acknowledge that learning programming is a com-
plex and challenging process [Qian and Lehman, 2017]. This issue is reflected
in the significant failure rates present in programming courses [Bennedsen and
Caspersen, 2019]. Thus, researchers have been attracted by the importance of
better understanding the factors that influence programming learning, as well as
constructing resources that can support students and teachers in this process.
Various factors were identified to influence the programming learning experi-
ence. Examples include: educational infrastructure, characteristics of the pro-
gramming subject, teaching methodologies used by instructors, and the student’s
behaviors and used learning strategies [Becker and Quille, 2019]. Each area in-
fluences the learning process differently and holds varying degrees of control
throughout the educational journey.
Educational institutions are responsible for offering adequate infrastructure and
pedagogical support for students and educators. The lack of these resources (e.g.,
access to the Internet and computer availability) has been reported as a barrier
to students’ programming practice with consequences to their learning success
1https://www.acm.org/education/curricula-recommendations
7

Chapter 2
[Apiola et al., 2011]. This is an issue over which the educational institution has
strong control and can employ physical and financial resources to mitigate it.
The inherently abstract nature of programming can also present challenges to
the learning process [Volet and Lund, 1994]. The programming syntax is often
unfamiliar, difficult to master, and requires consistent practice for memorization
[Pears et al., 2007]. Moreover, certain concepts, such as functions, are abstract
and pose difficulties to their understanding [Lahtinen et al., 2005]. Some tools
have been developed to minimize these difficulties, such as the use of block-based
programming and visual programming resources that allow students to visualize
the execution of more abstract concepts, like recursion [Medeiros et al., 2019].
Some tools often used during programming practice are criticized due to their
inadequacy to learning [Denny et al., 2020]. Some of them were designed for pro-
fessional use and have complex interfaces, which could impose cognitive load
on learners and hinder their performance [Timmermann et al., 2016]. These as-
pects highlight unique programming elements that can contribute to its complex-
ity while pointing out the need to provide educational support to mitigate these
issues.
Finally, students have a significant role in achieving success in the educational
process [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. Their control of learning resources is
crucial, and within the context of this doctoral research, there is a specific focus
on exploring the cognitive and metacognitive processes integral to programming
education, given their critical impact on academic achievement [Schunk, 2008].
The subsequent sections delve into these essential psychological resources and
discuss their relationship with programming learning.
2.1.1 Cognitive processes
Cognitive processes encompass mental operations directly involved in learning,
including attention, memory, and critical thinking [Lishinski, 2016]. These pro-
cesses pertain to the mental capacities orchestrated by the brain’s prefrontal cor-
tex, enabling students to set goals, retain information, devise strategies, and ar-
range their resources to accomplish a task [Elbistan and Stubbe, 2021]. The set
of cognitive processes are named "executive functions" and play a vital role in
problem-solving processes [Drigas and Karyotaki, 2019], which are important in
programming learning [Kovari, 2020].
Multiple cognitive abilities are essential in contexts where students consistently
engage in problem-solving, such as programming [Robins, 2019]. The nature of
the programming field inherently requires the constant utilization of problem-
solving skills, as students need to comprehend the intricacies of the problems
they aim to solve and effectively apply programming resources to address them
[Gomes and Mendes, 2007b]. Evidence indicates that students with proper problem-
solving skills tend to excel in programming tasks [Veerasamy et al., 2018].
Several models have been developed to elucidate the problem-solving process.
The Polya’s linear problem-solving model is one of the most widely used, which
8

Literature review
delineates this process as a series of four actions: problem understanding, plan-
ning, execution, and monitoring [Elbistan and Stubbe, 2021]. Understanding the
intricacies of this process holds a significant influence in programming education
research as it helps understand the stages students experience during learning.
Gomes and Mendes [2007b] adapted Polya’s model to the context of program-
ming learning. They situate problem-solving as a sequence of the following
phases: understanding the problem, characterizing the problem, representing the prob-
lem, solving the problem, reflecting on the solution , and communicating the problem
solution. These phases connect general-purpose abilities (e.g., text interpretation),
with the programming specificities, such as using programming resources to cre-
ate computational solutions.
Polya’s model has been a valuable reference for designing interventions to sup-
port problem-solving skills. For instance, Loksa and Ko [2016] devised a con-
ceptual framework outlining various stages of problem-solving in programming
learning, such as problem understanding, searching for analogous solutions, and
reinterpreting problem prompts. The authors offered explicit instructions on ex-
ecuting these stages and provided contextual hints during programming activ-
ities to help students recognize their current stage. Using a different approach,
[Prather et al., 2019] focused on supporting only problem understanding, by ask-
ing students to solve a test case before coding. The authors hypothesized that
this prompt would enhance problem awareness and evaluation of students’s own
limitations.
Programming learning also encompasses other cognitive resources, including
memory and reasoning [Helmlinger et al., 2020]. McGill and Volet [1997] formu-
lated an argument around these resources, suggesting that programming knowl-
edge could be categorized into five domains: declarative-syntactic, declarative-
conceptual, procedural-syntactic, procedural-conceptual, and strategic knowledge.
Declarative-syntactic knowledge involves understanding the fundamental ele-
ments of a programming language, such as basic concepts and syntax. Declarative-
conceptual knowledge pertains to explaining code fragments’ functionality or
predicting algorithms’ output. Procedural-syntactic knowledge encompasses em-
ploying language syntax to construct syntactically correct programming state-
ments. Procedural-conceptual knowledge revolves around the capacity to de-
sign programming solutions. Lastly, strategic/conditional knowledge refers to
students’ capability to integrate prior knowledge effectively. These propositions
draw from the literature in cognitive psychology and distinguish between know-
ing what to do (e.g., how to use a specific resource) and when to do it (e.g., deter-
mining the appropriate timing for resource utilization).
Cognitive resources are vital during error correction, a typical task during code
development. During this process, students must decipher error messages gen-
erated by programming compilers, form hypotheses regarding the underlying
causes of their errors, and devise solutions to resolve them. These elements ne-
cessitate storing a significant amount of information in working memory and can
potentially overwhelm the student [Türkmen and Caner, 2020]. These challenges
are further compounded by inherent characteristics of the programming learning
environments, such as unfriendly error messages in languages that may differ
9

Chapter 2
from the student’s native language [Denny et al., 2020]. Thus, these limitations
can impair cognitive processes, adding cognitive load and hindering their perfor-
mance [Berssanette and de Francisco, 2022].
The abovementioned elements highlight the complexity of cognitive processes in
acquiring programming knowledge. In many cases, students exhibit some level
of cognitive difficulty, such as a low degree of problem-solving ability, and need
support to mitigate these problems [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. These aspects highlight
the importance of providing regulatory support to help students overcome their
cognitive difficulties.
2.1.2 Metacognitive knowledge and strategies
The previous section emphasized the significant role of cognitive processes in
programming learning. However, the effective utilization of cognitive resources
relies on students’ active control over them, a concept defined by educational
psychologists as metacognition [Livingston, 2003].
Metacognition is commonly described as "thinking about thinking" [Schunk, 2008].
It entails assessing one’s knowledge of cognitive processes and exerting control
over them during learning activities. For example, self-evaluating one’s com-
prehension of an educational task or competence to execute an action [Veenman
et al., 2006]. This conceptualization has significantly impacted educational re-
search over the last decades, drawing attention to a previously underexplored
aspect related to the students’ capacity to evaluate their own cognitive processes
[Eteläpelto, 1993].
The term metacognition is often attributed to John Flavell, who posited that this
process encompasses four key components: metacognitive knowledge, experi-
ences, goals, and strategies [Moritz and Lysaker, 2018]. Metacognitive knowledge
refers to an individual’s understanding of their own learning processes, enabling
them to assess actions that may hinder or facilitate their learning. Metacognitive
experiences involve conscious reflection on the learning process, such as judg-
ments about one’s study performance. Metacognitive goals and strategies stem
from the preceding components and guide students in their actions, such as set-
ting criteria for what constitutes satisfactory learning (metacognitive goals). At
the same time, self-assessment serves as an example of a metacognitive strategy
to determine whether the goal has been achieved or not.
Metacognitive and cognitive strategies are both essential components of the learn-
ing process [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. Understanding the difference be-
tween these two types of resources is necessary to recognizing their unique and
significant contributions to learning. Moritz and Lysaker [2018] describes a sce-
nario where a student highlighting important parts of a text represents a cog-
nitive strategy. In contrast, the same student evaluating their understanding of
the material and identifying areas of comprehension or difficulty exemplifies a
metacognitive strategy. Thus, cognition and metacognition represent intercon-
nected mental resources.
10

Literature review
Metacognition plays a pivotal role in programming learning as it is intricately
connected to problem-solving [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. The ability to decide on ef-
fective learning strategies when approaching a problem, self-monitor one’s per-
formance, and utilize debugging resources to correct errors are all examples of
metacognitive strategies relevant to problem-solving [Izzati and Mahmudi, 2018].
These abilities are integral to learning programming and underscore the impor-
tance of metacognition for successful learning outcomes [Loksa et al., 2022].
A previous systematic literature review explored the connection between differ-
ent psychological variables and programming learning, emphasizing the signifi-
cance of metacognitive strategies in the learning process [Silva et al., 2023d]. The
review found that students’ metacognitive abilities were more closely associated
with learning outcomes than motivational factors (such as self-efficacy) and cog-
nitive strategies.
The metacognitive process often represents a challenge for students [Eteläpelto,
1993]. It can have an impact on various stages of the learning process. For in-
stance, during the problem-solving stage, some students struggle to assess their
own understanding of the problem, leading to the development of incorrect con-
ceptual models [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. Error correction is also affected as stu-
dents need to resolve inconsistent mental models, which depend on their self-
evaluative capabilities [Prather et al., 2018]. To mitigate these problems, support
could be provided by incorporating regulatory scaffolding focused on metacog-
nitive stimuli into educational environments [Çakiro˘ glu and Er, 2020].
Multiple resources can be used to foster metacognition in programming edu-
cation. For example, Volet and Lund [1994] implemented a metacognitive and
cognitive training program. This training involved reflecting on the necessary
programming resources before code development, using pseudocode to create
algorithms, and employing debugging strategies to rectify programming errors.
The work presented by Marquès Puig et al. [2021] is another example, consisting
of a learning environment with automatic feedback to facilitate students’ self-
evaluation, a recognized metacognitive ability.
The provision of metacognitive support to enhance programming learning has re-
ceived significant attention in the literature and presents an opportunity to foster
mental processes relevant to learning in this context [Garcia et al., 2018a].
2.2 Regulation of learning
The ability to proactively manage mental resources (e.g., cognition and metacog-
nition) to achieve educational goals is crucial to learning success [Schunk and
Zimmerman, 2023]. This ability is known as Self-Regulation of Learning (SRL),
which refers to the capacity for an individual to self-control the mental resources
undertaken in the execution of a an educational task [Zimmerman and Schunk,
2011].
The process of self-regulation extends beyond the educational realm and has been
11

Chapter 2
studied in various other areas. In the health context, self-regulation is crucial,
as many health-related behaviors hinge on an individual’s ability to proactively
control their actions. For example, in the case of a smoker attempting to quit,
self-regulation is key. Such a person needs to self-regulate to steer clear of cer-
tain triggers and situations, which includes the application of cognitive control
to manage their focus and memory [Evans et al., 2018]. Evidence points out that
self-regulation represents an important ability necessary in contexts where self-
controlling and autonomy are crucial [Duckworth et al., 2009].
Through the self-regulatory process, students either intentionally or inadvertently
exercise control over mental processes, thereby aiming to improve their learning
capabilities. To illustrate, a self-regulated student is expected to select the most
appropriate set of problem-solving strategies for a given problem [Schunk, 2008].
Additionally, these students possess the ability to reflect on their performance,
enabling them to adjust their learning strategies for optimal results [Zimmerman
and Martinez-Pons, 1988]. Lastly, being capable of regulating their motivations
and emotions involves recognizing the significance of a task and maintaining
composure in situations that are beyond their control [Winne, 2005].
SRL has emerged as a key psychological construct encompassing multiple educa-
tional theories due to its ability to capture the multifaceted nature of the learning
process [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. To illustrate, these processes refer to
cognition, which relates to attention control and the definition of the strategies
needed to perform an educational task. Metacognitive processes are also present
and externalized through the monitoring and managing one’s own mental pro-
cesses. Behavioral management, is part of the SRL process and refers to the control
of one’s own actions towards improving task performance. Lastly, emotional and
motivational regulation is the management of one’s own affective state and use of
goal-orientation to perform the task effectively [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011].
A body of evidence indicates a strong association between self-regulatory abilities
and academic performance [Ergen and Kanadli, 2017]. This conjunction offers a
robust foundation for educational researchers to use this theory across diverse
contexts [Winne, 2005]. SRL is gaining recognition as a key theory for studying
how students learn to program [Loksa et al., 2022]. Researchers found an associa-
tion between SRL abilities (e.g., time-management, help-seeking) with academic
performance in this learning domain [Ramirez Echeverry et al., 2018; Silva et al.,
2023d]. Some SRL strategies, such as self-evaluation were predictors of academic
success [Hui and Umar, 2011], and the lack of regulatory skills hindered students
in monitoring their performances during programming activities, impacting their
abilities to evaluate the completeness of exercises and the distribution of time on
task [Prather et al., 2018].
The combined usage of regulatory strategies through an educational activity is
named "regulatory process". Theoretical models were developed to describe them,
such as the model developed by Barry Zimmerman that is widely recognized and
extensively utilized [Panadero, 2017], including the programming education field
[Loksa et al., 2022; Silva et al., 2021b].
Zimmerman’s model conceptualizes SRL as a cyclical process consisting of three
12

Literature review
phases. The first phase, named "forethought", involves students employing cog-
nitive strategies to comprehend the learning task and plan their approach. Ad-
ditionally, the development of motivational beliefs occurs during this phase, as it
plays a crucial role in stimulating students’ interest and involvement in the task.
The second phase, referred to as the "performance" phase, encompasses various
actions undertaken during the execution of the learning activity. These actions
include the utilization of metacognitive strategies (e.g., monitoring one’s own
understanding of the task), the application of cognitive strategies (e.g., creating
a flowchart to predict programming code behavior), and the implementation of
motivational strategies (e.g., evaluating self-consequences and the rewards asso-
ciated with task performance). Lastly, upon completion of the educational task,
regardless of success, the "self-reflection" phase occurs. During this phase, stu-
dents assess their performance and reflect on ways to enhance their learning. The
knowledge acquired during this phase feeds into the subsequent cycle of SRL.
In practice, the processes above are externalized through regulatory strategies
that students use through a learning task [Pintrich and De Groot, 1990]. Some
examples include rereading an exercise statement, taking notes during classes,
highlighting the main parts of a text, reflecting on one’s performance, and orga-
nizing the learning routine. These strategies can enhance various aspects of the
learning process, such as a deeper understanding of the class material, identify-
ing exercise demands, and recognizing personal limitations [Schunk, 2005].
2.2.1 Regulatory strategies
Understanding students’ learning processes requires identifying the regulatory
strategies they utilize [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. The term "strategy" rep-
resents an action that is performed to achieve educational goals. Examples of
regulatory strategies include: planning a learning schedule, text-summarizing,
help-seeking, goal definitions, self-evaluation, and self-appraisement [Weinstein
et al., 2011]. The use of regulatory strategies may involve the learner’s conscious
effort but can also be performed automatically through repeated practice and ha-
bitual application without carrying significant conscious awareness [Garcia and
Pintrich, 1994].
Various methods can be utilized to identify regulatory strategies, such as in-
terviews [Zimmerman and Martinez-Pons, 1988], think-aloud protocols to cap-
ture students’ thoughts during tasks [Vandevelde et al., 2015], and the use of
data-mining techniques to record students’ interactions in computational envi-
ronments [Ko and Leu, 2016]. The data collected from these methods is a repre-
sentation of students’ learning behaviors, and can be subjected to thematic anal-
ysis within the lens of SRL theory to identify the regulatory strategies students’
use [Loksa et al., 2020].
Researchers often group the identified strategies according to the students’ aca-
demic performance. For example, the use of high and low-performers groups is
typical [Zimmerman and Pons, 1986], as evidence indicates that these two groups
often exhibit differences in their regulatory skills, which may account for varia-
13

Chapter 2
tions in their learning performance [Emagnaw, 2019]. High-performing students
typically demonstrate a more comprehensive repertoire of regulatory strategies
they can employ when needed [García-Pérez et al., 2020].
Grouping students proved valuable in identifying strategies with potential to
contribute to learning [Zimmerman and Pons, 1986]. For instance, Li et al. [2021]
discovered that high and low-performers used different strategies when encoun-
tering difficulty. Low-performing students focused on visualizing the exercise’s
solution, whereas high-performers focused on reviewing their learning materi-
als before viewing the solution. The authors argue that these differences in the
learning process might account for variations in learning performance. Falkner
et al. [2014] has also identified significant differences between high and low-
performing programming students, with the latter group doing less planning and
testing of their code.
Finally, researchers have extensively investigated the regulatory strategies exhib-
ited by students within a context-specific perspective [Cleary et al., 2012]. In
this regard, regulatory strategies are classified as general-purpose and context-
specific [Falkner et al., 2014]. The former refers to strategies that can be ap-
plied regardless of the educational context, such as setting learning goals, under-
standing the educational task, seeking information, and adjusting learning rou-
tines. Context-specific strategies, on the other hand, vary across different learning
domains. In programming education, for instance, examples include flowchart
drawing, program tracing, debugging, and test case analysis. Categorizing reg-
ulatory strategies into these groups helps to contextualize research and identify
variations in regulatory behavior specific to certain domains [Neroni et al., 2019].
Falkner et al. [2014] have made significant contributions in identifying general-
purpose and programming-specific strategies. The findings indicate that pro-
gramming learning encompasses a combination of general-purpose and context-
specific regulatory strategies, with final-year students employing specific strate-
gies to a greater extent. In comparison, first-year students relied on general-
purpose strategies. These results highlight how SRL process can be context-
specific.
Despite a wealth of publications on SRL in other educational domains, there is
a need to expand our understanding in the context of programming education
[Loksa et al., 2020]. This thesis aligns with the theoretical perspective that regu-
latory strategies can be categorized as general-purpose or context-specific.
2.2.2 Regulation of learning within a socio-cognitive perspective
One criticism of SRL research is its narrow focus on the individual student, which
suggests their isolation from the social environment and collective classroom ac-
tivities [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011]. However, learning is often a social process,
and the relationships established among students are expected to influence the
regulatory processes [Panadero and Järvelä, 2015; Schunk, 2012].
A growing body of literature has called for incorporating regulation of learning
14

Literature review
within a social perspective, emphasizing the importance of understanding how
the social relationship influences the regulatory process and learning achieve-
ment [Panadero and Järvelä, 2015; Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. In this con-
text, the phenomenon of learning regulation is viewed as a convergence of indi-
viduals regulating themselves and being regulated by others [Järvelä et al., 2019].
Rooted in sociocultural learning theories, social interactions are seen as influ-
encing various psychological processes, including cognition, metacognition, and
others [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011]. As early as the late 1980s, Barry Zimmerman
noted the impact of social-cognitive theory elements on the regulatory process
[Zimmerman, 1989]. In his work, Zimmerman discussed how the environment
could influence an individual’s actions, such as employing a self-regulatory strat-
egy. For example, when teachers request students to perform a specific action
that aids them in a task. Nevertheless, Zimmerman’s SRL model mostly limited
its extent to individual regulation [Panadero and Järvelä, 2015].
Over the years, there has been an increasing interest in contextualizing the study
of SRL processes from a social perspective [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011]. Theo-
retical frameworks have been developed to capture the regulatory processes that
emerge from social interactions, known as socially shared regulation of learning
(SSRL) and co-regulation.
SSRL refers to the collaborative efforts of participants in a group setting, work-
ing together to achieve a shared learning objective [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011].
Within SSRL, students working in groups engage in jointly regulatory actions,
such as defining strategies for task-solving, considering their peers’ perspectives,
and integrating diverse group perceptions [Järvelä et al., 2014]. Various regu-
latory processes co-occur, involving multiple participants engaged in collabora-
tive tasks, leading to collective negotiation and deliberation [Hadwin and Os-
hige, 2011]. This interaction enhances students’ ability to develop a more com-
prehensive understanding of the learning tasks and propose effective solutions
[Panadero and Järvelä, 2015].
SSRL relies on students activating their own SRL processes while collaborating
on a task [Bransen et al., 2021]. Subsequently, students articulate their thoughts
to the group, such as offering commentary or suggestions, which initiates inter-
actions that could stimulate SRL processes in their peers [Hadwin and Oshige,
2011]. The transition of regulatory processes between individuals and the collec-
tive, working together to achieve a shared goal, is the defining characteristic of
SSRL [Malmberg et al., 2017].
Co-regulation involves receiving regulatory cues from external sources, such as
another person or a software agent, but not necessarily during a collaborative
activity [Volet et al., 2009]. Co-regulation typically occurs when an individual,
such as a learner or professor, who possesses greater regulatory capabilities, en-
gages in activating or mediating the regulatory processes of others. This can be
achieved through various means, such as asking questions or requesting specific
actions to be taken [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011].
Co-regulation becomes particularly valuable when students face challenges in
progressing independently and require support to accomplish their tasks [Blau
15

Chapter 2
and Shamir-Inbal, 2017]. This characteristic reflects what Vygotsky’s defined as
Zone of Proximal Development [Hausfather, 1996]. In this sense, it underscores
the significance of social interactions in providing learners with guidance and
assistance in performing actions that would otherwise prove difficult for them
[Panadero and Järvelä, 2015].
While co-regulation involves facilitating regulatory actions in a supportive man-
ner, SSRL focuses on how students collectively regulate themselves toward achiev-
ing a common educational goal [Järvelä et al., 2013]. This fundamental difference
sets SSRL apart by highlighting the importance of collective regulation in attain-
ing a common goal rather than focusing solely on individual guidance. Nonethe-
less, co-regulation can occurs in collaborative tasks to facilitate the realization of
SSRL. For instance, if one student encounters difficulty understanding a problem,
others can provide support by employing different strategies.
2.2.3 Fostering regulatory processes using scaffolding
Some students struggle with autonomous self-regulation, which can have an im-
pact on their academic performance [Duckworth et al., 2009]. This issue also
extends to students who exhibit satisfactory levels of regulation in certain areas
(such as cognition) but face challenges in others (such as motivational regulation)
[Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. Differences in regulatory capabilities may stem
from various factors, including students’ prior experiences and the stimuli they
have received [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023], thereby highlighting the inherent
differences that are typical to educational settings.
An important aspect of learning regulation is that it should be viewed as a dy-
namic process subject to change rather than fixed traits that individuals either
have or lack [Duckworth et al., 2009]. Evidence suggests that the educational
context influences the development of learning regulation and that it can be nur-
tured through appropriate stimuli [Zimmerman, 1989]. In this regard, a promi-
nent research area focuses on providing support to guide the implementation of
regulatory processes that can benefit a wide range of students [Schunk, 2005].
Supporting students’ learning and regulatory process is often named "scaffold-
ing," a concept derived from Vygotsky’s theory of the zone of proximal devel-
opment [Delen et al., 2014]. Scaffolding represents the assistance that students
receive to perform tasks they would struggle with independently [Greene and
Azevedo, 2011]. The use of a learning journal is an example of regulatory scaf-
folding that has been employed, prompting students to plan and reflect on their
study routines continuously. These prompts seek to stimulate behaviors that are
important to learn and that may not occur naturally [Schwendimann et al., 2018].
Scaffolding can take numerous formats, such as graphics, reminders, and struc-
tured plans [Yilmaz-Na and Sönmez, 2023]. The choice of scaffolding depends on
the specific psychological areas targeted for stimulation. For example, sending
notification emails to remind students about incomplete tasks can support their
monitoring of learning progress, while asking students to set their own learning
goals is an effective approach for motivational regulation [Schunk, 1990].
16

Literature review
The development of regulatory scaffolding is an important research area [Azevedo
and Hadwin, 2005]. This process is deemed complex because it requires exper-
tise in educational psychology to understand how SRL occurs, and how it can be
incorporated into the educational context in which the scaffolding will be used
[Greene and Azevedo, 2011].
It is crucial to ensure that the selection of stimuli aligns with the educational
goals students are expected to achieve [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013]. For instance,
metacognitive scaffolding could be employed in programming education to stim-
ulate reflective coding development practices, a crucial ability [Robins, 2019]. In
this regard, students could be prompted to review their code upon completing an
exercise and suggest improvements. This activity requires both self-evaluations,
a metacognitive action, and the engagement of cognitive processes to assess the
suitability of alternative programming resources over those initially employed
[Robins, 2019]. Multiple scaffolding techniques can be combined to stimulate dif-
ferent psychological areas.
This thesis’s scope revolves around using computers to provide regulatory scaf-
folding. This technological resource presents various advantages, including sup-
port for multiple students, and the possibility to offer personalized support. These
aspects can enhance the effectiveness of regulatory stimuli compared to tradi-
tional models, such as using paper and pen [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013].
2.2.4 Measurement of regulatory abilities and strategies
Measuring regulatory abilities and strategies is a crucial aspect of research grounded
in the SRL theory [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. It has numerous applications,
including understanding the regulatory levels of a group of students, assessing
the effectiveness of an intervention (e.g., comparing regulatory performance with
a control group), and gaining insights into how students regulate themselves.
Several data collection procedures exist, and their selection depends on the re-
search objectives. For example, questionnaires are commonly employed in ex-
ploratory research to investigate the regulatory strategies present in the educa-
tional process [Pintrich et al., 1993]. This instrument has also been used to identify
associations between the regulation of learning and programming performance
[Ramirez Echeverry et al., 2018]. To illustrate this case, the authors found a re-
lationship between students’ level of self-efficacy and metacognitive monitoring
with learning. This result points to two factors that may influence learning and
opens the way for future research.
The measurement of regulatory abilities is also important for causal research,
such as experiments. For example, in this thesis, an experiment was performed
to evaluate the effectiveness of educational computational software in improv-
ing academic performance and/or the development of SRL skills [Silva et al.,
2021b]. A typical procedure in this research type is to use a design of experiment
composed of a pre and post-test design with a control group [Zimmerman and
Schunk, 2011].
17

Chapter 2
The Motivated for Learning Strategies Questionnaire (MSLQ) is one of the most
widely used instruments to measure self-regulation aptitude in different con-
texts, including programming education [Loksa et al., 2022]. This instrument
is structured around three constructs: motivation, cognitive strategies, and self-
regulation. Motivation is further divided into the scales of self-efficacy (e.g. Com-
pared with others in this class, I think I’m a good student), intrinsic value (e.g., I prefer
class work that is challenging so I can learn new things.), and test anxiety (e.g., I am so
nervous during a test that I cannot remember facts I have learned). Self-efficacy refers to
students’ own perception of their capabilities, intrinsic value measures students’
capabilities to sustain motivation across several topics, including those less in-
teresting, and test anxiety seeks to measure how students’ handle with anxiety
during tests. The cognitive strategies scale (e.g., When studying, I copy my notes
over to help me remember material ) refers to students’ capability to organize their
learning materials, focus and comprehend the relevant parts of a text, perform
annotations, among others. The self-regulation scale (e.g., I ask myself questions to
make sure I know the material I have been studying. ) focuses on students’ ability to
monitor and plan their learning and the capability to overcome their difficulties.
Although the MSLQ is widely used, its psychometrics properties in program-
ming education remain uncertain [Silva et al., 2021a]. Some of its constructs have
been found to have no association with programming performance, suggesting
that they may not be relevant in this learning context. An alternative is the use of
programming-specific instruments like the Self-regulation of Learning Program-
ming Strategies Questionnaire (SPSQ) [Lu et al., 2017].
The SPSQ consists of 23 items that evaluate students’ utilization of various learn-
ing strategies during programming tasks. This instrument is structured in seven
constructs: attention regulation, planning ahead of programming, content mon-
itoring, organization, checking and correcting, planning during code and self-
evaluation. Examples of the items used for each construct include: 1) attention
regulation - ATTR (e.g. When implementing a program, I focused on programming so
that I would not be distracted by other things ); 2 planning ahead of programming
- PA (e.g. Before implementing a program, I designed a flowchart of what I needed to
address in the program); 3) content monitoring - CM (e.g., When implementing a pro-
gram, I checked to see whether what I had implemented was correct ); 4) organization
(e.g., I wrote a user manual for the project); 5) checking and correcting - CC (e.g., At
the end of implementation, I went back and read the code of the module to make sure it
was OK); 6) planning during coding - PDC (e.g., When implementing the program, I
thought about what I was going to implement next ); and 7) self-evaluation - SE (e.g.
After I implemented a program module or part of it, I thought about whether what I had
implemented was correct).
Researchers also use qualitative measurements to capture the regulatory strate-
gies manifested by students. Compared to questionnaires, qualitative resources
can provide a wider understanding of students’ learning processes because ques-
tionnaires are constrained to the strategies encompassed by instruments’ items
[Garcia and Pintrich, 1994]. To illustrate qualitative research, Pedrosa et al. [2016]
used open-ended questions to identify students’ regulatory strategies in a project-
based learning activity. The authors used thematic analysis and identified differ-
18

Literature review
ent regulatory strategies they used to progress on the task, such as time manage-
ment, information organization, and planning. This knowledge helps understand
the mechanism students use during programming activities.
With advancements in computing, the use of techniques for data collection and
analysis from students’ interactions with digital learning environments has also
increased [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013]. This data can be used to gain insights
into their study habits and to establish connections between their actions and
regulatory theory [Winne et al., 2013]. This approach can measure the actions
that students actually perform, unlike instruments that rely on questions about
what they do. There may be a gap between what they think they do and the
actions they perform.
Castellanos et al. [2017] exemplifies the use of data analysis based on students’
interactions with a code development platform. Various program development
metrics were measured from students’ code, and the authors calculated the cor-
relation between these metrics with students’ MSLQ scores. The results suggest
that self-regulated learners exhibited a higher externalization of certain behaviors
(e.g., higher code complexity). However, the study also uncovered unexpected
findings, such as increased syntactic errors associated with higher metacognitive
regulatory skills. Metacognitive regulation was expected to provide higher sup-
port for code development, and the present finding highlights the complexities
of programming learning that inquiries for further investigation.
Finally, despite a wide range of possibilities for measuring individual regulation
using questionnaires, there is still a lack of measurement instruments for social
regulation [Malmberg et al., 2017]. Studies within this context often use indi-
vidual regulation questionnaires, such as the MSLQ, performance measures, and
qualitative approaches focused on students’ interactions to explore how social
regulation occurs [Shao et al., 2023].
The various measurement options discussed in this section emphasize the need to
combine these approaches to gain a holistic view of the regulatory process [Roth
et al., 2015].
2.2.5 Regulation of learning and programming education
Learning programming requires the active manage of cognitive, metacognitive,
motivational, emotional, and behavioral processes [Prather et al., 2020]. How-
ever, the correct utilization of these psychological resources depends on students’
capabilities to regulate their learning [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. A sub-
stantial body of research is pointing to the relevance of SRL to programming ed-
ucation, motivating a growing interest in grounding programming education re-
search on the regulation of learning theory [Loksa et al., 2022; Silva et al., 2023b].
Research on the regulation of learning in programming education focuses on
three key topics: i) identifying the association between the regulation of learning
and programming achievement, ii) exploring how programming students regu-
late themselves, and iii) developing scaffolding to promote the use and devel-
19

Chapter 2
opment of regulatory abilities [Silva et al., 2021b]. These investigations aim to
deepen our understanding of how the regulation of learning influences program-
ming learning and assess the effectiveness of supporting learning through regu-
latory scaffolding. This thesis focuses particularly on topic iii, but it also makes
contributions to topics i and ii.
2.3 Collaborative learning
As the interaction among students is where social regulatory processes come into
play [Malmberg et al., 2017], it is crucial to understand how collaborative pro-
gramming practices unfold. Therefore, within the context of this thesis, the objec-
tive was to investigate the regulation of learning processes from a socio-cognitive
perspective.
Social interaction is central to learning as educational settings primarily consist
of individuals who engage in constant interactions [Järvelä et al., 2013]. Conse-
quently, these interactions can significantly influence the learning process, and
also how students regulate themselves. However, viewing the learning process
as part of a social nature has not always been widely recognized or acknowledged
[Schunk, 2012].
To grasp the significance of social processes in learning, it is imperative to com-
prehend their incorporation into education [Panadero and Järvelä, 2015]. This en-
tails comprehending the evolution of educational theories over the decades and
integrating social relationships into other facets of learning. From a historical
perspective, there have been different approaches to understanding the learning
process. Skinner’s work was one of the most influential behaviorist psycholo-
gists, and had a significant influence on educational psychologists [Ertmer and
Newby, 1993]. According to Skinner’s "Operant conditioning" concept, behavior
is shaped through positive or negative reinforcements. Therefore, learning occurs
as a consequence of how students receive these reinforcements.
Critics of this learning theory argue that behaviorism places excessive emphasis
on the role of the external environment, such as stimuli provided by teachers,
while overlooking the significance of internal factors, such as the student’s cog-
nitive processes, in the learning process. This has motivated the emergence of
cognitivism, a theory concerned with the cognitive processes that occur during
learning [Ertmer and Newby, 1993].
Cognitivism represents a departure from the behaviorists’ focus on the exter-
nal environment and instead emphasizes the study of internal processes such as
thinking, problem-solving, and memory [Bárcena and Read, 2004]. This theory
recognizes learners as active participants in constructing knowledge and high-
lights the role of cognitive processes in internalizing concepts. A fundamental
part of this theory is the acceptance that the execution of certain mental processes
can be guided through scaffoldings [Garcia et al., 2018b].
Critics of cognitivism later argued that a purely mental perspective on knowl-
20

Literature review
edge overlooks other important factors that influence learning, including the so-
cial context [Arponen, 2013]. Socio-cognitivism emerges as a bridge between be-
haviorism and cognitivism by acknowledging that external stimuli and the social
context can shape behavior, and one’s psychological processes (such as cognition
and motivation) also play a significant role in this regard [Zimmerman, 1990].
From a historical perspective, Vygotsky’s work has emphasized the social ba-
sis of cognitive development [Hausfather, 1996]. A central aspect of his work is
recognizing that one’s psychological processes are shaped by the social context.
Vygotsky had a significant contribution to the current understanding that learn-
ing cannot be dissociated from the social environment in which it occurs [Taber,
2020]. Albert Bandura’s social learning theory has also made significant contri-
butions by emphasizing that learning involves observation and interaction with
others [Schunk, 2012]. While there have been other conceptualizations of collab-
orative learning, these two theories have played a crucial role in understanding
key aspects of the learning process within social contexts [Panadero and Järvelä,
2015].
Student interactions play a central role within the social context of educational
settings [Rummel, 2018]. There are multiple formats of interactions, and to the
scope of this thesis the primary focus lies on a specific type of interaction that
takes place when students collaborate in pairs or groups to accomplish a shared
objective, known as collaborative learning [Laal and Laal, 2012]. This type of in-
teraction goes beyond casual conversation, requiring argumentative and critical
thinking skills, enabling students to collectively develop a comprehensive under-
standing of the task and propose appropriate solutions [Kirschner and Erkens,
2013]. Through collaboration students develop shared knowledge through col-
lective negotiation and deliberation [Law et al., 2021].
Collaboration manifests in various forms, and comprehending how learning un-
folds within this context is crucial. Students engage in three primary modes of
interaction: synchronous, asynchronous, and a hybrid approach [Gallant, 2020].
Synchronous communication facilitates the exchange of experiences and conflict
resolution. However, scheduling conflicts may arise, posing challenges for all
participants to attend meetings at a specific time. Asynchronous interaction mit-
igates this issue but can introduce others, such as the need for a more structured
framework to support collaboration (e.g., chat and discussion boards). A hybrid
approach combines the advantages of both methods [Jeong and Hartley, 2018].
Synchronous and asynchronous interactions serve different purposes in program-
ming education. For instance, educators may opt for synchronous collaboration
when assigning students simpler activities like exercises, which require less time
[Alzaid and Hsiao, 2019]. This approach offers several benefits, as students can
assist one another and learn by observing their peers working on a task. On
the other hand, asynchronous interaction is suitable for more complex activities,
such as projects in project-based learning approaches. In this context, students
typically work independently on their tasks, necessitating less frequent interac-
tion [Silva et al., 2020].
Collaborative learning encompasses various interactions, such as propositions,
21

Chapter 2
commentaries, questioning, agreement, and clarification [Stahl, 2004]. A propo-
sition involves a student suggesting an action for the group to undertake, which
can elicit comments, questions, or requests for clarification. A deliberation pro-
cess ensues following these actions, leading to either agreement or disagreement.
The agreement is reached when participants in the group activity establish a
shared understanding or consensus.
Several factors can negatively impact the collaborative process, including: i) Lack
of engagement, where some students fail to make group contributions [Gunawar-
dena et al., 1997]; ii) Superficial interactions within the group, which offer little
assistance in achieving collective goals [Salonen et al., 2005]. These superficial
interactions prevent meaningful collaboration and hinder the group’s ability to
work effectively. iii) Limited coordination of cognitive, metacognitive, motivational,
and emotional processes among group members [Law et al., 2021]. This can prevent
group members from effectively expressing their difficulties, limiting their ability
to seek help from their peers and contribute with strategies to solve the task [Had-
win and Oshige, 2011]. This latter issue exemplifies a lack of social regulation that
commonly arises during group work [Järvelä et al., 2016]. As a result, students
fail to engage in discussions that stimulate crucial mental processes for effective
learning, ultimately affecting their ability to achieve collective goals [Panadero
and Järvelä, 2015].
To mitigate the aforementioned issues, researchers have been investigating how
to use computers to support the collaborative learning process.
2.3.1 Computer-supported collaborative learning environments
In a previous systematic literature review performed by the author of this thesis,
several characteristics of Computer-Supported Collaborative Learning (CSCL)
environments were summarized [Silva et al., 2020]. Various resources were em-
ployed to promote collaboration, including robotics and software, with their us-
age varying based on students’ academic levels. Robotics were primarily em-
ployed with children, engaging them in collaborative activities to build and pro-
gram robots to perform specific actions. On the other hand, research conducted in
undergraduate settings predominantly employed software, focusing on fostering
joint code development among students.
One earlier example of CSCL was the COLLEGE (COLLaborative Edition, com-
pilinG and Execution of programs), which comprises a programming editor, struc-
tured chat, and a group decision-making tool. This software exemplifies a basic
