set of features present in several other CSCL programming environments, allow-
ing students to interact and collaboratively build code. An important addition
provided by the COLLEGE was the structured chat that can assist students in
conducting a dialogue towards the group activity [Bravo et al., 2005].
The inclusion of collaborative support in traditional programming editors was
also observed. The Jazz Sangam is one example of a plugin for the Eclipse editor
that synchronizes its use across two different programmers. The focus is on a
pair programming approach, in which one student assumes the programmer’s
22

Literature review
role, whereas the other is responsible for assisting this process. The provided
plugin offers resources so this activity can occur remotely [Devide et al., 2008].
Tangible interfaces were also used to foster collaborative activities. The Code
Weaver is one example that aims to support the development of basic program-
ming concepts by using a tangible visual programming interface [Sakamoto and
Ohshima, 2019]. Students collaboratively program their code by placing cards on
a table, which are read by the Code Weaver, highlighting the snippet in execution
and its output.
The CSCL environments offered varying degrees of learning support. While most
of these environments focused on providing resources to facilitate student collab-
oration, such as chat features and collaborative programming editors, only a few
studies have explored scaffolding techniques to enhance psychological processes
during collaborative tasks. One example was the research conducted by [Su et al.,
2015], which encouraged students to annotate aspects of their performance in
the learning task and share them with their peers. This study’s findings demon-
strated this resource’s effectiveness in promoting learning gains, underscoring its
potential impact on collaborative learning outcomes. Another example includes
the WPASC, an acronym for "Web-based Programming Assisted System for Co-
operation" [Hwang et al., 2012]. The software allowed students to request help
from their peers and receive feedback from them.
Effective engagement in collaborative activities often does not occur spontaneously
[Rummel and Spada, 2005]. Therefore, providing support is necessary, which can
be accomplished using CSCL environments [Zurita and Nussbaum, 2006]. This
approach has been demonstrated to be an effective strategy in promoting learning
outcomes [Jeong et al., 2019] and is increasingly recognized as a valuable resource
for fostering social regulatory episodes [Järvelä et al., 2016].
Despite their numerous benefits, there are significant challenges in designing
CSCL learning environments. The challenges include effectively stimulating stu-
dents’ collaboration, promoting social regulation, and meeting the specific needs
of diverse educational contexts [Zurita and Nussbaum, 2006]. Orchestrating the
collaborative process involves guiding students to engage in actions that enhance
collaborative learning [Rummel and Spada, 2005]. This can be achieved through
scripting techniques, which involve using the CSCL to assign roles and actions
that students need to perform [Schellens et al., 2007]. Since many of these actions
are not typically carried out spontaneously by students, this approach aims to
mitigate the problem by providing guidance and structure to their implementa-
tion [Kirschner and Erkens, 2013].
Another challenge students face during collaborative learning is the collective
management of their cognitive, metacognitive, and motivational processes to
achieve a shared goal [Hadwin et al., 2017]. This process relies on students’
capabilities to socially regulate their learning, as it orchestrates regulatory pro-
cesses from the individual level (self-regulation) to the collective level, serving as
a central element of learning [Järvelä and Hadwin, 2013]. However, without ad-
equate regulatory support, students may struggle to manage their shared goals
and achieve productive collaboration effectively [Järvelä and Hadwin, 2013].
23

Chapter 2
Järvelä et al. [2014] proposed three design principles to promote social regulation
support in CSCL environments and mitigate the aforementioned issues. These
principles are: "(1) increasing learners’ awareness of their own and others’ learn-
ing process, (2) supporting the externalization of students’ and others’ learning
process in a social plane and helping in sharing and interaction, and (3) prompt-
ing the acquisition and activation of regulatory processes."
Järvela’s proposals provided a valuable framework for designing various scaf-
folding techniques to enhance social regulatory support. An example can be seen
in the work proposed by [Theophilou et al., 2021], who implemented a progress
bar that displayed each participant’s contribution to the learning task discussion.
This visual representation can enhance students’ awareness of their own and their
peers’ contributions, thereby promoting increased engagement in group work.
This aligns with the concept of "increasing learners’ awareness of their own and
others’ learning processes" as defined by [Järvelä and Hadwin, 2013].
The second and third design principles can be observed in the work of [Mor-
gan, 2020]. In their study, the authors created a group diary consisting of struc-
tured questions, such as "What was the greatest learning challenge this week?",
intending to promote metacognitive reflection. Students collectively utilized this
resource on a large-screen tablet, enabling them to externalize their thoughts and
evaluate the contributions of their peers.
Despite progress in this field, Lin [2018] asserts that further research is necessary
to determine how social regulation support should be designed in CSCL. This
involves exploring alternative formats of scaffolding and gathering evidence re-
garding their effectiveness [Panadero and Järvelä, 2015]. These concerns also ex-
ist in the context of programming education, where there is a limited number of
studies focused on fostering social regulation within CSCL environments [Silva
et al., 2021b].
At the time of writing the review, there was a dearth of research focusing on social
regulatory support in programming education, which hampers our understand-
ing of how to design such scaffolding and its effectiveness in enhancing program-
ming learning performance. The objective of this thesis is to address these issues
and make contributions in this area.
2.3.2 Collaborative activities in programming education
Collaboration among students is crucial in various educational contexts, includ-
ing programming learning [Sol et al., 2021]. In this educational setting, students
interact in diverse forms, such as requesting help from their peers to resolve a
code error or when they work together on collaborative exercises [Silva et al.,
2020]. This pedagogical approach not only proves effective for learning but also
has the potential to enhance motivation [Serrano-Cámara et al., 2014].
Incorporating collaborative work into programming education necessitates un-
derstanding how learning unfolds within this context. Various authors concur
that introducing programming concepts should follow a simplified and gradual
24

Literature review
approach, akin to what Jerome Bruner called the "Spiral curriculum" [Robins,
2019; Stapleton and Stefaniak, 2018]. From a collaborative standpoint, initial in-
teractions between students can be organized in pairs, working on straightfor-
ward tasks like debugging algorithms and solving small exercises [Robins, 2019].
Additionally, students can be paired in a way that allows those with more knowl-
edge to assist those facing difficulties, facilitating a co-regulatory process.
As students progress, they can engage in group work to tackle complex prob-
lems that are more suitable to stimulate collaborative problem-solving skills (e.g.,
discussion and negotiation) [Law et al., 2021] and the use of multiple program-
ming concepts [Silva et al., 2020]. An example of this is project-based learning,
where students are tasked with developing a solution to a problem with an open
or loosely defined scope. This activity requires students to integrate various col-
laborative skills, including task allocation, negotiation, and deliberation. These
aspects contribute to the emergence of social regulation and pose a challenge as
difficulties with this process can significantly hinder the achievement of collective
goals [Järvelä et al., 2019].
2.4 Related work
This thesis centers around three key aspects: i) identifying the correlation be-
tween regulation of learning and programming learning; ii) describing the reg-
ulatory strategies employed by students during programming activities; and iii)
developing a computer-supported collaborative learning environment to medi-
ate regulatory processes during programming activities. The subsequent sections
will present relevant studies from the existing literature pertaining to these areas.
2.4.1 Association between regulation of learning and program-
ming learning
Despite the apparent benefits of regulation of learning, there is a need to pro-
vide supporting evidence regarding its association with programming learning.
A systematic literature review was conducted to summarize the studies that ex-
amined the relationship between SRL skills and programming achievement [Silva
et al., 2021a]. The selected studies used questionnaires to measure SRL skills and
correlational analysis to calculate the associations.
The findings reveal that students who demonstrated higher levels of metacog-
nitive regulation and greater confidence in their abilities achieved higher grades
in programming courses. Even though correlation does not imply causation, this
finding, in combination with others, indicates that regulation of learning is an im-
portant skill for learning programming [Loksa et al., 2022]. In Section 4, a more
comprehensive exploration of the findings from this review is provided.
A surprising finding from the review was the lower association between cogni-
tive strategies and programming learning. Due to the inherent characteristics of
25

Chapter 2
programming that is relate to problem-solving abilities, these strategies were ex-
pected to be crucial in this learning domain [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. One hypothesis
is that not all cognitive strategies are relevant to this learning domain [Falkner
et al., 2015]. Another hypothesis is that the cognitive strategies measured by
general-purpose instruments such as MSLQ, might not be the most relevant to
programming learning [Bergin et al., 2005]. This thesis sought to provide insights
into this aspect.
Experimental studies also provided significant evidence of the importance of
SRL abilities to programming learning. In another systematic literature review,
the findings from studies that used SRL scaffoldings to support programming
learning were summarized [Silva et al., 2021b]. The results revealed that most
interventions proved to be effective in supporting programming learning. Subse-
quently, a meta-analysis was performed [Silva et al., 2023c], combining the results
of these studies and pointing to a positive effect on learning with an overall ef-
fect size of 0.58, considered moderate, but notable for educational research [Jeong
et al., 2019].
A more detailed description of the analyzed studies can be found in the men-
tioned literature reviews. Overall, the findings suggest that SRL is an important
ability for programming education. However, the small number of studies pub-
lished at the time of these reviews limits more robust conclusions. Therefore,
further evidence is necessary to build a holistic view of how SRL influences pro-
gramming learning, which this thesis seeks to contribute.
2.4.2 Regulatory strategies used by programming students
Identifying the regulatory strategies used by students is essential to understand
how learning occurs [Zimmerman and Martinez-Pons, 1988]. Falkner et al. [2014]
conducted a significant study in this theme, examining the reflections on pro-
gram development from first-year and final-year computing students. Regula-
tory strategies were identified from this data and categorized into two groups:
general-purpose and programming-specific. The former encompassed strategies
applicable in any educational context, such as setting learning goals, understand-
ing the task at hand, seeking information, and adjusting learning routines. The
latter comprised SRL strategies specific to programming education, including
flowchart drawing, program tracing, debugging, and test case analysis.
First-year students predominantly relied on general-purpose strategies, while
final-year students exhibited higher externalization of programming-specific strate-
gies. This finding emphasizes the importance of specific programming strategies
requiring developing time. It also raises concerns regarding the need to sup-
port first-year students as they work towards consolidating their programming-
specific strategies. Finally, these findings also corroborate that general-purpose
skills tend to be less used in the programming context.
In a subsequent study, the authors classified the regulatory strategies based on
academic performance [Falkner et al., 2015]. Most low-achieving students exhib-
ited similar behaviors, such as coding without proper planning and insufficient
26

Literature review
testing. This indicates a lack of cognitive strategies, particularly concerning plan-
ning behavior, a concern widely discussed in the literature [Qian and Lehman,
2017]. The authors also identified regulatory strategies predominantly utilized
by low-performing students, which may have a limited impact on learning. The
combined findings from both studies have significantly contributed to our un-
derstanding of how programming students regulate themselves and how their
abilities evolve throughout the course.
Loksa et al. [2020] also conducted a study involving programming students to
identify their learning strategies during program development. Most students
exhibited different self-regulation levels, being capable to monitor their progress
throughout programming activities. However, the authors observed that they did
not engage in deeper metacognitive performance evaluation. As a result, many
students were unaware of their challenges, which had a negative impact on their
programming performance.
The above mentioned finding is consistent with [Arakawa et al., 2021] results.
Some students were completely unaware of their learning process, which reflects
students showing regulatory limitations, such as not estimating the task time sat-
isfactorily and lacking some cognitive strategies. These difficulties were more fre-
quent among students who had difficulties completing their tasks, who in most
cases could not recognize them. Corroborating the previous discussion, the lack
of SRL abilities has been attributed as a factor that hindered students’ program-
ming performance.
Prather et al. [2022] expanded the discussion by incorporating the social context
into discussion. This study investigated the strategies employed by students dur-
ing collaborative work with their peers. The findings provides valuable insights
into analyzing regulatory strategies from a social perspective, currently lacking
in the existing literature. Many students emphasized the value of collaborative
work as a strategy for acquiring programming knowledge. They mentioned that
collaborative work enables them to compare and discuss solutions while appre-
ciating the opportunity to seek assistance from their peers. The authors highlight
the important role that social interactions play in programming learning.
The studies have significantly contributed to understanding the SRL strategies
used by programming students. Part of the identified strategies overlaps, sug-
gesting that programming students utilize similar regulatory strategies across
various educational contexts. At the same time, due to the complexities of the
regulation of the learning phenomenon, diverging and conflicting results were
also found. For example, part of the results suggests students have self-regulation
limitations [Falkner et al., 2015; Loksa et al., 2020], while others found the oppo-
site for some psychological dimensions [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. Diverging findings
indicate a need for further evidence, which can be gathered through multiple
sources that together provide a holistic view of the regulatory processes of pro-
gramming students [Loksa et al., 2022].
This thesis also aims to contribute to understanding how programming students
regulate. A qualitative exploratory investigation revealed a set of regulatory
strategies, some of which corroborate previous findings Falkner et al. [2015] and
27

Chapter 2
Loksa et al. [2020] while presenting new findings that expand knowledge about
this area. A detailed discussion is presented in Section 5.2.
2.4.3 Regulation of learning scaffolding in programming educa-
tion
As previously discussed in Section 2.2.3, regulation of learning is an ability that
can be supported and developed with proper stimuli [Dignath and Büttner, 2008].
Thus, starting from the premise that this skill is important for learning program-
ming, researchers began investigating how to incorporate regulatory scaffolding
into this educational context [Prather et al., 2020].
In a previous systematic literature review, fifteen studies proposing regulatory
scaffolding in programming education were identified and summarized to iden-
tify how regulatory stimuli were offered [Silva et al., 2021b]. They stimulated
different regulatory processes related to cognition, metacognition, and motiva-
tion. These studies aimed to foster students’ realization of regulatory actions
seeking to improve programming learning performance and the development of
regulatory skills.
Most of these scaffoldings were general-purpose stimuli adapted from other ed-
ucational areas. To illustrate, Alhazbi [2014] proposed a learning journal that
programming students used to reflect on their learning processes. This type of
regulatory scaffolding has been extensively researched in other areas and uses
questions to guide students to perform specific actions [Schwendimann et al.,
2018]. While this type of support is important, research shows that programming-
specific regulatory skills may have a greater impact on learning [Falkner et al.,
2015; Silva et al., 2024b].
Programming-specific scaffolding refers to resources that directly or indirectly
support activities related to the code development process. Seanosky et al. [2017]
used graphics to demonstrate how students’ code adheres to thirty-one Java com-
petencies. [Prather et al., 2019] offered regulatory support to assist students dur-
ing the code creation process. The authors prompted students to predict the out-
put of exercise test cases before writing the corresponding code. By employing
this technique, the expectation is to enhance students’ problem understanding
and improve code accuracy. However, this type of scaffolding was less investi-
gated by the existing literature.
The small number of studies exploring programming-specific regulatory stimuli
is a limiting factor to this research area because there exist specific learning de-
mands that general-purpose scaffoldings might not fully support [Prather et al.,
2020]. As a result, little is known about what regulatory scaffoldings should be
offered during programming practice, how to design them, and how effective
they are for learning. This thesis seeks to contribute to this respect by exploring
multiple regulatory scaffolding for assisting students during code development
that were not previously investigated.
In a more recent review, [Dayimani and Padayachee, 2023] presents a list of re-
28

Literature review
sources that can be used to stimulate regulatory processes in programming. The
propositions presented are mostly aligned with those in our review, indicating
that stimuli for regulatory processes should be directed towards supporting var-
ious mental resources to handle the multifaceted nature of programming learn-
ing. At the same time, there is a notable limitation in this review regarding the
discussion of programming-specific regulatory stimuli, and the lack of analysis
regarding how self-regulation fits into a collaborative programming learning con-
text. There is only a reference to the inclusion of social assistance as a regulatory
resource, but this limited perception overlooks the unique characteristics of col-
laborative learning and its specific regulatory process requirements.
Another limitation in existing literature identified by the systematic literature re-
view was that studies focused on supporting SRL, disregarding the social con-
text’s influence on the regulatory process. Therefore, understanding the social
regulation process within the programming education domain is still in its early
stages. Important exceptions are the research presented by Gogoulou et al. [2012];
Goswami et al. [2023]; Li et al. [2022]; Liu et al. [2023].
The work presented by Liu et al. [2023] shares similarities with the propositions
of this doctorate, by establishing an organization over the work process in a col-
laborative task. Students receive support for planning the task, as well as during
the writing of the code. However, this doctorate places a greater emphasis on
guiding the strategic planning of how the code will be built through questions
that aim to direct the actions to be carried out by the students, while [Liu et al.,
2023] offers a less structured model in which students must define for themselves
what they need to build their codes.
The work of Goswami et al. [2023] also falls within a context of social regulation,
but focused exclusively on co-regulation processes. A Jupyter2 tool extension was
developed, where students work individually on programming exercises with
the possibility to share questions or comments with their peers. The authors en-
vision that co-regulation processes will occur based on this interaction.
Li et al. [2022] is another work focused on supporting collaborative computa-
tional thinking activities. The authors used a shared documents platform (e.g.,
Google Docs), and several activities were used to facilitate SSRL, such as asking
students to discuss ideas for solving the task, assessing group progress, and eval-
uating one’s own accomplishments. The findings point to this support’s effective-
ness in increasing computational thinking performance. Gogoulou et al. [2012]
presents the software E-clip used to support collaborative programming activi-
ties. This environment guided students’ dialogues fostering productive collabo-
ration and creating awareness among them, elements deemed crucial for regula-
tion in social contexts Hadwin et al. [2017]. Students who used the environment
presented higher programming performance and greater receptivity to work in
group activities.
Even though the above works present important scaffoldings for encouraging so-
cial regulation, they are limited to collaborative activities. In this way, another
problem emerges, as learning must be seen as formed by individual and social
2https://jupyter.org/
29

Chapter 2
practices [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011]. This doctoral research also aimed to ex-
plore potential differences in the benefits gained from encouraging individual
regulation processes compared to those achieved through promoting social regu-
lation, whether in individual or collaborative activities. Another limitation found
in the studies of [Li et al., 2022] and [Gogoulou et al., 2012] was that they did not
directly support students during shared code development, a crucial part of the
programming learning process.
In summary, this thesis seeks to mitigate the aforementioned limitations by in-
vestigating the regulatory phenomenon from both individual and collaborative
perspectives. In particular, proposing regulatory support collaborative tasks and
assisting students during programming activities.
30

Chapter 3
Methodology
This thesis uses the self-regulation of learning theory as a basis to investigate
strategies to support programming learning and to explore how students learn
in this context. In this regard, this doctorate had three main objectives: i) de-
velop and evaluate a computer-supported collaborative learning environment
grounded in the regulation of learning theory to facilitate programming learn-
ing; ii) provide statistical evidence regarding the association between regulation
of learning and programming learning, and iii) explore the regulatory strategies
employed by students during programming activities.
3.1 Research questions
The research questions designed to achieve these objectives are presented in Ta-
ble 3.1. Each research question encompasses its unique set of methodological
approaches, which will be expanded in the subsequent sections.

scaffoldings are used in

utilized regulatory scaffoldings is vital. This un-
derstanding elucidates the resources that have
been employed to nurture regulatory processes,
highlights existing gaps, and identifies avenues
for future research.
RQ2) How to structure col-
laborative activities in pro-
gramming?
A central part of this thesis seeks to stimulate
the social regulatory process, which often oc-
curs during collaborative learning. Therefore,
this research question sought to investigate how
the previous literature explored programming
learning within social contexts.

and Hadwin, 2005]. The scarcity of studies in-
vestigating regulatory scaffoldings in program-

the possibilities of using this support to enhance
learning. This is more critical in the domain

Table 3.1: Research questions investigated in this thesis.
Figure 3.1 illustrates how each research question aligns with the thesis’ objectives:
the development of regulatory scaffoldings to enhance programming learning,
the examination of regulatory processes, and the investigation of the relationship
between self-regulated learning (SRL) and programming education.
3.2 Methodological approach
Designing and evaluating a CSCL environment grounded in the regulation of
learning constructs is the main objective of this thesis. This is a complex pro-
cess influenced by numerous factors [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013], such as the
multiple perspectives on how regulation of learning occurs [Panadero, 2017], the
complexities of the psychological process that takes part during learning [Zim-
merman and Schunk, 2011], and the educational context, each with its specific
characteristics that can influence the regulatory process in different ways [Dig-
nath and Büttner, 2008].
Conducting this research in the programming education field poses an additional
challenge because there is a scarcity of studies grounded on the regulation of
learning theory [Loksa et al., 2022]. This issue becomes even more apparent with
fewer studies aiming to stimulate social regulatory processes [Silva et al., 2021b].
The lack of comprehensive understanding surrounding the regulation of learning
in programming education poses challenges to developing computational scaf-
folding to foster these processes.
To deal with this ill-defined scope, an incremental research design and develop-
ment process was used in this doctorate. The Design-Based Research methodol-
ogy (DBR) was chosen because it is suited for tackling such complex situations
[based Research Collective, 2003]. According to Easterday et al. [2017], DBR is
33

Chapter 3
Figure 3.1: Alignment between research questions and thesis objectives.
34

Methodology
"a methodology conducted by education researchers to create practical interven-
tions and theoretical design models through a design process of focusing, under-
standing, defining, conceiving, building, testing, and presenting, that recursively
nests other research processes to iteratively search for empirical solutions to prac-
tical problems of human learning".
The DBR incremental perspective plays a pivotal role in organizing the research
process. Knowledge is built progressively, and along this process, the educa-
tional artifact is constructed by integrating theory and practice [Juan-Lazaro and
Area-Moreira, 2021]. This is particularly important when building technological
educational artifacts, as there are many uncertainties throughout the process, and
an incremental approach helps mitigate risks [Wang and Hannafin, 2005].
The incremental process is represented in DBR through iterations [Juan-Lazaro
and Area-Moreira, 2021]. Each iteration is defined by its specific objectives and
the corresponding activities undertaken to accomplish them. The insights gained
in one iteration inform and enhance the subsequent ones, facilitating the incre-
mental validation of research assumptions. The following iterations were under-
taken in this thesis and will be detailed in Section 4.
• The designing and pedagogical evaluation of the proposed computational
scaffoldings;
• RESPE’s prototype development and validation in a pilot study;
• RESPE’s experimental evaluation;
These iterations were conducted incrementally and aligned with the research ob-
jectives to develop and validate RESPE. In the first cycle, an investigation was
carried out to examine the current state of the art and identify how computational
scaffoldings for regulation were utilized to support programming learning, while
also identifying existing gaps. This knowledge served as the basis for the theoret-
ical design of the RESPE pedagogical foundation, which includes proposing new
scaffolding to mitigate gaps in the current literature. This theoretical design was
transformed into concrete software functionalities during the second iteration,
resulting in a prototype validated through a pilot study. The issues identified in
this validation were worked on in the third iteration, which resulted in the RESPE
version used in the experimental validation. Based on the collected data, proposi-
tions for future enhancements were made and directions for future research were
discussed.
3.3 Data collection and analysis
The specificities of each research question demanded multiple data collection
and analysis procedures tailored to each objective. Table 3.2 provides a concise
overview of the research methodology, data collection, and analysis procedures
for each research question. A complete description of how the research was con-
ducted is presented in Section 4, following the DBR iterations presented earlier.

RQ5) Do students who used

mance in individual program-
ming exercises than students
who did not?
A randomized controlled experiment was con-
ducted to assess the effectiveness of the RESPE
platform. Students’ course grades served as the
dependent variable and were used to compare
the performance of the experimental and control
groups.
RQ6) Do students who used
the RESPE exhibit higher per-
formance in collaborative pro-
gramming exercises than stu-
dents who did not?
Similar to the previous research question, but the
dependent variable was performance in collabo-
rative tasks.
RQ7) Do students who used the
RESPE demonstrate higher reg-
ulatory skills development than
students who did not?
Similar to the previous research question, but the
dependent variable was students’ scores on the
MSLQ and SPSQ instruments.
RQ8) What is the association be-
tween SRL and programming
learning?
A cross-national study was performed, measur-
ing students’ SRL skills using the MSLQ and
SPSQ instruments and examining their corre-
lation with programming performance. Addi-
tionally, a systematic literature review was con-
ducted, integrating the findings from our study
with existing literature.
RQ9) What regulatory strategies
are used by programming stu-
dents?
An exploratory investigation was carried out us-
ing qualitative data collection methods. Stu-
dents were asked to articulate their learning
strategies in specific scenarios, and the col-
lected data underwent thematic analysis to asso-
ciate the reported strategies with regulatory con-
structs.
36

Methodology
RQ10) What is the psychome-
tric validity of self-regulation
measurement instruments in the
context of programming educa-
tion?
Students’ answers to the SPSQ were used in a
psychometric evaluation.
Table 3.2: Description of the data collection and analysis conducted in this inves-
tigation.
Two standardized instruments were used to measure students’ regulatory abili-
ties, the Motivated for Learning Strategies Questionnaire (MSLQ), developed by
Pintrich and De Groot [1990], and the Self-Regulated Programming Strategies
Questionnaire (SPSQ) developed by Lu et al. [2017]. These instruments focus
on different aspects of the regulatory process, with MSLQ using items related to
general-purpose regulatory abilities, whereas the SPSQ measures programming-
specific regulatory abilities.
The MSLQ is segmented into three main constructs: motivational beliefs, cogni-
tive strategy use, and self-regulation. According to its creators, researchers have
the flexibility to apply selected constructs from the instrument tailored to their
study’s specific needs [Pintrich and De Groot, 1990]. Given that the scaffoldings
introduced in this doctoral research are primarily targeted at enhancing cognitive
and metacognitive processes, the investigation selectively employed the cogni-
tive strategy use and self-regulation constructs from the MSLQ. Table 3.3 presents
the items related to these constructs.
MSLQ selection was motivated by its reputation as one of the most reliable and
employed instruments for assessing SRL skills across various educational con-
texts [Panadero, 2017]. This instrument has a significant adoption in program-
ming research [Prather et al., 2020], but in comparison to other educational do-
mains it still deserves further evidence regarding its reliability [Silva et al., 2021a].
This doctorate work also sought to contribute with statistical evidence in this re-
spect.
MSLQ and SPSQ were translated into Portuguese by the authors of this research1.
The Cronbach alpha for the MSLQ instrument was .81, demonstrating good in-
ternal consistency [Cortina, 1993] and in consonance with other MSLQ transla-
tions [Jakesova and Hrbackova, 2014]. The Cronbach alpha for the SPSQ was .89,
slightly higher than the author’s original study (.81) [Lu et al., 2017].
SPSQ usage allowed the measurement of programming-specific regulatory strate-
gies, which is pertinent for evaluating specificities of this educational domain
[Prather et al., 2020]. The SPSQ items focus on cognitive and metacognitive pro-
gramming strategies, such as how students tackle programming challenges, their
attention in assessing the suitability of their algorithms, and their reflective ca-
pabilities to enhance their coding practices. As the organization scale focuses on
requirements design and documentation, which are out of the introductory pro-

able in Appendix C

Methodology
gramming courses’ scope, their items were not used. Table 3.4 presents the in-
struments’ constructs along with their items.
3.4 Pedagogical context
The data collected in this doctorate work was obtained in introductory online
programming courses targeting individuals with little or no prior programming
experience. The course was offered at a mid-sized public university in an urban
area of Brazil, and the instructor was the author of this thesis. Each course lasted
ten weeks and consisted of 40 hours of synchronous and asynchronous classes
held during the COVID-19 pandemic.
The course syllabus (Appendix A) was based on the ACM curricula 2 recom-
mendations for fundamental programming concepts. It includes variables, in-
put/output, conditionals, loop structures, and functions. The Python program-
ming language was taught in the course, due to its simplicity and versatility, cru-
cial for introductory courses [Robins, 2019]. Nineteen videos were recorded, to-
taling 228 minutes of programming content. The course content was released in
Portuguese, the student’s native language.
Students had asynchronous access to the recorded video materials consisting of
programming classes and exercises, grouped into closed questions, Parsons prob-
lems, and open-ended programming questions. Closed-ended questions were
utilized to evaluate the comprehension of programming concepts and the skill
to predict code execution output. Parsons problems involved presenting a pro-
gramming problem along with its solution broken down into disordered code
snippets, which students had to arrange in the correct sequence [Garcia et al.,
2018b]. Open-ended questions included a programming problem and a set of
test cases. The expected outcome was a working program based on the require-
ments. There was no limit to the number of times the students could test their
programs on the same exercise.
Programming practice took place in the proposed CSCL environment named RE-
SPE. The course included 201 exercises (44 from variables, 56 from conditionals,
43 from loops, and 58 from functions) from varying difficulties. They were or-
ganized according to Bloom’s Taxonomy, a hierarchized framework to organize
instructional activities to stimulate different cognitive complexity levels [Thomp-
son et al., 2008]. The exercises were made available following the course’s calen-
dar and syllabus sequence, and students could work on them until the end of the
course.
The RESPE automatically calculated students’ course grades based on the correct-
ness of the individual exercise’s test cases. A passing grade of 70 out of 100 was
required to obtain the course certificate, a criterion established by the educational
institution where the course occurred.
Every week, a 90-minute synchronous online session took place in which stu-

Methodology
dents worked in pairs or triads that were randomly assigned at each meeting.
Eight open-ended collaborative programming exercises were worked out through-
out the course, and the students had to complete them without the instructors’
support. The exercises comprise three test cases which were used by RESPE to
automatically correct the group’s code. The scores were assigned to the group so
that all students in the same group received the same score. The student’s partic-
ipation in the synchronous meetings was optional and did not add to their final
grade in the course.
The collaborative exercises were organized as follows: two exercises on vari-
ables, three on conditionals, two on loops, and one on functions. To foster re-
search reproducibility, the exercises were translated to English and are available
in the following Open Science Repository: https://osf.io/tu47x/?view_only=
aab2c35ee94d41b1abaf5ececea3f0dd.
41

Chapter 4
RESPE development and evaluation
RESPE’s development and evaluation process took part in three iterations of the
DBR cycle. In the first iteration, a comprehensive review of existing literature was
performed to gain insights into how the regulation of learning was stimulated in
programming education. Due to this doctorate’s scope related to social regu-
lation, a review was also performed to understand how collaborative activities
were orchestrated in this learning domain. The reviews also helped in identify-
ing research gaps, such as the lack of social regulatory support and scaffoldings to
support programming tasks. The first iteration resulted in a pedagogical proposi-
tion of regulatory scaffoldings to support programming learning, that were eval-
uated by experts in the field.
The results from the first iteration grounded the RESPE’s development that oc-
curred in the second iteration. The theoretical propositions were transformed into
software functionalities, and a pilot study was conducted as a first evaluation of
the proposed scaffolding. Lastly, the feedback was used to make adjustments for
an experimental evaluation in the third iteration. The following sections describe
each iteration in detail.
4.1 Iteration 1
The first iteration focused on the development and validation of a theoretical
proposition for support programming learning based on the use of regulatory
scaffoldings. This process demanded an understanding of the current state of
the art, and an alignment of the regulation of learning theory to accommodate
the specificities of the programming learning constructs. The following research
questions guided this first iteration: RQ1) Which regulatory scaffoldings are used in
programming education?, RQ2) How to structure collaborative activities in program-
ming? and RQ3) How can a CSCL be designed to foster regulation of learning among
programming students?
RQ1 and RQ2 were answered through two systematic literature reviews, which
is a procedure widely used to summarize the findings in a specific area. Kitchen-
ham’s methodology was used to structure the literature reviews in this work, due
43

Chapter 4
to its wide adoption in computer science research [Kitchenham et al., 2009]. This
methodology outlines several phases in the review process, including formulat-
ing research questions, identifying relevant keywords and selecting appropriate
databases, defining inclusion and exclusion criteria, conducting quality analysis,
and extracting relevant data. The methodology and findings of the reviews are
described below, and a more detailed presentation of them is available in the fol-
lowing publications: [Silva et al., 2021b] and [Silva et al., 2020].
4.1.1 RQ1) Which regulatory scaffoldings are used in program-
ming education?
A systematic literature review was performed to identify studies that used reg-
ulatory scaffolding to support programming learning. The findings from this
review help gain an understanding of the resources that could be used to support
regulatory processes in this learning domain.
The literature review was structured around the following research questions:
RQa) What features were used to foster the regulation of learning in introductory
programming?; RQb) Which aspects of the regulation of learning were explored
in the interventions?; and RQ c) What results were achieved by the proposed in-
terventions?
The following keywords were used to reflect the scope of this study: [self-regulat*
OR SRL OR metacognit* OR co-regulat* OR SSRL OR "regulation of learning"]
AND ["learning programming" OR "programming learning" OR "teaching pro-
gramming" OR "programming teaching" OR "novice programmer" OR "program-
ming education"]. These keywords were selected based on previous reviews of
the regulation of learning in other educational areas and on research in program-
ming education. Since there are interchangeable theoretical concepts between
metacognition and self-regulation, the term metacognit* was also included in the
research [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011].
ACM, IEEE, Science Direct, and Eric databases were used due to their extensive
use in computer science research. The established inclusion criteria were: i) the
pedagogical context is introductory programming; ii) studies have mentioned
theoretical constructs of SRL; iii) included the participation of students in a quasi-
experimental or experimental investigation; iv) must have used a control group;
and v) have proposed an intervention. If the intervention was not described suf-
ficiently to allow its replication, the study was not selected for inclusion. Papers
published until June of 2020 were considered for review.
RQa) What features were used to foster the regulation of learning in introduc-
tory programming?
Fifteen studies met the inclusion criteria (Table 4.1). The data indicate that the six
years prior to the review account for 80% of the publications, suggesting that this
is a field of research that has recently been expanding. Another important finding
44

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.1: The proposed model.
is that the earliest studies date back to 1994, with most subsequent publications
starting from 2014. There is a gap of 20 years between these two periods until
the field of self-regulation began to be more extensively studied in programming
education.
The scaffoldings used by the identified studies were categorized into nine areas
shown in Table 4.2. Most scaffoldings were adapted from other learning domains,
ranging from minor modifications to complete adaptations for the programming
education domain. For example, [Alhazbi, 2014] used a typical learning journal
where students had to write weekly about their study experiences, challenges,
and other aspects. This represents general-purpose actions that could be per-
formed regardless of the educational context. In contrast, [Loksa and Ko, 2016]
developed a training program based on the problem-solving and SRL processes
targeted to the programming education specificities.
A model (Figure 4.1) based on the proposed scaffolding classification was de-
veloped aiming to enhance the comprehension of how these resources could be
utilized to support programming learning. The model is divided into three parts:
a list of scaffoldings (center), SRL phases stimulated by them (left side), and the
corresponding learning strategies they could activate (right side). The defini-
tion of how the scaffoldings relate to these phases and areas of regulation were
grounded in the self-regulation theory, and in the findings from the studies in
which these scaffoldings were investigated.
The phases are Zimmerman’s model representation of how regulation occurs
through learning tasks [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. These phases are repre-
sented into three categories, namely forethought, performance, and self-reflection
[Panadero, 2017]. Forethought refers to the initial set of strategies performed dur-
ing learning, such as task analysis, self-assessment of one’s own knowledge re-
lated to the task, and activation of self-motivational beliefs. These strategies form
the base for the performance phase, representing the actions performed during
the execution of the learning task. Lastly, the self-reflection phase allows students
to assess their performance and reflect on areas that require improvement.
SRL can also be categorized based on the mental processes that are activated

Table 4.2: Scaffoldings identified in the reviewed studies, and the type of regula-
tory process (G.P . = general purpose and P .S. = programming-specific)
through self-regulation [Dignath and Büttner, 2008]. According to Boekaerts [1999],
these processes can be referenced as "Learning strategies" and could be classified
as metacognitive knowledge, metacognitive strategies, cognitive strategies, moti-
vational strategies, and managerial strategies. Metacognitive knowledge refers to
a person’s understanding of their own cognitive processes. Metacognitive strate-
gies are the resources used to monitor and control cognitive processes. Cognitive
strategies are resources used to structure and organize the conceptual knowledge
for a task. Through the learning process, motivational strategies serve as the driv-
ing force for students to self-regulate and encompass various elements, such as
task value and self-efficacy. Lastly, managerial strategies assist students in coor-
dinating their behavior toward achieving their educational goals.
The proposed model delineates the SRL phases and corresponding learning strate-
gies that scaffoldings aim to activate. These scaffoldings might intersect with
multiple phases or prompt various psychological processes. For example, Al-
hazbi [2014] used learning journals to engage students in planning their learning
(forethought phase), tracking their progress (performance phase), and reflecting
upon their accomplishments (self-reflection phase). Regarding the mental pro-
cesses, the proposed learning journal sought to enhance students’ metacognitive
knowledge of how they learn and act as a metacognitive strategy by facilitat-
ing performance monitoring. Lastly, through the documentation of the learning
journal, students can praise their achievements, which is a type of motivational
regulation [Thompson, 1991].
The model aims to serve as a guide for researchers to identify resources that can
47

Chapter 4
be used to foster regulatory processes in programming education. As the model
encapsulates insights gleaned from the literature review, thereby inherits its lim-
itations, such as the scarce availability of scaffoldings tailored to programming
tasks and the lower focus on regulatory processes in collaborative learning set-
tings.
RQb) Which aspects of the regulation of learning were explored in the inter-
ventions?
The identified scaffoldings were analyzed to investigate how they adhered to the
SRL theory. In this sense, the studies were categorized according to the phases
of Zimmerman’s model they stimulated, due to the relevance of this model in
studying SRL processes [Panadero, 2017]. Furthermore, it was observed which
psychological processes these resources aimed to stimulate, following the classi-
fication proposed by Pintrich, which classifies them as of cognitive, metacogni-
tive, motivational, and behavioral nature [Schunk, 2005]. Finally, the classifica-
tion of the regulatory processes into general-purpose and programming-specific
[Falkner et al., 2015], was also used in the analysis.
Scaffoldings mostly focused on the performance phase (78.5%), followed by fore-
thought stimuli (71.4%), and self-reflection (64.2%). The emphasis on supporting
students’ planning and monitoring processes, aligns with reviews from different
educational contexts [Dignath and Büttner, 2008]. This emphasis has also its roots
in programming education research, which often highlights the planning process
and code creation, which is situated within the performance phase, as the foun-
dation of learning in this field [Robins, 2019]. This finding raises some questions
regarding the need to consider new formats of stimuli for self-assessment, as well
as investigating how this process impacts programming learning.
The identified scaffoldings primarily targeted general-purpose learning strate-
gies, such as establishing study routines and evaluating one’s understanding of
a topic [Alhazbi, 2014]. On the contrary, programming-specific scaffoldings, such
as guiding students in the problem-solving process [Loksa and Ko, 2016], had a
lower number of studies. The data available limits the understanding regarding
the causes of this greater emphasis on general regulatory processes. One hy-
pothesis is that, given this theme is still underexplored [Loksa et al., 2022], initial
studies might have preferred to incorporate interventions already validated in
other areas (e.g., learning journal) into the context of programming. At the same
time, this finding represented a research opportunity and a key motivator for the
research conducted in this doctoral work, leading to the development of regu-
latory scaffolding aimed specifically at aiding students in the code development
process.
The most stimulated psychological processes were metacognition and cognition,
followed by motivation and behavior. Encouraging knowledge revision and sup-
porting the organization of the problem-solving process exemplify metacognitive
and cognitive actions, respectively. In the motivational area, stimuli were ob-
served for the student to establish learning metrics and appraise their progress.
Finally, behavioral actions are present in organizing study routines, and eliminat-
48

RESPE development and evaluation
ing distractions, among others.
Some studies explored social data (e.g., allowing students to compare their progress
with their peers). However, they focused on using this data to support individual
regulation (SRL) and not to orchestrate group work. As a result, aspects related
to social regulation were not explored. This finding could suggest a lack of re-
search in this area or a potential lack of awareness among researchers regarding
the theory of regulation of learning, despite utilizing its constructs. This partic-
ular aspect has been previously reported as a challenge in summarizing studies
on the regulation of learning, as mentioned in earlier studies [García-Pérez et al.,
2020; Loksa et al., 2022].
RQc) What results were achieved by the proposed interventions?
Studies were interested in assessing whether the use of scaffoldings contributed
to programming learning and/or the development of SRL skills. The combined
evidence suggests that these resources supported programming learning (12 out
of 15 studies achieved favorable results for the experimental group). However,
some inconsistencies were observed, as some interventions successfully stimu-
lated programming learning but failed to foster regulation of learning devel-
opment, and vice versa. Due to the higher number of studies with favorable
outcomes for learning support, it is assessed that the development of regulatory
skills presents greater complexity, which justifies further studies on this topic to
evaluate the factors that may contribute to this.
Main findings
Despite SRL being a topic investigated for decades, the small quantity of studies
demonstrates that there is still much to be explored on this theme in the domain
of programming education. Initial evidence summarized in this work helps un-
derstand the scaffoldings that were used to foster regulatory processes in pro-
gramming education, their effectiveness, and current limitations. One of them
was the small number of programming-specific scaffoldings that were explored,
indicating that the field needs to mature to integrate theoretical aspects of SRL
with specific elements of programming learning. Moreover, there was an empha-
sis on the regulatory process within individuals, leaving the research on the social
regulatory process in programming education least investigated.
Advancements were observed, but most studies showed little alignment with
self-regulation theory, either due to the lack of grounding in theoretical models of
the field or due to the superficial description of regulatory processes, an issue also
reported by Loksa et al. [2022] in their literature review. The findings reveal open
questions that deserve further research, such as how can programming-specific
regulatory scaffoldings be designed and how can social regulation be fostered
in programming activities. This doctorate sought to contribute to investigating
these issues.
49

Chapter 4
4.1.2 RQ2) How to structure collaborative activities in program-
ming?
A systematic literature review was also performed to answer RQ2. The objec-
tive was to understand how existing literature structured programming collabo-
rative activities in CSCL environments and to what extent social regulation was
explored in this context. Five research questions guided the literature review
process: RQa) What collaborative resources were utilized?; RQb) Which resources
have shown the highest effectiveness? RQc) What aspects were measured in these
experiments? RQ d) How was collaboration structured? RQ e) How was collabo-
rative work assessed?
The following keywords were used to filter studies: cscl [“learning program-
ming” OR “programming learning” OR ”teaching programming” OR ”program-
ming teaching” OR "novice programmer”]. Keyword selection aimed to identify
CSCL environments for programming learning. The inclusion criteria were i)
pedagogical context must be related to the introductory programming concepts;
ii) collaboration was structured in a CSCL environment; iii) must be a quasi-
experimental, experimental, or observational study.
Twenty-seven studies were selected for review based on the established criteria
(Table 4.3). Collaboration occurred mainly in two formats: a) pair programming
(n=13) and b) group programming with more than two students (n=14). Pair
programming, a well-known technique in software development, was replicated
in programming education in two different arrangements. The first arrangement
was a cooperative format, where a student with higher knowledge guided and
supported another student. The second arrangement was collaborative, where
students worked together to solve programming tasks. In this format, students
were often assigned the roles of drivers or navigators, with the driver responsible
for writing code and the navigator reviewing it. These roles were often switched
over time.
Paper Description
[Lin et al., 2018] Used a wiki-like resource to simulate a collaborative
programming environment.
[Tsompanoudi
et al., 2016]

bilities to mimic pair-programming roles.
[Serrano-
Cámara et al.,
2016]
Employed a voting system for structuring collabora-
tive decisions.
[Lovos, 2015] Developed a Moodle plugin to allow collaborative
program development.
[Jiménez Toledo
et al., 2018]
Created a mobile application with augmented reality
to stimulate collaboration in programming exercises.
[Othman et al.,
2015]
Provided a CSCL software with features to allow

Chapter 4

Table 4.3: Selected papers for review.
In group programming, students collaborated on the same tasks, which were
usually more complex than individual tasks. The distribution of tasks among
group members followed two formats based on the level of group autonomy.
The first format was more rigid, where the problem was divided into parts by the
environment, and students were responsible for distributing these parts among
themselves. The second format was more flexible, allowing the group to decide
how to approach the problem and distribute the tasks.
Studies examined collaborative activities across different settings, including in-
formal learning environments with children and formal undergraduate courses.
The resources used to encourage collaboration varied according to the context.
Tangible objects were predominantly utilized in learning scenarios involving chil-
dren, while software tools were employed in undergraduate course settings.
In the case of tangible objects, they served as a stimulus for socialization, where
their manipulation led students to collaborate. For example, Xambó et al. [2017]
built an interactive table where different elements were placed on it, and arrang-
ing them in the correct algorithmic order would play a sound sequence. In this
way, students planned and discussed how the elements should be arranged on
the table. In more formal environments, the use of less playful programming en-
vironments was observed, comprising collaborative programming editors with
chat features. These studies operated under the assumption that providing these
resources would be sufficient for students to effectively structure their group
work. However, evidence points out that students often need to receive support
to manage their cognitive, metacognitive, and motivational processes during col-
laborative activities [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011; Järvelä et al., 2019].
Main findings
CSCL environments have been explored by researchers with a greater emphasis
on supporting learning gains. The main rationale found is that the interactions
resulting from teamwork facilitate learning through shared knowledge construc-
tion. This aspect highlights a strong influence on the study of cognitive processes,
with a smaller number of investigations into metacognitive and cognitive pro-
cesses.
As noted in the previous review, most studies lacked a thorough theoretical foun-
dation, in this context, in the literature on collaborative learning and CSCL. This
limitation may have led to studies primarily focusing on providing computa-
tional resources to facilitate collaborative coding processes (e.g., through chat
tools or collaborative editors), rather than delving into how psychological pro-
cesses could be orchestrated to enhance collaboration, which is a more commonly
52

RESPE development and evaluation
addressed topic in CSCL literature in other domains [Miller and Hadwin, 2015].
It is assumed that students will be able to collaborate effectively simply because
they have access to the resources for it to happen. However, there are several
studies indicating that students often encounter difficulties in collaborative work
and that in many cases, they require some facilitator for their regulation processes
to occur properly at both individual and group levels [Hadwin et al., 2017].
Lastly, the review identified a notable gap in the use of the social regulation of
learning theory. Meanwhile, the current evidence underscores the critical role of
social regulatory processes in fostering effective shared knowledge construction
[Järvelä et al., 2019]. This doctorate work sought to reduce this gap by investigat-
ing how the social regulation of learning could be incorporated into collaborative
programming tasks.
4.1.3 RQ3) How can a CSCL be designed to foster regulation of
learning among programming students?
Critics argue that research on self-regulation of learning in programming edu-
cation lacks a deep understanding of how these two fields connect theoretically
[Loksa et al., 2022]. This issue has also been found in the reviews discussed in
previous sections, demonstrating important gaps in the literature, such as the
limited regulatory support offered during coding development and group pro-
gramming activities. Shortening this gap involves researching new formats of
regulatory scaffolding and aligning them with the programming education speci-
ficities. Both aspects were investigated in this doctorate work and this research
question sought to describe the theoretical foundations for the scaffoldings used
in RESPE.
Regulatory scaffolding development requires establishing the theoretical basis
that will support its design [Azevedo and Aleven, 2013]. The pedagogical design
of the scaffoldings proposed in this thesis was theoretically influenced by differ-
ent authors in the field of self-regulation in general, as well as by researchers who
have begun to incorporate this theory into programming education. In this sense,
a fundamental part of the process involves understanding how regulation occurs
in its most general sense, and then evaluating how regulation occurs at specific
moments in programming, such as coding.
The work of Barry Zimmerman and Paul Pintrich has had a profound influence
on research on self-regulation [Schunk, 2005]. The theoretical framework pro-
posed by these authors to describe the regulatory process as being carried out in
phases (e.g., planning, monitoring, and reviewing) and related to different mental
processes (e.g., cognition, metacognition, motivation), is still an important con-
ceptualization for describing how regulation occurs in education [Schunk and
Zimmerman, 2023]. The definitions proposed by these authors were also impor-
tant for this research, having influenced how the scaffoldings were structured and
the mental processes that they aimed to activate. Thus, the design of the RESPE’s
scaffoldings aimed to support students’ planning, monitoring learning, and re-
flection. In particular, with a focus on cognitive and metacognitive processes.
53

Chapter 4
Expanding the discussion on how the SRL theory integrates with the constructs
of programming education is crucial for designing regulatory scaffoldings that
meet the educational needs of this area. In this context, an important starting
point is the work of Falkner et al. [2014] which investigated the regulatory strate-
gies adopted by programming students and identified that these involve a combi-
nation of general-use regulatory strategies acquired throughout life, with others
specific to the programming area. This conceptualization is also important for
this thesis, which proposes a combination of scaffoldings aimed at stimulating
these two types of regulatory strategies.
One important contribution of this thesis is the research on new formats of reg-
ulatory scaffoldings to support programming practice. As previously reported,
most studies in the existing literature focus on using general-purpose scaffold-
ings [Silva et al., 2021b]. Even though they have their contributions [Falkner
et al., 2014], programming learning comprises unique elements known to be of
difficulty for students [Qian and Lehman, 2017]. Consequently, the pedagogi-
cal support might have to be adapted to consider these specificities, such as in
the coding development process where students have to translate understanding
about a problem into programming requirements. Tailoring pedagogical support
to accommodate these unique requirements is crucial, emphasizing the need for
scaffolding that reflects the specialized nature of programming education [Volet
and Lund, 1994].
The literature review previously discussed in Section 4.1.1, points to regulatory
scaffoldings tailored to the programming education domain. To illustrate, Prather
et al. [2019] introduced a tool that prompts students to identify the inputs and
outputs of an algorithm based on the problem under analysis. Inputs and out-
puts are an important topic in code development, and the authors hypothesized
that their identification before coding would lead to performance improvement.
This example highlights how scaffolding could prompt students to perform ac-
tions unique to the field of programming education. In this thesis, new scaffold-
ing formats were proposed, which help to broaden the understanding of how to
stimulate regulatory actions during programming learning.
Another novelty proposed in this doctorate was the design of scaffolding to sup-
port social regulation in programming education. This is a topic with even fewer
research studies, and it is necessary to investigate how this type of support can
be offered to students and its impacts on learning [Prather et al., 2022]. From
the studies identified in the literature, there is also a greater focus on stimulat-
ing general-use regulatory processes [Silva et al., 2023c], and this thesis sought to
investigate how social support could be included in programming practice.
The design principles proposed by Järvelä et al. [2014] established the basis for the
social regulatory scaffoldings proposed. These principles include elements that
are crucial for stimulating social regulation, such as 1) making students aware of
their learning processes, as well as those of their peers; 2) allowing students to
externalize their learning processes; 3) providing support to activate regulatory
processes. The social regulatory support proposed in this doctorate incorporates
these theoretical principles that were adapted to the context of collaborative pro-
gramming activities.
54

RESPE development and evaluation
The scaffolding propositions discussed below can be integrated into existing tech-
nological learning environments or adapted for use in the classroom. In the latter
case, professors can use the actions that the scaffoldings aim to stimulate to con-
duct them independently or ask their students to perform them, even without
computational resources. For example, one of the scaffoldings aims to encourage
the recognition that regulatory actions are important in programming learning
and to educate students on what they can do. This role can also be taken by
the teacher, who can include this regulatory training as part of the programming
learning process, a topic that has gained prominence [Prather et al., 2020]. Other
resources are more technology-dependent, such as monitoring programming er-
rors. The computer’s storage and processor capacity were used to record stu-
dents’ programming errors during learning and summarize this data (e.g., using
graphics) to make them aware of the programming areas they need to improve.
Resources like these can be easily integrated into traditional programming devel-
opment environments.
