56

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.2: RESPE’s scaffoldings categorization by Zimmerman’s SRL phases.
The model described in Section 4.1.1 was an important starting point for the de-
velopment of the RESPE’s scaffoldings. For example, the model presents the use
of graphics as a resource that can be used to foster regulatory processes, this
knowledge was used in the design of the Learning dashboard, which uses different
graphical formats to present learning metrics so that the student can recognize
their progress and limitations. Question prompts were extensively used in the
Code planner to guide students in planning their code development process. Feed-
back was another resource incorporated to enable the student to become aware
of their progress, particularly in the scaffoldings ofExercise feedback, Error analysis
and Course progress. Finally, training motivated the development of SRL Tips that
seeks to train students in certain aspects of self-regulation.
Zimmerman’s conceptualization of SRL occurrence in phases also influenced the
design of the scaffoldings. Figure 4.2 illustrates the distribution of each scaffold
across the SRL phases.
Scaffoldings are presented to students at specific moments in the learning process
and can be either mandatory or manual. To illustrate, the SRL Tips scaffolding
is a mandatory scaffolding in the sense it is presented regardless of students’
interaction with the software. On the other hand, the Help-seeking scaffolding
exemplifies a manual scaffolding that demands students click on a button to be
triggered.
Workflow of scaffoldings usage
The following paragraphs describe how students could use scaffoldings and pro-
vide the rationale behind some pedagogical decisions. Describing the theoretical
57

Chapter 4
Figure 4.3: Scaffolding usage in the RESPE platform.
foundation for the designed scaffoldings is important to increase the understand-
ing of how self-regulation can be incorporated into programming education. Ac-
cording to Loksa et al. [2022], one fragility in the current literature on SRL in
programming education is the lack of a deeper discussion of how these two ar-
eas integrate. The presented knowledge is expected to be one contribution of this
doctorate to support future researchers interested in this theme.
The figures in this section present the wireframes used in the first design ver-
sion of each scaffolding, followed by the figure representing its implementation
in the RESPE. Wireframes were used in an expert evaluation that assessed the
scaffoldings’ adequacy in supporting programming learning and alignment with
the regulation of learning theory.
Figure 4.3 shows the sequence of using regulatory scaffoldings. These have been
grouped into general-purpose, used in any educational context, and programming-
specific, specialized to programming.
Upon logging into RESPE, students visualize aSRL tip in a popup window. These
tips were randomly selected from 20 sentences that aim to teach regulatory strate-
gies. Some examples include: "Search for other sources of information, use videos
on YouTube, lesson materials from other teachers, and websites" and "Collaborate
with fellow students to engage in group activities. Coordinate a mutually conve-
nient day and time, come prepared with your questions, and assist your peers.
This collaboration is of utmost importance!".
The SRL tip was designed to enhance students’ metacognitive knowledge, which
pertains to their understanding of the learning strategies they can employ [Veen-
man et al., 2006]. It also aimed to stimulate motivational interest in taking spe-
cific actions, thereby facilitating behavioral changes that promote effective learn-
ing [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. This knowledge aims to expand students’
repertoire of regulatory strategies and cultivate their interest in self-regulation,
crucial aspects for their engagement in regulatory practice [Schunk and Zimmer-
man, 2023].
After dismissing the tip, and once per week, a learning journal was presented
58

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.4: The learning journal wireframe.
(Wireframe in Figure 4.4 and software version in Figure 4.5). The learning journal
is a question-based scaffolding designed to activate cognitive, metacognitive, mo-
tivational, and behavioral processes [Schwendimann et al., 2018]. Open-ended
questions were used to stimulate students’ planning behavior, such as "How do
you plan your study week?" and reflective processes, such as "How was your last
week of studies?", which relates to cognitive and metacognitive processes, re-
spectively. Closed questions prompted students to set weekly learning goals, in-
cluding the number of exercises and hours dedicated to studying programming.
According to Schunk, goal setting should be one of the initial steps for students as
it provides a reference for their desired achievements and serves as an evaluative
criterion, which are essential aspects for learning regulation [Schunk, 1990]. Ad-
ditionally, students were encouraged to assess their self-efficacy level and motiva-
tion to sustain their studies, both constructs are relevant to the learning process,
and reflecting upon them is an important learning strategy [Garcia and Pintrich,
1994].
Learning journal enabled students to plan and reflect on their learning processes,
elements associated with the forethought and reflection phases [Panadero, 2017].
These actions are important for students to guide and review their learning, but
many do not do so either due to a lack of knowledge or difficulty in the process
[Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. The scaffolding sought to support students in
performing these actions.
The learning dashboard was presented after students completed the learning jour-
59

Chapter 4
Figure 4.5: RESPE’s learning journal scaffolding.
60

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.6: The learning dashboard wireframe.
nal (Figure 4.6 presents the wireframe and Figure 4.7 presents the software ver-
sion). The dashboard uses graphics to display learning metrics, including the
course progress and achievement of goals established in the learning journal (e.g.,
number of exercises completed in the week). A time-series chart exhibited the
programming errors made throughout the course, and personalized recommen-
dations were made to assist students on how to address these errors. This data
is important for metacognitive monitoring, allowing students to recognize areas
of improvement and where they have made progress. Furthermore, recognizing
educational progress can enhance students’ motivation to sustain their studies
[Schunk, 1990].
The learning dashboard aligns with the principles of the Open Learning model
by allowing students to access their learning metrics [Schumacher and Ifenthaler,
2021]. This strategy has been demonstrated to be a practical feature in supporting
self-regulated learning [Chou and Zou, 2020; Hooshyar et al., 2020].
Students used a menu to access RESPE’s other scaffoldings and functionalities,
such as the ranking to visualize the top five students in the course, visualize their
previous learning journals, and interact with the course material. They were free
to decide which of these features they would use.
The ranking was designed to foster extrinsic motivation, which is a factor that
can stimulate students’ self-regulation [Kamberi and Danuza, 2022; Moos, 2014].
However, worth noting that scholars hold different views regarding utilizing ex-
ternal motivational incentives. Some emphasize its value when combined with
intrinsic motivation [Kong et al., 2012], while others raise concerns about its po-
tential negative impacts, such as an individual who is oriented towards carrying
out tasks for the external gains it will bring [Williamson, 2015].
Course material presented a list of the subjects covered in the course (Left side
61

Chapter 4
Figure 4.7: RESPE’s learning dashboard scaffolding.
of Figure 4.8). Progress bars were used to indicate students’ performance in each
topic, a scaffolding referred to as Course progress. Making students aware of their
progress is an important metacognitive knowledge that can help them situate
how they are advancing in the course, reflect, and change their behavior if neces-
sary [Winne et al., 2013].
Clicking on a specific course subject reveals a list of corresponding materials,
including videos, texts, and exercises (Right side of Figure 4.8). Different col-
ors were used to indicate the completion status of each exercise: black signifies
unanswered, green denotes a correct answer, and red represents an incorrect one.
This feedback also seeks to increase students’ metacognitive knowledge, inform-
ing them about their workload and performance, which are crucial aspects to
promote their engagement in self-regulation [Seufert, 2018].
A slight modification was made in the software version (Figure 4.9) and instead
of separating the materials from exercises, a single list was used and organized
them in a sequential order. This design aimed to help students understand what
is the sequential order they should undertake during learning.
Exercises were categorized into multiple-choice questions, Parsons problems, and
programming code development exercises. These types of exercises were com-
bined at different moments of learning following Bloom’s taxonomy as it has
proven relevant in organizing programming concepts [Masapanta-Carrión and
Velázquez-Iturbide, 2018]. For instance, in the first moment, students interacted
with multiple-choice questions (Figure 4.10) that posed questions related to pro-
gramming concepts and syntax, which addressed the "remember" and "under-
stand" phases of Bloom’s taxonomy. [McGill and Volet, 1997] defines this knowl-
edge as "declarative-syntactic" and "declarative-conceptual", and they form the
basis for applying programming knowledge to code development.
An incorrect answer in the multiple-choice exercises triggered feedback indicat-
62

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.8: The list of course subjects and exercises wireframe.
ing what was the correct alternative and an explanation. This feedback is part
of a scaffolding named Exercise feedback and this information is essential to learn-
ing, enabling students to reconstruct inconsistent mental models [Panadero and
Lipnevich, 2022]. From a self-regulatory perspective, feedback is an important
resource to make students aware of their limitations and identify areas for im-
provement [Chou and Zou, 2020].
Subsequently, Parsons exercises were introduced, where students began the pro-
cess of understanding how to combine programming elements into a functional
code aiming to stimulate the "apply" and "analyze" phases of the taxonomy. Being
able to construct programming code is defined as "procedural-conceptual knowl-
edge" [McGill and Volet, 1997].
Parsons problems present a programming code divided into multiple parts and
students have to organize them in the correct sequence mitigating the possibility
of making syntax errors (Figure 4.11). By using this pedagogical approach, stu-
dents can focus on understanding and combining programming elements, elim-
inating the issues that arise with syntax errors [Garcia et al., 2018b]. They can
be challenging to overcome and demotivate students at this critical early stage
of programming [Coto et al., 2021]. Parsons problem effectiveness has been re-
ported in the literature, and evidence suggests that it is effective in supporting
learning as traditional code writing [Ericson et al., 2017].
The interface for Parsons problems is shown in Figure 4.11. Students visualized
a list of code snippets in an unordered manner (left side of the image) and had to
arrange them correctly (right side).
Multiple-choice questions were presented again to foster the practice of reading
programming codes and predicting their output. This ability relates to the "an-
alyze" and "evaluate" phases and indicates that students can explain code frag-
63

Chapter 4
Figure 4.9: RESPE’s materials exercises list.
64

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.10: Multiple choice exercise example.
Figure 4.11: RESPE’s parsons problems editor.
65

Chapter 4
Figure 4.12: The code planner wireframe.
ments and predict algorithms’ output [McGill and Volet, 1997].
Finally, open-ended questions were introduced, and students were required to
construct their own codes. The first exercises of this type included codes miss-
ing some parts for the student to complete, while as they progressed through the
course, this support was removed, and they were expected to build their codes
independently. These two situations correspond to the "evaluate" and "create"
phases, respectively, and represent students’ capability to integrate prior pro-
gramming knowledge as classified by McGill and Volet [1997] effectively.
Prior to answering open-ended exercises, students were presented with a scaf-
folding named code planner (Wireframe shown in Figure 4.12 and RESPE’s inter-
face in Figure 4.13). The objective was to guide students in planning their coding
process. Despite the importance of task planning, it is often neglected by students
[VanDeGrift et al., 2011], resulting in difficulties in program creation [Arakawa
et al., 2021].
Code planner was a question-based scaffolding comprised of the following ques-
tions: "What is the problem you have to work on in this exercise?" along with
personalized questions based on the exercise’s characteristics. For example, if
the exercise required the student to use conditionals, the following question was
66

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.13: RESPE’s code planner scaffolding.
presented: "What are the conditionals required in this exercise?". Completing the
code planner was a requirement to access the programming editor (Figure 4.14).
The scaffolding’s pedagogical design was influenced by Winne and Hadwin’s
definition of task conditions, particularly the concept of instructional cues [Winne,
2017]. In this sense, the questions guided students’ cognitive processing by mak-
ing them perform actions that could improve task success and foster SRL [Loksa
and Ko, 2016].
The programming interface (Figure 4.14 contains a set of scaffoldings to support
code development. As students execute their code, feedback on their correctness
was automatically provided. The test cases associated with each exercise will be
compared to the code’s output to assess their correctness. This feedback is in-
strumental in programming education because students often have difficulties in
monitoring their program progress, believing they have a correct or near correct
solution when, in fact, they do not [Prather et al., 2018]. According to Winne and
Hadwin, providing feedback is crucial for students to develop a consciousness
of their performance and oversee their learning process [Panadero, 2017]. Fur-
thermore, explicit knowledge of the evaluative criteria is essential for effective
cognitive processing [Greene and Azevedo, 2007].
The presented wireframe resulted in the interface shown in Figure 4.15. The pro-
gramming editor was implemented using Microsoft’s Monaco library4, the same
library used in the Microsoft VSCode editor. It enabled Python’s syntax high-
lighting and had features that allowed the adaptation of the editor according to
the needs of the proposed scaffoldings. For example, as part of the Error feedback
scaffolding, when the student committed an error, the code line that triggered
the error was highlighted (using the Monaco API) and a supportive message was
displayed to assist them in their correction.
4https://microsoft.github.io/monaco-editor/
67

Chapter 4
Figure 4.14: The programming editor wireframe.
Figure 4.15: RESPE’s programming editor.
68

RESPE development and evaluation
During the code development process, the RESPE platform gathered various stu-
dent data such as their programming code, error messages, and interpreter out-
put. This data was used to offer tailored feedback. For example, the scaffolding
Error visualization used syntax errors to build a pie chart (presented in Figure 4.14)
that summarized the main errors committed during the exercise. Students could
use this information to build awareness about their difficulties, which are often
unknown to them [Arakawa et al., 2021].
Syntax error data was also used by theError analysis and Intelligent agent scaffold-
ings. Error analysis consisted of personalized guidance messages on overcoming
the committed error. This is necessary because error messages presented by the
programming languages are often confusing and of little support for beginners
[Denny et al., 2020]. Thus, the scaffolding presented possible causes of the com-
mitted error, and suggestions for corrections. The student still had to analyze
their code because the scaffolding did not present the fixing necessary for their
problem.
The implementation of the error analysis scaffolding employed regular expres-
sions to analyze the output from Python’s interpreter and the student’s submit-
ted code, extracting pertinent information. This extracted data was then fed into a
rule-based system designed to assess the likelihood of the error being attributable
to specific causes. Based on this assessment, a tailored error message pinpoint-
ing the presumed cause is displayed to the student, replacing the generic out-
put Python would typically provide. This approach is intended to simplify the
student’s comprehension of the error, addressing the common difficulty students
face when interpreting standard programming error messages [Smith and Rixner,
2019]. Additionally, this information supports an intelligent agent that tracks the
students’ error patterns and offers individualized recommendations for supple-
mentary learning resources.
The intelligent agent was also an additional support in case of programming er-
rors. It suggested complementary learning materials related to students’ diffi-
culties. Both scaffoldings were inspired by the co-regulation concept, where one
individual’s regulatory process is guided by another [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011].
In a classroom context, co-regulation can be facilitated by a peer or a professor.
However, as human support may not always be accessible, an automated com-
putational interface was designed as a complementary resource.
Error correction often involves mental debugging, in which students navigate
mentally through their code and think about their behavior. However, it is a dif-
ficult skill that needs time to mature and novices might struggle with [Smith and
Rixner, 2019]. To assist in the debug process, aDebug scaffolding was included in
the programming interface. This tool allowed students to navigate through their
code in a step-by-step process, visualizing their code execution flow and variable
values, important data for error correction. Even though debugging is not a new
resource in programming development, it often presents a complex interface that
might cause difficulties in their usage. Therefore, a minimalist design was cho-
sen, demanding the exclusion of some traditional debugging features, such as the
possibility to use the breakpoints.
69

Chapter 4
Debugging was a manually activated scaffolding that depended on students’ ex-
plicit intention of using it. From a regulatory standpoint, debug data could fa-
cilitate metacognitive monitoring. For example, a student might have some hy-
potheses about their code execution flow and use the debug to validate them.
This knowledge can be used to adjust cognitive strategies accordingly towards
error correction.
If the available support was deemed insufficient, students encountering chal-
lenges had the option to engage with the Exercise Solution and Help Seeking scaf-
foldings. In utilizing the Exercise Solution, students were presented with a model
answer comprising programming code developed by the course instructor, de-
signed to illustrate an approach to solving the exercise. To prevent misuse of this
functionality, students were notified that utilizing the feature would result in the
exercise not being graded. However, students who complete the exercise without
employing this feature will be allowed to view the teacher’s solution afterward
without incurring any penalty, and this information is also presented to them.
The intention is to encourage students to compare their work, explore alterna-
tive solutions, and self-evaluate their performance, vital aspects of self-regulated
learning [Winne, 2017].
Help-seeking was manually triggered by a button in the programming editor in-
terface. This action generated a forum thread including a duplicate of the stu-
dent’s code and a message expressing the need for assistance in resolving the
issue. The forum could also be used by students to discuss other topics of interest
and reply to other students’ threads. A forum is valuable for facilitating student
interactions and promoting collaborative co-regulation episodes [Chan, 2012].
Scaffoldings were also designed to support collaborative problem-solving and
group work coordination. Effective collaboration requires students to self-regulate
themselves and engage in social regulation by jointly establishing shared goals
and strategies [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011]. This process is continuous and re-
quires effort, as students must actively engage, negotiate, select learning strate-
gies, seek help from peers, and adapt their learning objectives. However, shared
regulatory processes do not always occur naturally, and students may require
support [Miller and Hadwin, 2015].
Research shows that structuring the collaborative process can effectively result
in social regulation and successful collaborative episodes [Rummel and Spada,
2005]. Thus, one of the objectives of the scaffolding named Collaborative code
planner was to guide students in engaging in productive collaboration by stim-
ulating the realization of certain actions. Firstly, the aim was to encourage stu-
dents to perform a self-assessment of their knowledge to activate self-regulation
processes, which is crucial for social regulation [Panadero et al., 2015]. Sub-
sequently, engagement in social interactions began through knowledge sharing
among group members and actions directed toward orchestrating task execution.
The scripting technique was employed to structure the collaborative problem-
solving process in the collaborative code planner. This method was previously
utilized in CSCL research to direct students towards engaging in activities and
roles critical for collective knowledge construction [Miller and Hadwin, 2015]. In
70

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.16: The collaborative code planner wireframe.
this study, scripting was applied to delineate the sequence of actions that group
members were expected to follow. This process occurred in three distinct phases:
i) analyzing the problem, ii) planning the exercise solution, and iii) collabora-
tively developing the code.
During the problem-analysis phase (Wireframe presented in Figure 4.16 and RE-
SPE’s interface in Figure 4.17), students were presented with the exercise state-
ment and prompted to articulate their understanding and self-assess their abil-
ity to tackle the task. The following question was used to trigger the analysis:
"How difficult do you find this question?" and had to be answered with a Likert
scale from 1 (easy) to 5 (difficult). Students were asked to justify their rating and
pinpoint any expected challenges in completing the exercise. These responses
were then shared with their group to encourage dialogue and the examination
of diverse viewpoints. To ensure unbiased responses, group answers were only
revealed after a student submitted their own answer, preventing any influence
from their peers’ responses.
Reflecting on a task’s difficulty and self-assessing involve both cognitive and
metacognitive processes [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. There is a demand
for logical reasoning to grasp the exercise’s requirements and applying program-
ming knowledge to identify the necessary resources to develop a computational
71

Chapter 4
Figure 4.17: RESPE’s collaborative problem-analysis.
solution [Robins, 2019]. Self-assessment plays a vital role here, as individuals
might not fully understand the task’s requirements or may lack the necessary
programming knowledge. From a self-regulatory standpoint, this knowledge
is essential as it lays the groundwork for future regulatory actions [Schunk and
Zimmerman, 2023].
Gaining knowledge of their peer’s opinions and self-assessments is a crucial as-
pect of social regulation [Järvelä et al., 2016]. It represents an essential step to
establish a baseline in which the group members share common knowledge re-
garding the exercise. It can also reveal any misunderstanding of the task require-
ment and be used to correct inconsistent students’ mental models. Lastly, the
difficulties that were externalized can generate co-regulation episodes through-
out the activity [Chan, 2012; Järvelä et al., 2019]. The externalization of individual
limitations and perspectives on the task among group members is a fundamental
step for the group’s initial alignment and engagement in social regulation.
In the collaborative code planning second phase, the student group had to for-
mulate a strategy to address the programming challenge (refer to Figure 4.16).
A crucial aspect of this stage is appointing a team leader responsible for guid-
ing the group’s task management, as suggested in the literature [Schellens et al.,
2007]. The group begins by clarifying the specific problem they need to resolve,
prompted by the question, "What is the problem that your team will be tackling?".
Following this, they were tasked with identifying and agreeing on the program-
ming constructs necessary to construct a solution. To facilitate these discussions
and plan documentation, a collaborative editor is employed, allowing all group
members to view and contribute to the content synchronously.
Students had access to textual and voice-based chat (bottom right of the pictures)
where group members could interact. To develop this resource, the RxJS5 library
5https://rxjs.dev/
72

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.18: The collaborative code planner.
was used for reactive asynchronous programming.
After completing the two stages the team leader could confirm the completion of
the collaborative planner. Once this was done, participants were directed to the
programming editor interface, which was similar to the one presented in Figure
4.14. Resources to foster collaboration were used, including live code synchro-
nization, enabling all group members to work on the same programming code
simultaneously.
The effectiveness of group work can be significantly compromised by inadequate
interaction during collaborative learning tasks, necessitating structured support
for effective communication and joint problem-solving [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011].
In response, the collaborative intelligent agent was designed to enhance these pro-
cesses by monitoring the students’ chat interactions. When a lack of engagement
is detected, the agent prompts the student with messages to encourage active par-
ticipation, such as "Your active involvement is crucial for the group’s success,"
"Remember to engage with your peers!" and "Encountering challenges? Discuss
them with your group; they might offer the support you need!" Additionally,
the agent provides strategic advice to refine the group’s approach to problem-
solving, suggesting actions like recalling previous experiences with similar prob-
lems as a foundation for tackling the current task. The agent also supplies guide-
lines to facilitate effective teamwork and problem-resolution coordination.
The above-described pedagogical design underwent an expert review that vali-
dated the adequacy of the scaffoldings to foster regulation of learning and sup-
port programming learning. This process was performed by a Ph.D. cognitive
psychologist who specialized in SRL research and two Ph.D. computing science
professors with more than 15 years of experience in programming education re-
search.
Synchronous meetings were performed with the experts, and the scaffolding propo-
sitions were presented, along with the wireframes that illustrated their usage.
The scaffolding’s adequacy to foster learning regulation and support program-
ming learning was validated, and during this process, several questions were
73

Chapter 4
pointed out by the experts:
1. Will the student perceive the usefulness of the scaffoldings?
2. Will they be used during their learning?
3. How will they be used?
The questions raised by the experts were validated in a pilot study conducted in
the second iteration.
Technical aspects of RESPE’s development
The RESPE’s software architecture is presented in Figure 4.19. The software com-
prises two applications: a frontend and a backend, whose responsibility is pre-
senting the user interface and process programming code, respectively. Both ap-
plications were hosted in a Linux virtual machine rented from the Google Cloud
Platform. This process involved preparing the machine with the necessary soft-
ware and configuring its network to enable external access, such as setting up a
static IP and acquiring a host domain. The Nginx 6 web server was used to make
RESPE available for students’ access.
Students interacted with the frontend application that comprised the scaffoldings
shown in Table 4.4, a programming editor, and the course materials (e.g., videos
and text). This application was written in Typescript/Javascript using the Angu-
lar framework7 and PrimeNG8 to design the user interface.
The backend consisted of REST endpoints used to run students’ programming
codes and evaluate test case correctness using a Python instance. The backend
outputed the existence of syntax errors along with the Pythons’ stacktrace or the
indication of completeness for each test case (true or false). The backend was
built in the Python programming language using the Django framework 9.
Data produced in the RESPE were persisted in Firestore, a NoSQL cloud database
that is part of Google’s Firebase platform. Google offers a JavaScript SDK that
implements communication with the Firestore services, allowing data to be per-
sisted and read from the database. However, this process involved writing boiler-
plate code, slowing the development process. To address this issue and optimize
communication with the database, a library called "AngularFire Document Map-
per"10 was developed. This library is based on the Active Record Pattern 11 and
provides a simplified interface and was used by the frontend application.

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.19: Software architecture of the RESPE.
75

Chapter 4
The entity-relationship diagram is presented in Figure 4.20. For clarity, some less
important tables were omitted from the diagram. The "Subject" table represents
a topic in the course syllabus and has a 1:N relationship with the tables "Par-
sonProblem" "ClosedQuestion" and "OpenEndedQuestion" which represent the
three types of exercises used in RESPE. The "OpenEndedQuestion" table has an
optional 1:N relationship with the table "GroupExercise", indicating that it is a
collaborative activity.
For each group exercise, there were N records in the "GroupPlanning" table that
store the data written in the collaborative code planner. Group responses to the
exercise were saved in the "OpenEndedResponse" table, which has an association
with "GroupPlanning". These data were subsequently used to analyze whether
students were understanding and using these scaffoldings properly. Messages in
the Forum were persisted in "ForumMessage".
Data on students’ interactions with the RESPE were collected to comprehend if
they understood how to use the provided scaffolding. Responses to the learning
journal and code planner were recorded in the "LearningJournalResponse" and
"CodePlannerResponses" tables, and the accesses to the RESPE’s features (e.g.,
pages, scaffoldings) were recorded in the table "PageTrack". This latter data al-
lowed an analysis of how students were interacting the the RESPE, which helps
study their learning behavior [Gupta et al., 2022].
Main findings
The development of regulatory scaffoldings for programming educational soft-
ware involves integrating general-use scaffoldings with those that are specific to
the field. Literature review indicates that these two types of scaffoldings com-
plement each other and need to be jointly integrated into learning environments
[Falkner et al., 2014]. Due to the extensive research on general-use regulatory
scaffoldings in other areas, it is a seamless process their adaptation to the pro-
gramming context. However, programming-specific regulatory scaffoldings re-
quire a deeper understanding and exploration of the theoretical aspects of self-
regulated learning and programming education. This is necessary to appropri-
ately incorporate self-regulation constructs into programming practice.
Much of what Zimmerman’s model proposes reflects the learning process in pro-
gramming. There is already an established body of knowledge in programming
literature about planning actions (forethought phase of the model) and executing
programming tasks (performance). However, actions related to self-evaluation
seem to be less explored, and some propositions were made in this respect (e.g.,
scaffoldings.
The framework proposed by Pintrich regarding the mental processes that are
part of self-regulation helps guide the aspects that can be explored by scaffold-
ings. Some of these processes have been already discussed by the previous lit-
erature in programming education, and the Pintrich’s framework helps expand
the knowledge by providing a holistic view of how multiple interconnected men-
tal resources are part of the learning process. The knowledge provided by this
76

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.20: RESPE’s Entity relationship diagram.
77

Chapter 4
framework helps situate the areas that the scaffoldings could focus on.
The findings from the literature reviews conducted in RQ1 and RQ2 helped to
understand existing gaps in the field. In particular, the need for programming-
specific scaffoldings to support during programming practice and the need to
support social regulation processes. These two themes guided the design of some
scaffoldings in this doctoral research, such as the code planner, error analysis, col-
laborative code planner, and collaborative intelligent agent. The presented design
process is expected to bridge a gap in the literature, and guide new research on
how this regulatory support can be provided in the field of programming educa-
tion.
4.2 Iteration 2
The theoretical propositions developed during the initial iteration established the
educational basis for RESPE, guiding the development of its first prototype. This
version underwent validation through a pilot study aimed at addressing concerns
highlighted by experts in the preceding iteration.
4.2.1 RQ4) How do students use and evaluate the RESPE’s regu-
latory scaffoldings?
This research question aimed to investigate how students use RESPE and their
perceptions regarding the provided scaffoldings. This knowledge is important to
identify whether the provided scaffoldings were used adequately, and to investi-
gate the issues pointed out by the experts during the first iteration.
A pilot study was conducted and consisted of a regular course as described in
Section 3.4. Students were informed about the data collection procedure and the
sample consisted of those who signed the consent form. The data described in
this section are from eight students (2 females), with an average age of 30.75 years
(10.71 standard deviations). All students had access to the same RESPE version.
A multimodal data collection approach was employed, combining quantitative
and qualitative methods to gather data on the students’ RESPE usage. Quantita-
tive data consisted of records of students’ interactions with the RESPE, such as
access to pages and scaffoldings. The qualitative data included students’ entries
in the learning journal and code planner, along with responses to a structured
questionnaire that sought to obtain their perceptions regarding scaffolding us-
age. By integrating both quantitative and qualitative data, a more detailed un-
derstanding of students’ behaviors and the underlying reasons for their specific
decisions can be achieved [Järvelä et al., 2021].
78

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.21: Models of students’ learning behavior.
Quantitative analysis
Students’ interactions were collected based on their mouse clicks on the platform,
along with a timestamp indicating the moment in which it occurred. This data
allows an understanding of which resources were used and the navigation se-
quence students’ performed. To interpret this data, Markov Chains were em-
ployed to model the students’ learning behaviors within the RESPE environment.
By analyzing these models, researchers could gain insights into the diverse ways
students engaged with the platform for their learning process, enabling a com-
parison of various learning behaviors and patterns.
Previous literature used Markov models to model the learning process based on
students’ interactions with a computer-based learning environment. For exam-
ple, Gupta et al. [2022] collected a set of students’ data, and based on them, a
Markov model representing the sequence of students’ actions was built, and this
knowledge was used to comprehend how they experience the given educational
software to learn.
Markov Models are represented by a set of states along with a connection be-
tween them, representing the probability of transitioning from one state to an-
other. In this study, a Markov state is represented by the RESPE resource that a
student clicks on, which could either be access to a scaffolding or a feature that
is not a scaffolding, such as viewing the course syllabus. The collected data of
students’ interactions was used to calculate the probability of transiting from one
state to another.
Figure 4.21 presents Markov chains illustrating the navigation behavior of two
students who participated in the pilot study. The nodes are used to represent
the RESPE resources they used, and the edges indicate that the student transited
from one resource to another, along with the probability of its occurrence. The
models were simplified and omitted some states to facilitate discussion.
79

Chapter 4
Models like these help comprehend the students’ learning behavior during RE-
SPE usage. For example, it was observed that student #1 accessed the learning
dashboard 16% of the time immediately after utilizing the program editor. This
pattern suggests that the student often sought to assess their learning progress
post-coding, reflecting a metacognitive action. On the other hand, student #2 ex-
hibited a distinct approach, showing no inclination to consult the learning dash-
board after coding activities. This comparison highlights the variance in metacog-
nitive engagement among students during their interaction with RESPE.
Markov chains proved to be a productive resource in revealing how students in-
teracted with RESPE, uncovering some unforeseen uses of its features. Notably,
the code planner, designed to facilitate the planning stage of coding, was fre-
quently employed during the coding phase itself. This trend, consistent across
several students, indicates the code planner’s dual utility: not only as a planning
aid but also as a monitoring instrument during code development. Students ap-
peared to use the data entered into the code planner to reassess and structure
their coding strategy actively. This dual functionality underscores the planner’s
versatility and its integral role in supporting both planning and monitoring as-
pects of the coding process.
The data illustrates that students were capable of adapting scaffolding usage to
suit their individual learning needs, extending beyond the original intentions en-
visioned by the research. This adaptability underscores the significance of pro-
viding versatile support resources, as students could creatively integrate them
into their study routines, finding unique ways to benefit from them in their learn-
ing process.
The qualitative data analysis focused on students’ responses to the learning jour-
nal and code planner to assess their comprehension of the scaffoldings’ questions.
This evaluation helped determine if students could effectively engage with the
scaffoldings as intended. In this sense, it is necessary to consider that these scaf-
foldings asked students to perform some action (e.g., describe what their study
plan for the week would be) and that the answer should reflect what was asked.
The analysis of student responses was conducted by the author of this doctorate,
who verified the adequacy of the responses to the questions asked. A binary clas-
sification was not suitable because the cases where the answer was considered
inappropriate revealed two particular behaviors: avoidance and difficulty of use.
In the first case, the student ignored the scaffolding and did not respond, and this
behavior was most prevalent in the code planner, being constantly repeated by
a specific group of students. It was also observed that during the synchronous
meetings, some students reported dissatisfaction with having to answer ques-
tions before coding. These two data reveal a behavior known in the programming
literature, which is the perception of some students that planning is not necessary
[Veerasamy et al., 2018].
Responses from some students indicated challenges in effectively utilizing the
scaffolding. To illustrate, the learning journal prompted students to reflect on
their past week of learning. However, certain responses lacked depth, failing to
address challenges encountered or progress made, which are key components
80

RESPE development and evaluation
of metacognitive reflection expected in a learning review [Schwendimann et al.,
2018]. Such responses suggest that these students struggled to engage with the
task as intended, highlighting potential issues with how they interpreted or val-
ued the scaffolding’s purpose.
The analysis also revealed that over the initial weeks of the course, there was a
noticeable decline in engagement, particularly with the learning journal, where
the average entry length reduced from 236 characters in the first week to 60 by
the third week. Concurrently, the quality of the entries deteriorated, becoming
more superficial, a trend that was similarly observed in the responses within the
code planner. This outcome has various implications, as it suggests that the use
of scaffolding tends to decrease over time. The data collected restrict in-depth
analyses of the causes behind this behavior, which could be related to a loss of
interest or difficulties in sustained use of the resource.
Qualitative analysis
The data gathered from the questionnaire administered to the students helps in
understanding some of the behaviors observed through the use of the scaffold-
ings. The instrument was composed of open-ended questions, following the pat-
tern: "What is your opinion about using the « scaffolding name »?", where «
scaffolding name » was the name of a specific scaffolding accompanied by one
screenshot. One question was presented for each scaffolding described in Ta-
ble 4.4. The instrument was delivered online at the end of the course and was
anonymously answered. Even though standardized instruments exist to assess
educational software heuristics [Vieira et al., 2019], our objective was to use open-
ended questions specific to each feature to gain a more holistic view of students’
perceptions of them.
Students appreciated the resources made available through RESPE. The majority
acknowledged the importance of employing specific learning strategies to reach
their educational objectives but reported that they often did not engage in cer-
tain strategies. RESPE support was perceived as a valuable tool in helping the
execution of certain strategies, thereby facilitating their achievement of learning
goals:
"It is a lack of culture, at least on my part, of making plans. But I admit it is a
mistake, and I have to work it out. (The software) is a resource that I have
always needed."
The following excerpt demonstrates that the scaffolding adjusted an inappropri-
ate behavior, such as the lack of planning in code development.
"I particularly want to go to the code right away, without making a sketch, but
this (forcing) the thought of how we are going to program the code helps.
It makes me realize what I really need in the code."
81

Chapter 4
This statement also highlights a behavior well-documented in the literature: the
tendency to avoid planning code and a preference for coding without prior plan-
ning [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. The student acknowledges that the scaffolding guides
them to perform an action they would not have done without support, empha-
sizing that performing this action facilitated their programming process.
The learning dashboard was also reported as a useful feature. Presenting learning
metrics was evaluated as an important resource because it allowed the student to
assess their progress and use this knowledge to decide which subjects they would
study.
"I can follow what I did or how much I still need to do. It is useful. I used (this
resource) to change the subject I was studying."
Difficulties in using the scaffoldings were also reported, especially the use of the
learning journal and code planner.
"I confess that I did not describe detailed things. I found it difficult to abstract."
This was an intriguing result as it indicates that performing the action supported
by the learning journal may require other mental processes that the student might
find difficult to execute independently. This finding has direct implications for
the design of scaffoldings, as it is also necessary to assess which mental processes
will be directly or indirectly required for carrying out the action stimulated by
the scaffolding. Therefore, it might be necessary to consider additional forms of
support to complement any potential limitations of the students.
Students reported challenges in consistently reflecting on their learning each week.
These challenges might stem from various factors, including potential difficulties
in maintaining metacognitive activities over an extended period or dwindling en-
gagement possibly due to diminished motivation. While the exact reasons require
further exploration, the feedback suggests a need for enhancing the scaffolding to
better accommodate and support students in their reflective practices over time.
One student reported privacy issues of not knowing if the data would be shared
with their peers, which hindered their interest in exposing their personal data.
"The fact that it was not clear, at least to me, whether what I put would be visible
only to me or not, this also helped me not to make a more detailed descrip-
tion even. If the visibility of my report was clear only for my analysis, I
would be more comfortable with being detailed."
This statement suggests that students may feel uncomfortable with their data
being shared with other users. This is an important consideration, as there are
studies where the analysis was based on sharing students’ journal data with their
peers [Alhazbi, 2014]. It is necessary to consider that this could lead to unnatu-
ral behavior from students who might refrain from writing certain information.
82

RESPE development and evaluation
While this was not the case in this doctoral study, it underscores the importance
of clearly explaining to students how their data will be used.
Feedback regarding the code planner indicated that some students found the re-
source challenging to use:
"I didn’t like it very much! For people who never programmed, the information
was very complicated..."
The statement indicates the need to review the code planner. This may be related
to the user experience of the scaffolding, but it could also be connected to the issue
identified with the use of the learning journal, where it is necessary to include
additional support forms to mitigate any difficulties that will arise with the use
of the scaffolding.
Some students expressed their discontent with the code planner, deeming it re-
dundant or irrelevant to their main objective of coding. Our current data set
does not clarify whether a student’s disuse of scaffolding stems from their self-
sufficiency in task execution or a lack of awareness of the role of regulation in
learning. Nevertheless, this feedback highlights critical areas for enhancement:
educating students on the value of self-regulation, ensuring scaffoldings are more
naturally integrated into their learning journey, and tailoring these aids to the in-
dividual’s learning stage, removing them when no longer beneficial.
Insights from students’ responses call for a more personalized learning environ-
ment that accommodates their specific needs. While this thesis did address cus-
tomization to some extent (e.g., the error analysis tailored feedback based on stu-
dent interactions), it did not explore the adaptive removal of scaffoldings based
on learner proficiency. Future research directions are suggested in Section 7.
Main findings
The data collection on the use of the scaffoldings was beneficial for comprehend-
ing the usage of these resources and for investigating the students’ learning pro-
cess. The findings highlight that these resources were utilized at varying inten-
sities, which underscores the individual nature of the learning process. Different
characteristics were observed, suggesting behaviors more focused on metacog-
nition, while in other cases, there is a greater emphasis on motivational aspects,
as seen in the extensive use of the Ranking scaffolding. However, the reasons
behind students’ decisions are not clear, which is a point for future investigation.
Particularly, to assess whether the student can use the scaffoldings as a means to
mitigate their own limitations.
The responses from students to the question-based scaffolding and questionnaire
were crucial to assess the extent to which the requested tasks were understood.
Some students were unable to use the scaffoldings appropriately, which suggests
that modifications are necessary to facilitate their use. In this sense, examples of
how to answer the questions were included in the RESPE subsequent iteration.
Furthermore, the data uncovered unexpected practices, highlighting instances
83

Chapter 4
where some students bypassed the provided resources by inputting random char-
acters. This observation underscores that the mere availability of resources does
not guarantee their utilization. Therefore it is crucial to assess the factors influ-
encing scaffolding usage, such as the students’ perceptions on them. The avoid-
ance behavior might also stem from a lack of awareness among students about
their own learning barriers, coupled with a belief that they do not require the of-
fered support. These insights emphasize the necessity for educational software
designs to incorporate considerations of these dynamics. Moreover, there should
be an effort to promote engagement with scaffolding by emphasizing the role of
self-regulation as a supporting mechanism in the learning process.
4.3 Iteration 3
To address the issues identified in the pilot study, the RESPE underwent modi-
fications targeting three primary goals: enhancing student support for engaging
with question-based scaffolding, reducing instances where questions were left
unanswered, and emphasizing the value of the regulatory process. The initial
learning journal setup involved static questions presented each week, which was
revised to a dynamic format introducing questions that varied each week. To
support students in answering the questions, exemplar responses were provided,
aiming to kickstart the writing process. A similar strategy was implemented in
the code planner by offering sample outlines for planning.
To mitigate students’ avoidance of question-based scaffoldings, a minimum re-
sponse length of 50 characters was introduced. While there is an awareness that
this might lead to forced or irrelevant inputs (e.g., inputting random characters),
the author of this thesis was aware of these possibilities but it was necessary
to validate this assumption. Moreover, to enhance students’ recognition of self-
regulated learning’s importance in their educational journey, additional guidance
was incorporated into the SRL tips scaffolding.
The RESPE version after modifications was evaluated in an experiment to assess
its effectiveness in supporting students’ programming learning. To accomplish
this goal and ensure research reliability, a randomized, single-blind, controlled
experiment design of experiment was conducted, guided by the following re-
search questions:
• RQ5) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit higher performance in indi-
vidual programming exercises than students who did not?
• RQ6) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit higher performance in col-
laborative programming exercises than students who did not?
• RQ7) Do students who used the RESPE demonstrate higher regulatory skills
development than students who did not?
The subsequent sections detail the experimental design employed to address the
aforementioned inquiries. The findings are presented in alignment with the se-
84

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.22: design of experiment.
quence of the research questions and were documented in the publication [Silva
et al., 2023c].
4.3.1 Methodology
This section details the design of the experiment. This procedure includes an
explanation of the data collection and analysis procedures, the methodology for
determining the sample size, and the sample characteristics.
Design of the experiment
Figure 4.22 presents the experimental design.
Based on the understanding that regulation of learning is crucial for program-
ming learners and that it can be stimulated [Garcia et al., 2018a], four hypotheses
were raised: H01: Students’ course grades in the experimental groups would outper-
form the students in the control group ; H02: The performance in collaborative activities
of students who received social regulation support will outperform the students who did
not receive this support; H03: Students’ MSLQ scores in the experimental groups would
outperform the students in the control group , and H04: Students’ SPSQ scores in the
experimental groups would outperform the students in the control group.
The independent variable chosen in this research was the learning platform used

by students, comprised of three levels. The ExpSRL and ExpSRLSocialReg had
access to the RESPE’s scaffolding but in different formats. ExpSRL had access
only to the RESPE’s scaffoldings focused on self-regulation (see Section 4.1.3),
whereas ExpSRLSocialReg had access to both the self-regulatory and social reg-
ulatory scaffoldings. This distinction was necessary as it allowed observation of
whether the use of social scaffoldings had any influence on individual and collab-
orative learning. Finally, a control group was also present, and they used a RE-
SPE modification without any regulatory support and solely included the course
materials and a programming editor comprised of a code editor and Python in-
terpreter. Participants within the control group were exposed to standard Python
interpreter messages, resembling the experience of a conventional programming
editor. Detailed information regarding the scaffolding availability to the experi-
mental conditions is presented in Table 4.5.
Four dependent variables were assessed in this study:
• Students’ final course grades.
• The number of correct collaborative exercises.
• Scores obtained from the Motivated Strategies for Learning Questionnaire
(MSLQ).
• Scores obtained from the Self-regulation of Learning Programming Strate-
gies Questionnaire (SPSQ)
Course grades are a common metric used in research to assess programming
learning [Medeiros et al., 2019]. The Course grade varied from 0 to 100 and was
86

calculated based on the correctness of the exercise’s test cases answered by the
students. Completing all test cases in one exercise scores one point, which was
reduced in incomplete test cases. For example, if the exercise had four test cases
and the students’ code correctly achieved three correct results, the score would be
0.75. Therefore, the final grade calculation takes into account the computation of
the sum of these scores for each exercise as expressed by the following formula:

The assessment of collaborative learning involved a binary classification of group
responses to collaborative exercises. A group’s response was classified as "cor-
rect" if their code produced the expected output for all of the exercise’s test cases.
If not, the response was classified as "incorrect."
The regulation of learning abilities was assessed using two distinct instruments:
the MSLQ and the SPSQ. The MSLQ is designed to evaluate general-purpose
regulatory skills, while the SPSQ targets programming-specific regulatory strate-
gies. Employing both instruments facilitates an in-depth investigation of how
programming learning correlates with both specific and general regulatory skills.
Notably, there is a debate regarding the MSLQ’s applicability in the program-
ming context, and this thesis seeks to provide additional insights into this issue,
contributing valuable data to the ongoing discussion [Silva et al., 2021a].
Confounding variables with recognized potential influence on the learning pro-
cess were controlled. As documented in Ewert and Sibthorp [2009], these vari-
ables encompassed course duration, instructor, and learning material, and in this
study were standardized across all groups, ensuring uniformity.
Bias acknowledgment and mitigation
This section acknowledges and addresses potential biases that could impact the
experiment’s results. This study adheres to a single-blind experimental design,
where participants are unaware of their assigned group, thereby mitigating the
risk of participant bias. This approach is vital to minimize the potential for partic-
ipants to alter their behavior based on the treatment they are aware of receiving
[Gluud, 2006].
87

Chapter 4
The course professor, however, knew the group allocations, which could have
introduced researcher bias. This refers to the researcher’s unconscious favoritism
toward a particular treatment group. While a double-blind experiment is optimal
for mitigating participant and researcher bias, the educational research context,
characterized by the interaction between teachers and students, often impedes
this approach [Torgerson and Torgerson, 2003]. To reduce bias, the researcher es-
tablished consistent procedures across all experimental conditions. This included
maintaining uniform durations for synchronous meetings and ensuring equitable
interaction with students by adhering to a consistent weekly routine applicable to
all groups. These measures were designed to ensure that all participants received
a similar level of engagement and support throughout the study.
Another bias typical in experimental research is attrition bias, which stems from
participant dropouts. To mitigate this bias, students received regular encourage-
ment emails to foster participation in the course. Additionally, it is important to
investigate the underlying reasons for students’ dropouts, which can reveal un-
desired conditions that might affect the experiments’ results. This procedure was
performed.
Pearson’s chi-square test was conducted to assess whether there was an associa-
tion between the frequency of students’ dropouts and the experimental condition
to which they were assigned. This analysis aimed to determine if dropouts were
disproportionately higher in particular experimental groups. The results indi-
cated no statistically significant difference (p = .64). Consequently, the outcomes
suggest that dropouts were not influenced by the treatment students received.
Those who left were contacted via email, and an anonymous questionnaire was
administered featuring an open-ended question about the reasons for dropping
out ("Please specify the causes that led you to drop out of the course.") and six
items gauging various factors that may have influenced their decision, answered
with a Likert scale from "I completely disagree" to "I completely agree".
The exploratory analysis of the responses to the open-ended question revealed
four primary themes: time constraints (65%), technological difficulties (20%),
programming challenges (10%), and dissatisfaction with the virtual class format
(5%). These are common issues in online courses [Kebritchi et al., 2017].
Responses to the Likert items are presented in Figure 4.23, highlighting that most
dropouts were unrelated to the experiment.
Finally, performance bias arises from uneven conditions provided to participants.
This bias can be reduced through the identification and control of potentially con-
founding variables that could impact learners’ performance. For example, ex-
posure to different learning materials, course duration variations, and different
course professors could influence learner’s achievement. Therefore, these factors
were controlled and standardized across all treatment groups.
88

RESPE development and evaluation
Figure 4.23: Likert responses on the dropout questionnaire.
Data collection and analysis
RESPE’s effectiveness was evaluated through a combination of students’ pro-
gramming performance and their scores on regulatory tests. Programming per-
formance was represented by an individual score measured by the student’s final
course grade and a group score measured by the number of correct answers to
collaborative exercises.
To test the hypothesis H01: Students’ course grades in the experimental groups would
outperform the students in the control group, the Analysis of Covariance (ANCOVA)
represents a suitable choice [Field, 2013]. ANCOVA was selected as the statisti-
cal test due to the experimental design’s independent variable with three levels
(ExpSRL, ExpSRLSocialReg, and C.G.), facilitating a comparison of course grades
among these groups. However, it was necessary to mitigate this variable’s impact
to account for the potential influence of students’ prior programming knowledge
on learning achievements [Campbell et al., 2016].
ANCOVA integrates the concept of a covariate to control the influence of an ex-
traneous variable on the outcome of interest. In this study, the covariate was stu-
dents’ initial programming knowledge scores, measured by a programming test
administered at the beginning of the courses’ to assess their pre-existing Python
proficiency (see Appendix D).
Pearson’s Chi-Squared test was used to test hypothesis H02: The performance in
collaborative activities of students who received social regulation support will outper-
form the students who did not receive this support. The collaborative performance
metric for each experimental condition was determined by the number of cor-
rect and incorrect group responses in collaborative exercises. The Chi-Squared
89

Chapter 4
test compared the distribution of correct and incorrect responses across different
experimental conditions to determine if there was a significant difference in per-
formance outcomes. The objective was to identify if the experimental condition
(receiving social regulation support or not) had a statistically significant impact
on whether the groups achieved correct solutions in their collaborative exercises.
Assessment of SRL development using MSLQ often involves a pre-test and post-
test design [Loksa et al., 2022], which was adopted in this study. Only a post-test
design was used for SPSQ scores because this instrument measures programming-
specific abilities, and many students did not have previous programming experi-
ence.
Hypothesis H03: Students’ MSLQ scores in the experimental groups would outperform
the students in the control group was tested using ANCOVA. The pre-test scores
were used as a covariate to assess differences in the post-test across students in
different experimental conditions.
Finally, the comparison of students’ SPSQ scores across the experimental con-
ditions was assessed through hypothesis H04: Students’ SPSQ scores in the ex-
perimental groups would outperform the students in the control group . It was hy-
pothesized that providing regulatory scaffolding would foster the development
of programming-specific regulatory abilities. This hypothesis was tested using
an ANCOVA analysis with the MSLQ post-test as a covariate because the SPSQ
scores had some degree of correlation with MSLQ scores (Section 5.3), suggest-
ing that general regulatory abilities could influence programming-specific regu-
lation. Thus, establishing MSLQ post-test scores as covariate helps mitigate the
influence of this variable on this analysis.
A priori power analysis
The sample size for the experiment was determined using power analysis [Kang,
2021]. This statistical method helps determine the number of participants needed
to achieve a desired level of statistical power, which refers to the likelihood of
detecting an effect within the investigated sample [Lakens, 2013]. The effect size
quantifies the magnitude of the relationship between an effect in a study, pro-
viding a meaningful interpretation of the impact of an intervention [Cohn and
Becker, 2003].
One of the methodologies for determining sample size uses the average effect
size derived from previous studies situated within a comparable context [Kang,
2021]. A summary of studies that promoted interventions utilizing regulatory
scaffoldings to support programming learning was performed to calculate the
average effect size from these studies. These studies come from three systematic
literature reviews [Garcia et al., 2018a; Loksa et al., 2022; Silva et al., 2021b]. Effect
sizes from these studies were incorporated in the analysis and, if not explicitly
provided, were calculated based on reported statistics.
The power analysis was calculated using the Gpower software [Kang, 2021], tar-
geting a statistical power of 95%, a widely accepted level in statistical analysis

minimum sample size requirement of 41 students.
Sample
The sample consisted of students enrolled in three online programming courses.
These courses were conducted with a one-month interval between them and were
held during the COVID-19 pandemic. The same health restrictions applied in
Brazil were the same during the execution period of these three courses.
One hundred and twenty-three students enrolled in the courses, and fifty-two
dropped out (Table 4.6). High dropout rates are a prevalent challenge in online
courses, and the observed rate aligns with those reported in other online course
settings [Bawa, 2016]. As discussed in Section 4.3.1, the investigation with stu-
dents who dropped out of the experiment indicates that this was not related to
the study and suggests various external factors as the causes.
Table 4.6 presents the baseline characteristics of all participants, including those
who dropped out. The final column ("remaining participants") delineates the
dataset of students involved in the experiment (n=71).
Before initiating the first course, the order for the experimental condition for each
course was randomly chosen. All students within a given course were subjected
to the same experimental condition. At the beginning of the course, students were
given information about how their data would be collected. They were also asked
to sign a consent form before participating in the study. There was no financial
incentive to participate.
A sequence of statistical analyses was conducted to ascertain potential differences
among the sample’s characteristics within the various experimental conditions.
A Pearson’s chi-square test was executed to investigate gender frequency dif-
ferences, yielding a non-significant result ( p = .12). To evaluate the medians of
participants’ ages, a Kruskal-Wallis test was employed. Subsequently, a post hoc
analysis employing the Dunn test was conducted, with the p-values adjusted us-
ing the Holm-Bonferroni method. The sole statistical distinction observed was
that the median age of students in the ExpSRL group differed from that of stu-
dents in the ExpSRLSocialReg group.
