Regarding students’ prior programming scores (test available in Appendix D),
the average score was 1.31 out of 10, with a standard deviation of 2.41. This
average score reflects a limited or negligible familiarity with the programming
language. Employing a Kruskal-Wallis test, differences in medians were inves-
tigated across the three experimental groups (ExpSRL, ExpSRLSocialReg, and
C.G.), yielding a non-significant result ( p = .63). Therefore, no statistical differ-
ences exist among the groups concerning previous Python knowledge.
4.3.2 RQ5) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit higher per-
formance in individual programming exercises than stu-
dents who did not?
Prior research has contributed to the understanding that regulatory scaffolding
is a strategy that can potentially support programming learning [Prather et al.,
2020]. However, some limitations exist, mainly due to the scarcity of research on
this subject. Consequently, few types of scaffoldings were investigated, which
limits understanding of the possibilities for resources based on self-regulation
theory that can be utilized. Furthermore, current literature studies focus on sup-
porting general-use regulatory processes, with few works offering support for the
code creation process [Silva et al., 2021b], a crucial stage in programming learning
where many students struggle [Qian and Lehman, 2017]. This doctorate sought
to enhance the available evidence regarding the feasibility of using regulatory
scaffolding to support programming learning.
The RESPE’s effectiveness in fostering programming learning was assessed in
this research question. The following null hypothesis was tested: H01: Students’
course grades in the experimental groups would not outperform the students in the con-
trol group.
Table 4.7 presents students’ course grades’ mean and standard deviation across
the experimental conditions. The ExpSRL group achieved the highest grades,
followed by the ExpSRLSocialReg group (with a difference of -6.17). The control
group exhibited the lowest grades, with a difference of -30.25 and -24.08, from
the ExpSRL and ExpSRLSocialReg, respectively. Notably, a higher standard de-
viation was observed across all experimental conditions, reflecting that students
possess significant variations in their learning achievements, a characteristic com-
monly encountered in programming courses [Robins, 2019].
A one-way ANCOVA was conducted to compare the course grade means from
the three experimental conditions. In this approach, students’ prior programming
knowledge scores were used as a covariate due to their potential to influence the
outcome. The homogeneity of variance was evaluated using Levene’s test (F =
92

RESPE development and evaluation
0.11, p = .89).
A statistical difference in mean course grades was identified [F(2, 68) = 5.607, p
= .006], leading to the rejection of null hypothesis H 01 (α = .05). In this sense,
students’ means differed across the experimental conditions. A post-hoc analy-
sis was performed to identify in which groups these differences had statistical
significance.
Tukey’s HSD posthoc analysis unveiled statistically significant differences be-
tween ExpSRLSocialReg and C.G. (p = .02), as well as between ExpSRL and C.G.
(p = .008). No statistically significant differences were observed between Exp-
SRL and ExpSRLSocialReg. This result points out that students who received the
regulatory scaffolding achieved superior statistically significant grades than the
control group without support.
The effect size was calculated using Partial eta-squared and computed with the
R package named effectsize. The effect size was 0.18 [95% C.I.:[0.01, 0.34]]. In this
context, the Partial eta-squared indicates the proportion of total variance in the
course grade that is explained by the usage of regulatory scaffolding, when other
variables are controlled. Thus, the findings indicate that approximately 18% of
the variation in student performance can be attributed to regulatory scaffolding.
This effect size can be interpreted as a meaningful impact in the context of ed-
ucation [Richardson, 2011]. This result indicates that irrespective of the type of
regulatory support provided (SRL alone or combined with social regulation), stu-
dents exposed to these interventions performed better than the control group.
Main findings
The results of this research question support the suitability of the designed scaf-
folding in support programming learning. Moreover, the findings are consistent
with previous research and corroborate the feasibility of using regulatory scaf-
folding in this learning domain [Silva et al., 2021b]. This knowledge can help
researchers and educators in their evaluation of whether or not to use regulatory
scaffoldings.
The inclusion of social regulatory support does not appear to enhance learning
outcomes in individual tasks. In such contexts, only the assistance provided by
regulatory scaffoldings, designed to foster individual self-regulation, was consid-
ered sufficient. However, the following section will delve into the effectiveness
of social regulatory support within collaborative task environments, illustrating
its significant contribution to the performance in these settings.
4.3.3 RQ6) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit higher per-
formance in collaborative programming exercises than stu-
dents who did not?
This research question delves into a topic that is even less explored regarding the
understanding of how the support for social regulation contributes to program-
93

Chapter 4
C.G. ExpSRL ExpSRLSocialReg
Correct 10 7 53
Incorrect 12 10 21
Table 4.8: Contingency table representing the number of correct and incorrect
groups’ responses to the collaborative exercises.
ming learning [Silva et al., 2021b]. This issue hinders the knowledge of how this
support can be designed and the eventual advantages of their use. This issue lim-
its the understanding of how scaffoldings aimed at supporting social regulation
can be designed and what are their contributions to learning.
The initial evidence discovered in RQ5 suggests that social regulatory support
does not contribute to advancements in individual learning activities. However,
it is necessary to investigate how this support facilitates collaborative learning, an
aspect that this research question endeavors to investigate. To achieve this objec-
tive, the null hypothesis was tested: H02: The performance in collaborative activities
of students who received social regulation support will not outperform the students who
did not receive this support . The ExpSRLSocialReg group was the only condition
that had access to social regulatory support.
Collaborative performance was measured for each experimental condition using
the groups’ responses to the collaborative exercises. These responses were classi-
fied as correct or incorrect based on the code output correctness according to the
exercises’ test cases.
The count of correct and incorrect responses was calculated for each experimen-
tal condition and is presented in Table 4.8. The differences in the total number
of responses among the experimental conditions can be partly attributed to the
different number of participants in the groups (Table 4.6). Besides this factor, the
larger quantity of responses from the ExpSRLSocialReg group might also sug-
gest that this group was more interested in this type of activity, considering that
participation was voluntary and did not affect the final course grade. While this
could be one of the benefits of social regulation support [Hadwin and Oshige,
2011], the current data limit more in-depth conclusions about this and require
further investigation.
Data in Table 4.8 points out that 71.6% of the responses of the students in the
ExpSRLSocialReg group were correct, compared to 41.1% of ExpSRL and 45.4%
of the control group. The Chi-square test was employed to determine if there
was a significant association between the students’ experimental conditions they
belonged and the response classifications. This data enables an understanding
of whether performance in collaborative activities varies among the groups. The
Chi-squared test is robust and can be applied when the counts being compared
are of different sizes [Weaver et al., 2017], which is the case of this doctorate.
The results showed significant evidence of an association ( χ2 = 8.59, p < .01),
indicating that the correctness of the collaborative task was associated with the
groups’ experimental condition. As there are three conditions, it is necessary
to identify in which pairs there are statistically significant differences. A post
94

RESPE development and evaluation
hoc analysis was performed to identify these differences, using the residuals of
the Chi-Square test with the p-value adjusted using the Bonferroni correction, a
standard procedure to be performed in this situation [Sharpe, 2019]. The post
hoc analysis showed a statistically significant difference ( α = .05) between the
ExpSRLSocialReg and ExpSRL and between the ExpSRLSocialReg and C.G. No
statistically significant difference between the C.G. and ExpSRL groups was iden-
tified.
The null hypothesis H 02 was refuted, signifying that the performance of the Ex-
pSRLSocialReg group diverged from the other groups. A Cramer’s V effect size
of 0.36 was discerned (95% C.I. [0.1, 0.58]), denoting a medium-level effect [Sun
et al., 2010]. The results showed that students who received social regulatory
support outperformed both the control group and those who only received indi-
vidual regulatory support.
Main findings
This finding suggests that using scaffoldings to structure collaborative work can
positively affect learning outcomes in collaborative programming tasks. This
finding supports the hypothesis that social regulatory support is appropriate for
collaborative work [Järvelä et al., 2016], and sheds light on the feasibility of using
this support in programming education.
The observation that the ExpSRL group’s performance, who received individ-
ual regulatory scaffolding support, did not significantly differ from the control
group’s performance led to hypotheses: i) scaffoldings designed exclusively for
SRL may not provide sufficient support for collaborative tasks, indicating that
group-based activities has specific demands; ii) students might face challenges in
translating SRL-focused skills to collaborative settings, suggesting a gap in the
transferability of these skills. These considerations open up new avenues for fu-
ture research, emphasizing the need to examine the adaptability and efficacy of
SRL-only scaffoldings in collaborative contexts.
It was expected that the gains of the ExpSRL group would, at least partially, trans-
fer to collaborative tasks. This findings raise questions about students’ internal-
ization of the benefits provided by the scaffoldings. It might be related to the fact
that collaborative tasks has specificities, but could also point out to the students’
limitations in regulating themselves in these tasks [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011].
This result also raises a question about the ability of students in the ExpSRLSo-
cialReg group to transfer their gains to collaborative tasks when the supporting
scaffoldings are removed.
It is necessary to investigate how the internalization mechanisms of the regula-
tory process mediated by scaffoldings occur. As indicated by [Breitwieser et al.,
2022], it is conceivable that extended exposure to regulatory interventions might
be essential for the effects to endure over time. A prospective avenue for future
research is longitudinal research to assess students’ long-term programming per-
formance after being exposed to regulatory support.

4.3.4 RQ7) Do students who used the RESPE demonstrate higher
regulatory skills development than students who did not?
In addition to fostering learning achievements, regulatory scaffolding can help
students develop their self-regulation skills [Shao et al., 2023]. This research ques-
tion sought to identify modifications in SRL aptitudes after participating in the
experiment.
Regulatory skills were measured using two different questionnaires: the Moti-
vational Strategies for Learning Questionnaire (MSLQ) and the Self-regulation
of Learning Programming Strategies Questionnaire (SPSQ). Two null hypotheses
were established: H03: Students’ MSLQ scores in the experimental groups would not
outperform the students in the control group , and H04: Students’ SPSQ scores in the
experimental groups would not outperform the students in the control group.
A pre-post-test design was employed for MSLQ, while SPSQ was solely mea-
sured through a post-test. As MSLQ focuses on general-purpose regulatory abil-
ities, these stem from students’ previous experiences, including those outside the
computing education context. On the other hand, SPSQ’s items refer to strate-
gies specific to programming education, and due to the numerous students lack-
ing prior programming knowledge, their responses to the instrument could have
been biased. Hence, it was decided to administer the SPSQ exclusively after the
course.
The study utilized a pre-post-test design for the MSLQ and administered the
SPSQ only as a post-test. The rationale behind this approach is that the MSLQ
assesses general-purpose regulatory skills that students develop through their
lives, influenced by a variety of experiences in multiple educational contexts. In
contrast, the SPSQ is tailored to assess strategies specific to programming edu-
cation, and the test that measured prior programming experience revealed that,
on average, students had lower programming proficiency. Thus, a SPSQ pre-test
could lead to random and biased responses.
Students answered the MSLQ and SPSQ using a five-Likert scale. To analyze
these responses, an average score was computed for both questionnaires based
on the Likert scale ratings, aligning with methodologies from prior research that
aimed to quantify SRL skills [Loksa et al., 2022; Silva et al., 2023d]. The mean
and standard deviation calculated from the imputed data for the MSLQ pre and
post-tests are displayed in Table 4.9, while the data for SPSQ is presented in Table
4.10.
Due to some students’ absences during the final synchronous class when the in-
96

RESPE development and evaluation
SPSQ mean (sd)
ExpSRL 4.06 (0.37)
ExpSRLSocialReg 4.23 (0.38)
Control group 4.07 (0.45)
Table 4.10: Students’ SPSQ mean scores and standard deviation grouped by ex-
perimental conditions.
struments were administered, missing data was detected in the MSLQ (30.9%)
and SPSQ (36.9%) post-tests. Various strategies exist for handling missing data,
with listwise deletion historically being one of the most used approaches [Kang,
2013]. In this approach, missing records are excluded from the analysis, but this
has been criticized by statisticians due to the potential bias that this removal can
induce [Manly and Wells, 2014]. An alternative method is multiple imputation,
which has garnered attention as a more suitable solution [Pampaka et al., 2014].
Multiple imputation addresses missing values by generating estimates based on
the distribution and relationships among variables in the dataset [Li et al., 2015].
The mice12 package from R was used in this doctorate to perform the multiple
imputation. To achieve a statistical power of 90% or higher, as recommended by
[Bodner, 2008], 258 imputations were generated, which means that 258 distinct
datasets were created. The default parameters of the mice package were utilized.
Statistical computations based on the data generated through multiple imputa-
tions utilize a pooling technique, wherein calculations are conducted for each
imputed dataset and subsequently merged. This step is essential because ev-
ery dataset produced by the multiple imputation procedure introduces a certain
degree of uncertainty, potentially leading to errors in subsequent statistical anal-
yses [Pampaka et al., 2014]. To address this concern, imputed data are analyzed
individually and aggregated using pooling methods. The mice package employs
Rubin’s rules, which estimate a desired parameter (such as a regression coeffi-
cient) by taking into account the variance between and within imputations [van
Buuren, 2018].
To assess differences in the aptitudes measured by the MSLQ and SPSQ, two
ANCOVA analyses were performed. In this context, the overall MSLQ pre-test
score was used as a covariate for both analyses. In the MSLQ, there is a connection
between the skills assessed before and after the intervention [Roth et al., 2015].
For the SPSQ, evidence in the literature suggests that general regulatory skills can
affect the ability to use programming-specific regulatory strategies [Falkner et al.,
2015]. Therefore, in both cases, it was assumed that a student’s initial regulatory
capacity can influence their outcomes on both the MSLQ post-test and the SPSQ.
This is the rationale for selecting the MSLQ pre-test score as a covariate.
The assumptions for the ANCOVA analysis for the MSLQ as dependent variable
were satisfied, with independence between covariate and treatment ( F = 0.51, p
= .60) and homogeneous variances across samples as measured by Bartlett’s test
(K = 1.45, p = .51). No statistically significant improvements in post-test scores
12https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/mice/index.html
97

Chapter 4
among the experimental conditions were observed ( F = 0.58, p = .55). Conse-
quently, the null hypothesis H03 could not be rejected. This result suggests that
the means of MSLQ scores across the three groups are not statistically signifi-
cantly different and that the RESPE usage seems to not have influenced their
development.
Table 4.10 indicates that all three groups slightly decreased their MSLQ scores,
which is a phenomenon that lacks a singular explanation. Due to the small differ-
ences and the lack of statistically significant variation, it can be assumed that there
were no changes in skills before and after the intervention. Other factors also help
to understand this result, such as the possibility that exposure to a pre-test could
change a student’s behavior in a post-test [Roediger and Karpicke, 2006]. The
initial exposure might have triggered metacognitive awareness, prompting stu-
dents to be more mindful of their learning processes, engage in self-reflection,
and offer more cautious responses in the post-test [Boekaerts and Corno, 2005].
Nonetheless, further research can further explore this observation.
The same statistical procedure was applied to evaluate the effectiveness of RESPE
in fostering regulatory skills measured by the SPSQ instrument. The samples
exhibited homogeneous variances, as indicated by Bartlett’s test (K = 1.79, p = .45).
Despite students in the ExpSRLSocialReg group achieving higher SPSQ scores
(Table 4.10), no statistically significant differences in means among experimental
conditions were evident (F = 1.38, p = .25). Consequently, the null hypothesis H04
could also not be rejected, indicating that the groups presented similar scores.
Main findings
The underlying mechanisms driving the development of regulatory skills follow-
ing interventions remain a not fully understood process [Silva et al., 2021b]. A lit-
erature review by [Heikkinen et al., 2022] found that only 7.1% of identified stud-
ies reported positive effects on SRL development, but on the other hand, most of
them had successfully supported learning gains. A similar trend emerged in the
previous literature review reported in Section 4.1.1, where interventions mostly
supported students’ programming learning but not the development of regula-
tory abilities [Silva et al., 2021b].
The findings suggest the need for further research to comprehensively compre-
hend the factors influencing the development of regulatory skills through inter-
ventions. In this sense, a meta-analysis can be performed, comprised of modera-
tor analysis to provide insights into the key factors that contribute significantly to
the development of SRL skills. Based on the previous literature, suggestions for
moderators include exposure duration and frequency, psychological processes
(e.g., cognition, metacognition), and scaffoldings used [Duckworth et al., 2009].
98

Chapter 5
Complementary analysis
The integration of self-regulation theory as an investigative field within program-
ming education research involves a broad set of research areas. One vital research
field on this theme explores the use of stimuli for regulatory scaffolding [Schunk
and Zimmerman, 2023], as presented in Section 4. Other research areas assist in
building an understanding of how self-regulation occurs in programming and its
influence on learning. This doctoral work also aimed to make contributions along
these lines, which were guided by the following research questions:
• RQ8) What is the association between SRL and programming learning?
• RQ9) What regulatory strategies are used by programming students?
• RQ10) What is the psychometric validity of self-regulation measurement
instruments in the context of programming education?
5.1 RQ8) What is the association between SRL and
programming learning?
Regulatory skills are hypothesized to correlate with learning performance across
multiple educational domains [Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. However, there
is limited statistical evidence to support this in the context of programming edu-
cation, and additional data is necessary [Silva et al., 2021a]. This research ques-
tion sought to contribute to this respect by providing empirical evidence on the
relationship between students’ SRL aptitudes and their programming learning
outcomes.
The methodology adopted to achieve the objective proposed in this research ques-
tion involved three different approaches: a correlational analysis, systematic liter-
ature review, and meta-analysis. The correlational studies and the systematic re-
view focused on self-report data collected from students, while the meta-analysis
was aimed at summarizing data from experiments, where students received some
form of regulatory support (e.g., the scaffoldings proposed in this study), and it
99

Chapter 5
was assessed whether this type of support assisted in learning. These two formats
complement each other by providing knowledge on how students’ own regula-
tory abilities are associated with learning, and at the same time, on the gains that
can be obtained from stimulating regulatory processes.
Correlational analysis
Correlational analyses are widely employed to explore the relationship between
SRL skills and educational outcomes [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. In this
doctoral research, two correlational studies were undertaken: one analyzed data
from an experimental cohort of students, while the other used data collected in
a multinational investigation. These investigations were conducted within two
contrasting educational settings—the experiment took place in a more informal
learning environment, whereas the multinational study was situated within a for-
mal higher education context. This methodological approach enabled a compre-
hensive examination of how SRL competencies correlate with learning achieve-
ments across diverse educational landscapes.
Students who participated in the RESPE experiment completed the MSLQ and
SPSQ questionnaires, providing data on their SRL competencies. As outlined in
Section 4.3.1, the MSLQ was administered in both pre-test and post-test phases to
assess changes over time, while the SPSQ was only administered at the course’s
conclusion.
Table 5.1 presents the Pearson correlations between the students’ course grades
and their MSLQ scores, detailed in both pre-test and post-test phases, alongside
the scores from the questionnaire’s various scales. Similarly, Table 5.2 details the
correlations between course grades and SPSQ scores, with the latter also broken
down by its specific scales.
The findings indicate a negligible correlation between the MSLQ and SPSQ scores
with programming learning outcomes. In this context, the regulatory abilities
captured by these instruments appear to have minimal impact on course grades.
This finding requires a complex analysis and at a first look might suggest that reg-
ulation of learning abilities did not associate with programming learning. How-
ever, there are some factors that should be pondered when interpreting these
results.
The reliability of these results depends on the psychometric validity of the MSLQ
and SPSQ instruments. The MSLQ has an established history of usage and a
substantial body of research supporting its validity across multiple educational
contexts [Panadero, 2017]. However, some issues concerning the relationship be-
tween MSLQ scores and programming performance can be observed in the previ-
ous literature revealing conflicting evidence [Silva et al., 2023d]. The lack of psy-
chometric validation of the MSLQ in the programming education context hinders
a better understanding of the limitations of this instrument is currently lacking
[Silva et al., 2021b].
The psychometric properties of SPSQ are less documented and only their authors

Complementary analysis
have partially reported validation data [Silva et al., 2021a]. Only internal valid-
ity findings are available, showing positive but preliminary outcomes [Lu et al.,
2017]. However, a detailed exploratory factor analysis has not been conducted,
which is essential for a robust understanding of the instrument’s constructs. This
gap in the data should be carefully considered when interpreting the results de-
rived from SPSQ. To address this gap, this doctoral research has included a psy-
chometric evaluation of the instrument, detailed further in Section 5.3.
The complexity of programming learning, encompassing a range of psycholog-
ical processes, might elucidate the observed weak correlation [Robins, 2019]. In
the realm of programming, learners engage in cognitive processes to determine
strategies for constructing code. Concurrently, metacognitive processes enable
them to review their learning progress and implement adjustments to enhance
their understanding. Moreover, motivational factors play a crucial role in sus-
taining diligent study habits and resilience in overcoming challenges [Volet and
Lund, 1994]. These processes, with their varying impacts, contribute to the mul-
tifaceted nature of programming education. Consequently, the strategies mea-
sured by the instruments might not be the most suitable to represent this com-
plexity.
Another aspect this study aims to bring into the discussion is an analysis of
the performance of these instruments in different educational contexts. To this
end, an exploratory study was conducted with students enrolled in computing
courses. This research comprised ninety-one Macanese students enrolled in a
four-year bachelor’s degree in computing and twelve Brazilian students enrolled
in a three-year associate degree program. Both groups of students were enrolled
in introductory programming courses with comparable syllabus but using differ-
ent programming languages. Brazilian students were taught using Python and
Java for over 80 hours, while Macanese students were instructed in Java for 45
hours in one semester.
The Macanese student sample consisted of 86.8% males, 7.6% females, and 5.6%
who preferred not to disclose their gender. They had an average age of 20.37 years
with a standard deviation of 3.46. The Brazilian student sample was composed
of 75% males and 25% females, with an average age of 21.58 years and a standard
deviation of 9.11.
The students responded to the MSLQ and SPSQ questionnaires with a seven and
five point Likert scale, respectivelly. Macanese students completed the instru-
ments in English, the language of instruction for their courses, while Brazilian
students answered a Portuguese version identical to the one employed in the RE-
SPE evaluation (Appendix B). The detailed findings from this multinational study
are described in [Silva et al., 2021a].
The comparison between Brazilian and Macanese students revealed that Brazil-
ians scored higher on both the MSLQ and SPSQ instruments, with these differ-
ences being statistically significant (MSLQ: t = 2.6854, p <0.01; SPSQ: t = 3.2833,
p <0.001). Variability in regulatory skills across students from different countries
has been reported in the previous literature. The study performed by [Ambro-
sio et al., 2012], reported a similar finding comparing the regulatory aptitudes of
103

Chapter 5

Table 5.3: Descriptive statistics for MSLQ, SPSQ, and course grade.
Brazilian and Portuguese students. Such variations highlight the intricate nature
of self-regulated learning, which seems to be influenced by multiple factors, in-
cluding those related to educational institutions and cultural backgrounds [Tong
et al., 2019].
Before drawing conclusions from these results, it’s important to acknowledge po-
tential biases. These include the smaller sample size for Brazilian students and
the translation of the instruments into Portuguese. Regarding this latter issue, the
translated instruments with this sample demonstrated good internal consistency,
with Cronbach’s alpha values of 0.78 for MSLQ and 0.85 for SPSQ, which are con-
sistent with findings in existing literature, indicating that the translated versions
maintained their internal reliability [Lu et al., 2017; Saks et al., 2015].
Contrary to the findings from the data collected from students in the experimen-
tal study, the results point to a moderate correlation between MSLQ and SPSQ
scores with programming learning. The differences between the two studies can
help understand this result, such as the differences in pedagogical context and
demographic data. Therefore, one possibility is that these instruments may be
better suited to certain educational contexts, such as higher education, than oth-
ers.
Exploratory research like this points more towards questions than answers. Two
important aspects warrant future investigation: one is the need for further psy-
chometric validations of both instruments, particularly their appropriateness in
various educational contexts of programming education. There is also an oppor-
tunity to understand how cultural and pedagogical differences across countries
can influence the regulatory skills of programming students.
Systematic literature review
Another step to enhance the understanding of the relationship between SRL and
performance in programming was the summary of the existing state of the art
related to this topic. To accomplish this objective, a systematic literature review
was conducted, and the findings were published in [Silva et al., 2021a].
The literature review followed Kitchenham’s methodology [Kitchenham et al.,
2009], widely adopted in computer science research. Searches were conducted in
four digital libraries: ACM, IEEE, Science Direct, and Eric. Studies were included
if they met the following criteria: (i) they focused on introductory programming,
(ii) they were based on SRL theory, and (iii) they measured both SRL and metrics

related to programming learning. Out of 36 studies that were thoroughly read,
six met the inclusion criteria.
Table 5.4 displays the studies identified in the literature review, predominantly
situated within higher education settings. This focus aids in enriching the knowl-
edge base for this specific educational context. However, it also underscores a
significant gap, as other learning environments remain underexplored in exist-
ing research. The findings discussed in the preceding section, derived from stu-
dent data in the experimental study, offers a contribution from this doctoral work
in enhancing our comprehension of how self-regulation correlates with learning
across a variety of pedagogical settings.
The literature review revealed a widespread adoption of the MSLQ to assess
self-regulated learning abilities. However, there is a noticeable lack of essential
psychometric validation information, such as Cronbach’s alpha, in these studies.
Consequently, this gap underscores the critical need for comprehensive psycho-
metric evaluations of the MSLQ specifically within the context of programming
education, emphasizing the necessity to establish its reliability and validity in this
educational domain.
The identified research focused on understanding the relationship between var-
ious regulatory processes and their impact on learning outcomes in program-
ming. These processes refer to motivational, metacognitive, behavioral, and cog-
nitive processes, and were represented through diverse psychological constructs
105

Chapter 5
measured by the instruments, including self-efficacy, satisfaction levels, intrin-
sic motivation, and various strategic approaches like self-regulation and resource
management strategies.
Correlational analysis was the primary statistical approach used by these studies,
followed by regression models and Structural Equation Modeling (SEM). Regres-
sion analyses and SEM aimed to identify the constructs that accounted for higher
variations in students’ grades and to construct predictive models of their perfor-
mance.
Table 5.5 lists the evaluated outcomes (e.g., course grade) and their correlation
with constructs from the instruments used by the studies, representing regulatory
resources of different psychological nature. Acronyms used in the table: N.S.
= Non-significant; S.E. = self-efficacy; I.M. = Intrinsic motivation; G.C. = Goal
commitment; S.S. = Self-satisfaction; T.A. = Test-anxiety; SRL = Self-regulation;
C.S. = Cognitive strategies; R.S. = Resource strategy; I.T. = Interactive in the online
learning environment; P .S. = Perceived satisfaction; P .U. = Perceived usefulness;
LSR = Learner Self-regulation; R.V . = Regulation of value; R.P . = Regulation of
performance goals; S.C. = Self-consequating; E.S. = Environmental structuring;
RSI = Regulation of situational interest; R.M. = Regulation of mastery goals.
MSLQ constructs varied in their association with course performance. For in-
stance, self-efficacy had most reports on its association with programming learn-
ing performance [Cigdem, 2015; Kuo et al., 2013; Lishinski et al., 2016]. This re-
sult corroborates earlier studies on the relevance of this construct in the domain
of programming learning [Tsai, 2019]. From a practical point of view, this finding
highlights the need to support students in developing their own belief in their
abilities through their programming journey.
Self-regulation was also a construct with a predictive influence on course grades.
This result is consistent with prior research indicating that students’ metacogni-
tive abilities influence their capabilities to learn how to program [Çakiro˘ glu and
Er, 2020]. Thus, this finding highlights the importance of providing metacogni-
tive support to enhance students’ learning outcomes [Volet and Lund, 1994; Wang
et al., 2023].
Other motivational constructs, such as intrinsic value, did not correlate with
programming learning outcomes, despite their commonly acknowledged link
with educational achievement [Gomes et al., 2018]. This unexpected result might
stem from motivation’s complex nature [Alexander et al., 2011; Poitras and La-
joie, 2013]. Such a finding underscores the need for additional research to delve
deeper into the motivational elements that impact programming learning,
The lack of correlation between cognitive strategies and course performance was
also another surprising finding and contrasts with the existing literature in pro-
gramming education [Robins, 2019]. One possible explanation for this discrep-
ancy could be the MSLQ limitations in measuring cognitive strategies relevant to
programming learning.

107

Chapter 5
Meta-analysis of the studies that used regulatory scaffoldings to support pro-
gramming learning
The systematic literature review presented in the previous section focused on ob-
servational studies that sought to understand how SRL abilities associate with
programming learning outcomes. In contrast, this meta-analysis was designed
with a distinct objective: to investigate the impact of self-regulation through ana-
lyzing outcomes from studies that implemented regulatory scaffolding interven-
tions.
A meta-analysis and systematic literature reviews share similarities in the use of
systematic processes to summarize research data. However, meta-analyses have
as their main purpose the statistical combination of quantitative data, which of-
fers several advantages over individual study analyses: a) combining multiple
studies results in the augmentation of sample size, which enhances the repre-
sentativeness of the studied population, b) the ability to combine effect sizes in-
creases statistical power [Cohn and Becker, 2003], and c) it provides a more objec-
tive basis for decision-making compared to systematic literature reviews [Moher
et al., 2007].
The studies included in the meta-analysis were extracted from three systematic
literature reviews that targeted on identifying educational software grounded on
the regulation of learning theory to support programming learning [Garcia et al.,
2018a; Loksa et al., 2022; Silva et al., 2021b]. The selection criteria encompassed
quasi-experimental and experimental studies that included control groups, en-
hancing the potential for drawing stronger causal conclusions. They should have
evaluated the impact of regulatory scaffoldings, on programming learning out-
come [Lakens, 2013]. Only studies that reported the effect size or provided the
necessary data for its calculation were included.
A total of seven studies met the inclusion criteria, resulting in a cumulative sam-
ple size of 844 students 1. Most studies (except Ilves et al. [2018]) achieved posi-
tive results using regulatory scaffoldings in support students’ learning outcomes.
There is a consistency in the results across different experiments, which suggests
that the use of regulatory scaffoldings has potential utility in programming edu-
cation.
Figure 5.1 presents a forest plot, serving as a graphical representation to visual-
ize the heterogeneity in effect sizes across the incorporated studies. This visual-
ization facilitates comparisons among studies, an essential component of meta-
analytic procedure [Cohn and Becker, 2003].
The analysis indicated an average effect size of 0.59 with a confidence interval of
[0.28, 0.89]. This finding sheds light on the feasibility of the use of SRL scaffold-
ings as a strategy that researchers and educators can use to support programming
learning. At the same time, this finding aligns with effect sizes observed in SRL
interventions in other educational domains [Dignath and Büttner, 2008; Ergen
and Kanadli, 2017; Zheng, 2016a]. This is a result that demonstrates that regu-
1For a comprehensive list of studies included in the meta-analysis, please refer to our Open
Science Repository: https://osf.io/tu47x/?view_only=aab2c35ee94d41b1abaf5ececea3f0dd.
108

Complementary analysis
Figure 5.1: Forest plot with the effect sizes from the studies analyzed in the meta-
analysis.
latory scaffoldings can be used in different pedagogical contexts, despite their
specificities.
Main findings
The results of this research question contribute to the development of a knowl-
edge base on the theme of self-regulation in programming education, which is
necessary for their consolidation as a research field [Zimmerman and Schunk,
2011]. The findings suggest that under multiple forms of analysis, self-regulation
is a relevant construct for programming learning. Associative analyses indicate
that abilities like metacognition and motivational regulation have a higher corre-
lation with performance, while experimental studies demonstrate that the use of
regulatory scaffoldings can promote learning gains.
There are still open questions regarding the psychometric validity of some instru-
ments used to measure regulatory abilities and the pedagogical contexts in which
these instruments are most appropriate. Especially considering the importance
of the MSLQ, future studies should focus on its psychometric evaluation in the
field of programming education. Additionally, there is a lack of understanding
regarding which scaffoldings are most effective.
5.2 RQ9) What regulatory strategies are used by pro-
gramming students?
The regulation of learning holds significant relevance as a theoretical framework
to aid in understanding the learning processes involved in knowledge acquisition
[Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. This perspective has influenced many studies
109

Chapter 5
that have adopted this framework to explore the regulatory strategies students
employ during their learning journey [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. This area
of research is important for identifying and understanding the specific behaviors
and strategies students use while learning, as well as for assessing how these
strategies influence their educational outcomes [Zimmerman and Pons, 1986].
In a programming learning context where multiple mental processes are involved,
it is important to understand how students regulate themselves. This knowledge
aids in understanding how students learn programming by examining how they
deal with various situations that arise during learning and what actions they take
to achieve their goals. The use of self-regulation theory as a means to investigate
learning strategies in programming is still a topic underexplored by studies in the
field of programming education [Prather et al., 2022].
Understanding the regulatory strategies within programming education demands
further empirical exploration [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. This knowledge can provide
valuable insights into how programming students learn and identify the regula-
tory strategies that may uniquely suit this particular domain [Falkner et al., 2014].
The present research question is situated within this context and designed to in-
vestigate the regulatory strategies employed by students.
The sample consisted of 50 students who participated in the RESPE experimental
evaluation (ExpSRL and ExpSRLSocialReg). Questions were used to foster stu-
dents’ reflections upon their learning experiences across different stages of the
coding development process. These questions were presented in the format of
popups, the first one was presented right after the student read the programming
exercise statement and before having access to the programming editor: "(1) How
do you intend to solve this exercise?". The second question was presented in the
occurrence of successive syntactical programming errors: "(2) What can be done
to solve these problems?" Finally, the third question was presented after students
completed the test cases for the exercise: "(3) Now that you finished, what went
right and what went wrong?" This question was not displayed in cases where
students did not complete the exercise (e.g., by dropping out).
Students’ responses underwent thematic analysis guided by the theoretical frame-
work of SRL. Most of the coding emerged from the data, but its construction was
also influenced by previous research on this theme. The analysis sought to un-
derstand how students’ actions reflected regulatory strategies. For this purpose,
Zimmerman’s theoretical framework was used, particularly his definition of the
regulatory process occurring in phases, and the mental processes involved in the
regulatory processes as used by Pintrich (e.g., cognition, metacognition, motiva-
tion, behavior). Thus, the analysis aimed to identify how the strategy referred to
these mental processes, and in which phase of learning it had been referenced.
Research in the field of programming education also influenced the coding pro-
cess analysis. The problem-solving process presented by Loksa and Ko [2016]
and Gomes and Mendes [2007b], contextualizes how programming learning oc-
curs within a problem-solving perspective. Additionally, the conceptualization
that regulatory strategies are of general-purpose or programming-specific was
also considered [Falkner et al., 2014].
110

Complementary analysis
Data on students’ performance on the course was used to segment them into
high-performing and low-performing. Those who achieved a grade superior to
70 and earned the course certificate were classified as high-performers, otherwise
as low-performers. This classification allowed the understanding of how the reg-
ulatory strategies within these subgroups to discern potential differences.
Finally, students indicated their prior experience with programming. In this indi-
cation, they reported less than 6 months, more than six months but less than one
year, more than one year but less than two years, and two years or more.
A limitation of this data is that it does not assess the students’ proficiency in
executing the regulatory strategies. Instead, it reflects the students’ recollection
of employing a particular resource in a given context.
Thirty-seven distinct regulatory strategies were identified (Table 5.6). A percent-
age of the occurrence of these strategies is presented according to the student’s
performance of high (H.P) or lower-performers (L.P) and by their prior program-
ming experience in years and categorized as : <6m = less than six months; 6m

Table 5.6: Strategies used by students and the corresponding coding scheme.
A variety of regulatory strategies was observed, corroborating the view that pro-
gramming learning consists of a multifaceted set of mental processes. An inter-
play is observed between cognitive strategies used in understanding problems
and creating codes, metacognitive processes for reviewing learning milestones
and correcting errors, and motivation to praise achievements and persist in the
face of difficulties.
It is observed that the number of students reporting some strategies varied de-
pending on their performance, indicating that these two groups, in some cases,
externalize different regulatory strategies. For example, regarding the strategy of
planning the program logic (1.2.1), approximately twice as many respondents
from the high-performance group indicated they carry out this strategy com-
pared to the low-performers group. Most high-performers (91.7%) reported be-
tween two to more than five strategies, compared to 73.3% of low-performers.
Although no causal relation could be attributed to this data, combined with the
previous one, it points to an important finding regarding the prevalence of reg-
ulatory strategies among high performers. However, it should be pondered that
further investigation is necessary.
Table 5.7 shows the distribution of students categorized by the number of self-
regulatory strategies they reported employing. Thirty-two percent of students
with less than one year of prior programming experience did not report any reg-
ulatory strategies or reported only one. This indicates that a set of strategies
matures over time as students engage with programming. This also indicates
that students with less programming experience had more difficulty transferring
general regulatory strategies, acquired throughout their lives, to programming
learning. It is known that knowledge transfer is a complex process and, as the
data suggests, it also appears to be influenced by the student’s length of experi-
ence in the domain being investigated. These findings highlight a challenge for
professors on how to stimulate these strategies early in the learning process so
that students can benefit from them at the beginning of their academic journey.

Conceptual framework proposition
The findings from the regulatory strategies externalized by the students moti-
vated the development of a conceptual framework called Strategies for Regulat-
ing Learning in Programming (SRLP). This framework organizes the set of reg-
ulatory strategies observed during programming learning activities, providing
a structured perspective on their application throughout different phases of the
learning process. To enhance the clarity of this framework, the strategies were
also categorized based on their pertinence to either general-purpose regulatory
processes or those specific to programming. This approach adheres with the
schema proposed by Falkner et al. [2015], and allows for a more nuanced un-
derstanding of the specific demands of the programming domain.
The framework phases comprised: planning, program creation, error correction,
progress monitoring, and self-assessment. These phases were inspired by the
phases proposed by Zimmerman’s model (forethought, performance, and self-
reflection) but were adapted to fit the context of programming learning. It was
assessed that these phases oversimplified the stages of programming learning
and that a new division would more accurately represent these specifics.
The ’forethought’ phase, where students plan their task execution, corresponds
to the "planning" phase in programming. The ’performance’ phase, which refers
to the execution and monitoring of the educational task, was divided into ’pro-
gram creation’, ’error correction’, and ’progress monitoring’. These three phases
represent distinct processes with different purposes and are integral during code
development. Finally, the equivalent to self-reflection in this context is ’self-
assessment’.
Figure 5.2 presents the framework, along with its phases and the regulatory strate-
gies. The colors green and red indicate the strategies most frequently reported by
high-performing and low-performing students, respectively.
The planning phase serves as the initial stage in code development, encompass-
ing strategies aimed at comprehensively understanding the exercise’s problem,
its requirements and identifying the programming elements necessary for de-
veloping the code. During this phase, there is a notable integration of general
regulatory strategies with those specific to programming, reflecting the neces-
sary actions to transform a problem into a computational solution. This result
highlights the interplay between general-purpose strategies and programming-
specific strategies. For instance, insights gleaned from problem analysis were
commonly used to identify requisite programming resources. This interplay is
116

Complementary analysis
Figure 5.2: The Strategies for Regulating Learning in Programming (SRLP) con-
ceptual framework describing the regulatory strategies externalized by program-
ming students.
117

Chapter 5
exemplified in the following excerpt, which features the concurrent externaliza-
tion of three strategies: "1.1.1 Understanding the problem" , and "1.2.2 Define the
necessary programming resources".
#50 "First, I will identify what the problem is requesting. Then I will use the
variables that fit the programming structure. [...]"
The analysis of students’ responses revealed that most of them (86.2%) articu-
lated at least one planning strategy, indicating the existence of basic planning
competencies. However, the data in Table 5.6 show that this quantity is mostly
concentrated on the ’1.1.1 understanding the problem’ strategy. The other strate-
gies exhibited a significant lower number of students who indicated using them.
These observations suggest that a comprehensive set of planning strategies is of-
ten lacking, thereby corroborating the findings of [Loksa et al., 2020].
In some situations, students reported the strategies following a specific order.
This sequence may imply an interdependence among the strategies, indicating
that the utilization of specific strategies could depend on or be enhanced by the
application of preceding ones. The excerpt below provides an example of this
phenomenon:
#1 "1) try to understand the problem; 2) think about the variables it will have; 3)
think about the necessary inputs and outputs; 4) think about the logic that
needs to be computed;"
This student used numerical labels to delineate the actions they undertake, sug-
gesting an ordered sequence in which the tasks were executed. The following
strategies: "1.1.1 Understanding the problem", "1.2.1 Plan program logic", and "1.2.2
Define the necessary programming resources" can be observed in this response.
The interrelationship between different strategies can pose a challenge, as an er-
ror in one strategy could potentially lead to complications in subsequent strate-
gies. For example, a misunderstanding of the problem could result in the selec-
tion of inappropriate programming resources. In this situation, students’ metacog-
nitive abilities play a fundamental role in helping them identify their mistakes.
Despite motivational regulation being an important element in fostering self-
efficacy and goal establishment, critical processes for successful academic per-
formance [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011], our data reveals that students did not
report strategies related to these aspects. Future research is encouraged to under-
stand how students regulate their motivation during the planning phase and its
implications for their success in the subsequent phases of the code development
process.
During the program creation phase , students translate their understanding of
the problem into a computational solution. In this phase, students combine their
programming knowledge with problem-solving strategies, such as "2.2.1 Divide
the code writing process into parts". The effective completion of this stage is highly
118

Complementary analysis
dependent on the strategies adopted in the planning phase. A concise under-
standing of the problem is crucial and is a basis for preliminary decisions about
the required programming components to complete the task [Loksa and Ko, 2016;
Robins, 2019].
Program creation depends upon students’ programming knowledge competency.
According to McGill and Volet [1997], this knowledge refers to the understand-
ing of the syntactic elements of a programming language, and the ability to use
them accurately to design programming code. Difficulties with programming
knowledge can often lead to programming errors, which will be discussed in the
subsequent phase.
To compensate for limitations with programming knowledge, students frequently
reported utilizing general-purpose strategies. For example, "2.1.2 Review lesson
materials" was commonly cited, indicating that some students were metacogni-
tively able to recognize the need for adjustments in their learning process, and
use this strategy to help them acquire the desired knowledge:
#29 "I review the class materials again whenever I can [...]"
Additional strategies that were commonly cited include "2.1.1 Reviewing prior ex-
ercises" and "2.1.4 Reading annotations". These strategies represent attempts to re-
visit programming concepts or their practical implementation, highlighting their
role in reinforcing the students’ understanding of the task. Some corrective strate-
gies such as "2.1.3 Reread the exercise statement" and "2.1.4 Reading annotations"
were only reported by high-performers, suggesting a higher usage of strategies to
overcomer their limitations. Nonetheless, it still need to further investigate how
programming students are aware of their limitations and how they deal with this
problem.
Encountering errors is a common aspect of code development that can impact stu-
dents differently. While some students may resolve programming errors quickly,
others may struggle [Smith and Rixner, 2019], and difficulties in this process can
harm their motivation [Bosch et al., 2013].
The error correction phase entails various strategies used for error identification
and correction. Students’ responses revealed that multiple psychological pro-
cesses are associated with error correction, demonstrating the complexities of this
phase. For example, evaluating one’s own understanding of the current error is
an example of a metacognitive resource, whereas identifying and correcting the
programming error requires cognitive knowledge. At the same time, students
must show resilient behavior to overcome their difficulties, which is a motiva-
tional factor [Pintrich and De Groot, 1990].
A critical element of this phase is the comprehensive understanding of errors, of-
ten facilitated by tools like "3.2.3 Analysis of the Python’s interpreter output" and
"3.2.4 Use of test-case data". These tools, integrated within the programming envi-
ronment, provide valuable insights into the nature, location, and impact of errors
against test cases. The utilization of these strategies was reported predominantly
119

Chapter 5
among high-performing students. Nevertheless, only a minority of these stu-
dents explicitly mentioned them. This finding highlights a notable gap students
possess to effectively leverage the resources provided by the programming envi-
ronment for error diagnosis and correction.
Programming interpreter or compiler outputs are examples of the above-mentioned
resources provided by the programming environment. However, existing litera-
ture points out that students often struggle with interpreting these outputs, partly
because these tools were not designed explicitly with educational objectives in
mind [Becker et al., 2019]. Consequently, further support and guidance may be
needed to enable students to understand better and utilize the information of-
fered by these tools.
Upon achieving a thorough error understanding, the next logical step is "3.1.6
Reflect on the error’s cause," which involves the application of both metacognitive
and cognitive resources. Metacognitive monitoring includes reconsidering what
the student already understands about the error, whereas the cognitive aspect
involves evaluating the potential causes of the issue and using programming
knowledge for its correction. Again, difficulties or limitations with this type of
knowledge can lead students to difficulties in providing corrective actions as ex-
emplified by the following excerpt:
#17 "I had trouble at first deciding which would be the repeat: "for" or "while"
[...]."
Challenges in understanding the problem can lead to a particular category of mis-
take known as a logical error, where the code, while syntactically correct, does not
produce the intended output. This issue stems from an inaccurate mental repre-
sentation of the problem, which can complicate the coding process, as demon-
strated below:
#40 "I was misreading the statement and didn’t realize that I needed to use 3
variables, and for lack of attention, I didn’t see that I was wrong."
To address this issue, students reported utilizing the strategy of "3.1.5 Rereading
the exercise’s statement" to enhance their problem comprehension. Notably, this
strategy was exclusively reported by high-performing students. This observation
suggests that while a considerable proportion of students emphasized the impor-
tance of understanding the problem during the planning phase, fewer have re-
visited this crucial step as a corrective strategy during the error correction phase.
Students also exhibited the capacity to formulate personalized strategies for tack-
ling programming errors. For example, the following excerpt features a high-
performing student who articulates they mental processes during the error cor-
rection phase, methodically tracing the code execution to pinpoint elements that
may be contributing to the errors.
#1 "I try to map all the coding snippets that might have an error, and I will test
them separately by putting (programming) comments."
120

Complementary analysis
The excerpt underscores a sophisticated hypothesis-testing approach employed
by the student, which involves several steps: i) identify areas of their program-
ming code that could be the root of the error, which involved segmented testing
of the code ("3.2.1 Test code in parts"), and iii) utilization of debugging strategies
to isolate problematic code sections ( "3.2.5 Use of debug strategies" ). To achieve
this latter approach, students have repurposed programming comments not in-
herently designed for debugging to assist in their error correction.
This finding demonstrates how students adapt their learning environment to
overcome their difficulties. Whether these strategies emerged spontaneously or
evolved remains an open question, but they constitute a compelling area for fu-
ture research.
Providing an adequate error response also depends on students’ emotional flex-
ibility to deal with the difficulties that are part of this process [Bonilla R. et al.,
2020]. This dimension is elucidated in the subsequent excerpt:
#20 "Keep calm, knowing that the error can actually happen."
The excerpt elucidates the intricate relationship between emotional regulation
and programming, emphasizing the critical role of managing emotions that sur-
face during the learning journey.
Emotional regulation becomes particularly salient in problem-solving activities
like programming, where individuals often have to forego immediate rewards
in favor of long-term objectives, thus navigating through inevitable complexities
[Thompson, 1991].
Without appropriate support, struggling students might abandon the task, ad-
versely affecting their academic performance. Students acknowledged the ad-
verse effects of errors on their emotional well-being and self-esteem, particularly
when unable to complete an exercise. However, only a few mentioned strate-
gies related to motivational regulation. Given the pivotal role of motivation in
educational success in programming [Gomes et al., 2018], this calls for additional
research to delve into how students emotionally navigate during error corrections
and the subsequent impact on their academic outcomes.
Students frequently resort to external resources when they encounter program-
ming errors they cannot resolve by themselves, such as "3.1.1 Search for comple-
mentary material" or "3.1.2 Help request".
#31 "[...] if necessary I search the internet or ask for help."
Some students faced substantial challenges in identifying, understanding, and
correcting errors. Such challenges may be indicative of underlying deficiencies in
metacognitive skills, as exemplified in the following excerpt:
#38 "I can’t understand what I need to do and what my difficulties are. I don’t
understand how to make it work and what I did wrong."
121

Chapter 5
Loksa et al. [2020] and Arakawa et al. [2021] have found that students often expe-
rience metacognitive difficulties when evaluating their performance, hindering
their progress in programming activities. Deploying computational scaffolding
and training programs could serve as strategies to mitigate this issue [Loksa and
Ko, 2016].
The error correction process is intricate, making the role of professors critical in
supporting students. First and foremost, educators should encourage students
to perceive mistakes through the Productive Failure framework, which treats errors
as opportunities for deeper understanding and learning opportunities [Kapur,
2015]. In addition, professors should guide students through the emotional and
motivational challenges that arise during the error correction process, helping
them stay engaged and motivated to learn from their errors.
The progress monitoring phase unfolds concurrently with the program creation
and error correction phases. In this phase, students keep track of their progress
in fulfilling the task requirements and evaluate the adequacy of their code con-
cerning programming syntax. Progress monitoring is vital, enabling students
to observe their performance and implement necessary corrections [Zimmerman
and Schunk, 2011].
Fewer students reported utilizing progress monitoring strategies in this phase
compared to others. This finding suggests a potential deficiency in their capa-
bility to engage in effective monitoring, as previously indicated in the literature
[Law et al., 2017]. Students often encounter challenges in monitoring the progress
of their program development, often inaccurately believing they are closer to
completing an exercise than they actually are [Pedrosa et al., 2016; Prather et al.,
2018].
Students only externalized programming-specific strategies to monitor their per-
formance, such as "4.1.2 Use the test-case data. Providing students with exercises’
test cases is important, as it represents an explicit evaluative criteria, which is
crucial for cognitive processing [Greene and Azevedo, 2007].
Students also cited the feedback from the Python interpreter as a valuable re-
source for monitoring their progress. Specifically, some students have employed
the "4.1.3 Test the program frequently" strategy to gather more frequent feedback
from the interpreter, thereby enhancing their awareness of the accuracy of their
code. This practice is essential as it allows students to gauge their performance by
considering the completion status of the exercise’s test cases while also receiving
feedback on syntax accuracy of their programming code. We hypothesize that
this continuous evaluation approach is more effective than merely testing the
code after its development, as it enables ongoing performance assessment and
early identification of mistakes. However, given the limited scope of our data,
further investigation is warranted.
Finally, the self-assessment phase occurs at the end of an exercise, regardless
of whether it was successfully completed. Self-assessment is vital as it enables
students to identify areas for improvement and to sustain their motivation for
ongoing studies [Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. This practice facilitates student
reflection on their performance and behavior, elements crucial for enriching their
122

Complementary analysis
learning experiences [Day et al., 2015].
During this phase, students reported undergoing "5.1.1 Emotional regulation", ex-
periencing either positive or negative emotions based on their performance. Ad-
ditionally,"5.1.2 Effort self-assessment"was activated as students assessed the level
of effort they had invested in the task:
#21 "I had a hard time understanding what the question asked. But, I practiced
and studied a little more and was able to do the activity."
Loksa and Ko [2016] observed that the most recurring reflections students made
were on their learning process, confidence, and mistakes. Expanding upon this
observation, a further dimensions was observed in our data related to the evalu-
ation of the quality of students’ program. This behavior can be observed in the
strategies of "5.2.1 Code Refactoring" and "5.2.3 Code Review".
Code refactoring involves making modifications to the code after meeting evalu-
ative criteria to enhance its quality. This process implies that the student recog-
nizes the value of refining their code as a critical aspect of their learning journey,
justifying the investment of time and effort. Such an activity demands that stu-
dents meticulously examine their code to pinpoint areas for enhancement. On
the other hand, code review is characterized as revisiting the code without im-
plementing changes. Students engage in this strategy to reflect on their accom-
plishments and consolidate their understanding, viewing it as an opportunity to
reinforce their knowledge.
Higher-performers presented increased externalization of code refactoring, sug-
gesting a higher metacognitive awareness, as this strategy demands self-evaluative
processes and the identification of areas for improvements. Low-performers who
did externalize the code refactoring and review might indicate their attempt to
understand and evaluate their work, but may not know how to improve it.
Finally, 17.6% of the students explicitly declared that they refrained from any
form of reflective activity post-program completion. Coupled with a consider-
able portion of students who did not cite any self-assessment strategies, this data
indicates a probable deficiency in this capacity among a significant number of
students. Such a limitation may stem from multiple factors, such as a lack of
understanding of the value of self-regulation or challenges in self-evaluation, as
identified by previous literature [Prather et al., 2020].
5.2.1 How do the SRL strategies used by high and low-performing
students differ?
Comparing the strategies externalized by high-performing and low-performing
students is a typical procedure to study their behavior [Falkner et al., 2014]. The
findings indicate that high-performing students presented an average of 4.66
regulatory strategies (SD=2.64) compared to an average of 3.6 (SD=2.5) of low-
performers. However, it is important to consider that this result should be in-

terpreted cautiously due to a non-significant difference observed in the t-test (t =
1.36 and p-value = 0.18).
Table 5.8 groups students according to the externalization of strategies related to
the SRLP phases and on the categorization of these strategies as general-purpose
or programming-specific. The percentages indicate the frequency of students in
a specific group (e.g., performance and prior experience) who had externalized a
specific group of strategies.
Spearman’s correlation analysis between the quantity of externalized strategies
and programming performance yielded a low negative correlation of 0.06, with
a p-value greater than 0.05. This counterintuitive result implies that the number
of strategies articulated by students may not necessarily serve as an indicator of
their performance. One plausible hypothesis posits that the efficacy of strategy
implementation, rather than the mere count of strategies employed, might be a
more influential determinant of performance. This aspect warrants further inves-
tigation in subsequent research.
When evaluating the overall externalization of regulatory strategies (Table 5.8),
124

Complementary analysis
without segregating them into general-purpose or programming-specific cate-
gories, high-performing students manifested a greater percentage of strategies
across all phases of the proposed conceptual framework, except the error cor-
rection phase. This observation is consistent with prior research, indicating that
high-performers often externalize higher number of regulatory strategies [Bergin
et al., 2005; Cigdem, 2015].
Table 5.8 shows that low-performing students predominantly externalized general-
purpose strategies, while high-performers more frequently reported programming-
specific strategies. This observation corroborates existing research and under-
scores the importance of specific strategies in this domain [Falkner et al., 2015].
This finding reveals an opportunity for crafting interventions that nurture these
specialized regulatory skills. Nonetheless, additional evidence is necessary to
constitute a more concise body of knowledge on the students’ regulatory strate-
gies and further research is necessary. The insights garnered from this doctoral
research, in particular the conceptual framework, can inform and guide these fu-
ture endeavors.
5.2.2 Main findings
The strategies identified in this study provide important insights into the diverse
psychological resources students utilize in learning programming. The major-
ity of the strategies were associated with cognitive and metacognitive processes,
with motivational regulation being less prevalent. While this pattern may reflect
the research methodology, it potentially underscores an area of underemphasis
in motivational regulation. Future investigations should explore in greater depth
how students regulate their motivation and emotions during programming edu-
cation, highlighting the need for a more comprehensive understanding of these
processes.
The findings also indicate that categorizing the regulatory strategies deployed by
high-performing and low-performing students has proven instrumental in pin-
pointing differences that may account for variations in academic outcomes. No-
tably, the increased frequency of programming-specific strategies among high-
performers marks an area of significance for SRL research within this educational
domain. Future studies should deeper investigate the regulatory strategies pre-
dominantly used by low-performers to evaluate their potential contributions to
the learning process.
Finally, from a perspective of supporting the design of computational environ-
ments based on learning regulation, the strategies uncovered in this investiga-
tion can be a reference point for researchers and educators. Scaffoldings could be
designed to stimulate certain strategies, especially those more frequently used by
high-performers. However, additional evidence on how high and low-performers
regulate themselves is necessary.
125

Chapter 5
5.3 RQ10) What is the psychometric validity of self-
regulation measurement instruments in the con-
text of programming education?
The results presented by this doctoral research indicate that programming edu-
cation has unique characteristics pertinent to learning regulation. The evidence
presented so far underscores the feasibility of using programming-specific scaf-
foldings to support learning achievement. Moreover, it was observed that stu-
dents exhibiting higher performance levels tend to more frequently articulate
programming-specific regulatory strategies. Addressing the distinct aspects of
programming education is essential to comprehensively understand the nuances
of regulatory processes within this field, which includes the use of programming-
centric instruments for assessing regulatory skills [Loksa et al., 2022].
The Self-Regulation Programming Strategies Questionnaire (SPSQ) was devel-
oped by Lu et al. [2017], and seeks to measure the regulatory competency of
programming students. However, the lack of psychometric validity comprises
its usage [Souza et al., 2017]. In this regard, replication studies to test model as-
sumptions across varied samples are also crucial for comprehensive validation
[Kline, 2015]. This research question sought to mitigate this limitation by per-
forming a psychometric evaluation of this instrument.
This questionnaire is structured around seven constructs: 1) attention regulation;
2) planning ahead of programming; 3) content monitoring; 4) organization; 5)
checking and correcting; 6) planning during code; and 7) self-evaluation (Table
3.4). In psychological terms, a construct represents an abstract concept that cap-
tures a specific psychological attribute or process, which is not directly observ-
able [Yong and Pearce, 2013]. Each construct is operationalized through a series
of items, which collectively aim to capture the essence of that construct.
In the context of the SPSQ, these constructs are intended to reflect various stages
and components of the programming process, encompassing planning, execu-
tion, monitoring, and evaluation. This instrument specifically targets cognitive
and metacognitive aspects crucial to effective programming practice.
5.3.1 Methodology
The psychometric evaluation focused on the analysis of the instrument’s relia-
bility and validity, in line with standard psychometric procedures [Souza et al.,
2017].
Educational context and sampling
The study involved ninety-one undergraduate students from introductory pro-
gramming courses in a computer science program in Macao. The students were
enrolled in an introductory programming class taught in Java, lasting a total of 45-
126

Complementary analysis
hours in one semester. The syllabus covered variables, control flow statements,
methods, arrays, basic user interfaces, inputs/outputs, and recursion. The mean
age of participants was 20.41, with a standard deviation of 3.4. 84.4% identified
as male, 9.4% as female, and 6.2% chose not to disclose their gender. The stu-
dents answered the SPSQ and MSLQ instruments in English, the official course
language.
The sample size equated to a subject-to-item ratio of 4.8 for the SPSQ, meeting the
minimum criterion for adequate instrument validation. This ratio is consistent
with previous psychometric validation studies in other domains [Anthoine et al.,
2014].
Instrument validation
Reliability was assessed using the Cronbach Alpha coefficient, calculated for the
SPSQ’s six dimensions and an overall score. This technique aims to assess the
internal consistency of a set of items, by analyzing their inter-item correlation
consistency across the subjects that responded to the instrument. Reliability is a
relevant metric for assessing the adequacy of a test or scale, and combined with
other metrics serves as an indicative to support or discard their usage.
Three strategies were performed to assess instrument validity: i) correlation be-
tween the scores of SPSQ with the final course grade; ii) comparison between
SPSQ and MSLQ, and iii) a confirmatory factor analysis. The MSLQ was cho-
sen due to its acceptance as an instrument for measuring self-regulated learn-
ing across multiple educational settings [Roth et al., 2015], and as both instru-
ments aim to measure similar psychological constructs, a positive linear correla-
tion is expected to be observed [Astrua et al., 2007]. A linear regression model
was also developed to predict MSLQ scores based on SPSQ scores, adhering to
widely-used metrics for instrument comparison and validation [Cook and Beck-
man, 2006]. Lastly, a factor analysis was conducted to validate the SPSQ’s six
scales. A confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) was employed in this study to test
the original author’s scale proposition, which is a strategy consistent with the ob-
jectives of psychometric validation [Souza et al., 2017]. CFA aims to empirically
validate a theoretical model (e.g., the scales of the SPSQ) using collected data
(e.g., students’ responses). This step is crucial in the psychometric process, but
the SPSQ authors did not provide this statistical data.
5.3.2 Results
The instrument’s Cronbach’s Alphas are presented in Table 5.9. Values closer to
1.0 generally indicate high internal consistency, whereas values closer to 0 indi-
cate low consistency [Tavakol and Dennick, 2011].
The results from Table 5.9 indicate the overall alpha value for the entire instru-
ment supports its robustness and good internal consistency [Tavakol and Den-
nick, 2011], and is closer to the authors’ original data [Lu et al., 2017]. The scales of
content monitoring, checking and correcting, and self-evaluation demonstrated
