satisfactory internal consistency, but the scales of attention regulation, planning
ahead of programming, and planning during coding exhibited more significant
variability in students’ responses.
Correlation between SPSQ scales and final course grade
The correlation between the scales of an instrument and the students’ final course
grades is often used as a metric for evaluating the suitability of an instrument in
educational contexts [Pintrich et al., 1993]. In this context, Pearson’s correlation
results between the SPSQ scores and final course grades are detailed in Table
5.10. A moderate positive correlation of 0.39 (p <0.001) was found between the
SPSQ scores and the final course grades, close to the correlation observed for the
MSLQ scores at 0.44. This indicates that the regulatory skills measured by these
instruments in this educational context have some level of association with learn-
ing. Additionally, the similar correlation coefficients observed for the SPSQ and
MSLQ suggest that both instruments exhibit comparable effectiveness in measur-
ing regulatory skills associated with learning outcomes in this context.
128

Complementary analysis
Correlational analysis between SPSQ and MSLQ
The Pearson’s correlation between SPSQ and MSLQ scores was ρ = 0.64 [0.50,
0.74], p <0.001. This result indicates a moderate positive relationship between
the scores on the two instruments, supporting their concurrent validity [Bishara
and Hittner, 2012]. A regression model was employed to predict MSLQ scores
based on SPSQ scores. The model yielded the following statistics F(1,95) = 65.03,
p <0.001, and an adjusted R2 = 0.40. Corroborating the previous finding, the
result indicates a degree of association between the instruments, and SPSQ scores
can account for 40% of the variability in MSLQ scores. To assess the model’s
robustness, k-fold cross-validation (n=10) was performed, resulting in metrics of
R2 = 0.53, RMSE = 0.60, and MAE = 0.45 [Wong and Yeh, 2020]. These metrics
indicate the model’s performance in predicting actual values from the dataset
and provide a baseline for future comparisons.
Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA)
Prior to the CFA analysis, an analysis of the correlation between the instruments’
constructs (Table 5.11) was performed. This data is crucial in understanding the
interrelationships between these constructs [Yong and Pearce, 2013], which al-
lows for theoretical evaluations. For example, evidence from the MSLQ suggests
that the constructs of self-efficacy and lower test anxiety are interrelated, and are
expected to exhibit a higher correlation [Nie et al., 2011]. At the same time, if two
constructs were expected to associate and the data shows the opposite, it could
point out to a validity issue that demands further investigation. The correlational
analysis could also demonstrate the multicolinearity problem, where constructs
have a higher degree of association and might be redundant because they could
represent a similar phenomenon. Lastly, the analysis of the relationships between
constructs helps deepen the understanding of programming learning processes,
as illustrated in the following paragraph.
The data points out that some constructs had strong correlations, indicative that
the mental process involved in their realization could have some relationship. For
example, the planning ahead of programming construct, which refers to planning
strategies prior to code development, is highly associated with a student’s ability
to coordinate their attention. These constructs refer to cognitive processes [Zim-
merman and Schunk, 2011], and the presented correlation suggests that students’
attention can influence their planning capabilities. The presented evidence, albeit
fragile, supports future investigations to analyze how the stimulation of certain
mental processes (e.g., of cognitive nature) can influence the execution of other
related processes. In the context of the analyzed data, one hypothesis that could
be further investigated is how stimuli for attention regulation foster planning re-
alization.
The results also suggest that cognitive and metacognitive-related constructs ex-
hibit some level of independence from each other. For example, attention regu-
lation has only a moderate association with checking and correcting, which in-
cludes items with a more metacognitive characterization. This finding provides

an initial indication that providing support for the execution of related mental
processes could be more beneficial to their development, than combining stimuli
to distinct processes (e.g., cognition and metacognition). However, this decision
also depends on the research objectives, as it may be of interest to stimulate men-
tal processes of different natures in the same intervention, as carried out in this
doctorate. In addition, the evidence is still preliminary.
From a psychometric perspective, the higher correlations observed between cer-
tain constructs, such as content monitoring and self-evaluation, indicate a multi-
collinearity issue. This observation suggests that these constructs measure simi-
lar phenomenon, and could indeed be combined into a single measure [Watkins,
2018]. However, our analysis does not extend to an in-depth discussion of this
issue, and it would be advisable for future research to conduct exploratory anal-
yses to investigate this matter further.
The confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) diagram is illustrated in Figure 5.3. This
diagram displays the items from the instruments, categorizing them under spe-
cific constructs, and assigns a numerical value to each item, known as the fac-
tor loading. This value quantifies the relevance of each item to its respective
construct. According to the literature, a factor loading of 0.7 or higher is rec-
ommended, suggesting that the construct accounts for over fifty percent of the
item’s variance [Hair et al., 2019]. Nevertheless, lower factor loadings may still
be deemed acceptable if the overall instrument demonstrates robust internal con-
sistency [Hair and Alamer, 2022], a criterion met by the SPSQ. Despite this, items
3, 5, 12, 13, and 16 exhibited unsatisfactory loadings, raising concerns about their
appropriateness for the intended constructs. Future studies should undertake
exploratory analyses to determine whether these items align more closely with
different constructs or whether they should be excluded from the questionnaire
130

Complementary analysis
Figure 5.3: Confirmatory factor analysis for the SPSQ constructs.
altogether.
Regarding the goodness-of-fit indices, the following values were observed: a χ2
of 1.98, a Comparative Fit Index (CFI) of 0.83, a Tucker-Lewis Index (TLI) of 0.80,
a Root Mean Square Error of Approximation (RMSEA) of 0.1, and a Standardized
Root Mean Square Residual (SRMR) of 0.08. These indices suggest that the model
is generally satisfactory but leaves room for improvement [CHo and Summers,
2012]. These findings further underscore the need for an exploratory factor anal-
ysis to examine the factor structure of the questionnaire more comprehensively.
5.3.3 Main findings
The results of the psychometric validation suggest that the SPSQ is a promising
tool for use in programming education. Its use allows for studying the regulatory
process among programming students through learning strategies specific to this
field. The collected data also enables an understanding of the interrelationship
between strategies, as discussed in this section. From a teaching perspective,
this instrument can be used to identify students’ limitations (e.g., with certain
strategies), which can help educators guide interventions to mitigate them.
Nevertheless, the SPSQ’s usage should be pondered by some concerns raised dur-
ing the CFA. In addition, the absence of other extensive psychometric evaluations
minimizes the validity of this instrument. Another limitation of this instrument
is the lack of scales related to motivational and emotional regulation. To cope
with this limitation, researchers can use the MSLQ’s scales representing these
131

Chapter 5
constructs as they showed potential usage in the programming education domain
[Silva et al., 2021a].
132

Chapter 6
Discussion
This section delves into the implications of this doctorate findings. Firstly, it sheds
light on the current state-of-the-art in the use of regulation of learning scaffold-
ing and collaborative learning in programming education. Secondly, factors in-
fluencing the design of regulatory scaffolding for computer-based learning envi-
ronments were presented. Thirdly, the RESPE’s effectiveness in supporting pro-
gramming learning was assessed, followed by an analysis of how students regu-
late during programming activities are also discussed. Lastly, the relationship be-
tween students’ self-regulation abilities and their performance in programming
tasks was examined.
6.1 RQ1) Which regulatory scaffoldings are used in
programming education?
Identifying the regulatory scaffolding used in programming education from past
studies is essential in understanding what has been done, its effectiveness, and
areas with potential for future investigations. A literature review was performed
to achieve this objective [Silva et al., 2021b], and it helped in understanding how
regulatory stimuli were promoted in the context of programming education. At
the same time, significant limitations were observed, such as the limited num-
ber of studies and the excessive focus on scaffolding designed to foster general-
purpose regulatory processes.
The majority of the general-use scaffoldings identified were adapted from scaf-
folds that had already been validated in various educational settings, likely facil-
itating their widespread adoption in early studies on SRL in programming edu-
cation. In contrast, crafting programming-specific scaffoldings presents distinct
challenges, necessitating a deep dive into the unique learning processes within
programming and their interaction with regulatory strategies [Azevedo and Had-
win, 2005]. This complexity underscores the difficulty to design context-specific
regulatory scaffoldings, and a field in which the understanding of how regulatory
processes integrate with programming learning remains limited might suggest
the lower adoption of specialized scaffoldings [Loksa et al., 2022].
133

Chapter 6
The review findings suggest that research on learning regulation in programming
education is still in its early stages, which is an observation that aligns with other
reviews in the field [Garcia et al., 2018a; Prather et al., 2020]. Loksa et al. [2022]
highlighted that studies in programming education that were grounded in the
learning regulation theory frequently lack a deeper discussion about the theory
itself and how it integrates with the specificities of programming education. This
finding helps in comprehending the lower number of regulatory scaffoldings ex-
plicitly tailored for programming education.
The scarcity of studies focusing on socio-regulation is another limitation of this
research area. This result was not surprising, because historically research on
the regulation of learning concentrated on individual aspects of self-regulation
before shifting attention to social contexts [Hadwin et al., 2017]. This transition
is occurring in programming education research [Prather et al., 2022], including
this doctoral dissertation.
Lastly, the findings in the review aided the development of a guideline aimed to
assist researchers and educators in developing regulatory scaffoldings for pro-
gramming education. The guideline presents different formats of scaffoldings
that can be explored in this learning domain and helps in understanding how
self-regulation aligns with programming learning. A detailed presentation of this
scaffolding was published in [Silva et al., 2021b].
6.2 RQ2) How to structure collaborative activities in
programming?
An important part of this doctoral research comprised the study and design of
social regulatory scaffoldings. This process demands an understanding of collab-
orative dynamics and strategies to encourage their appropriate realization [Jär-
venoja et al., 2018]. As the foundation of social regulation lies in interpersonal
interactions [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011], it was necessary to understand how col-
laboration occurs in programming tasks and existing gaps.
A literature review was performed centered on studies that utilized educational
technology to facilitate collaboration in programming education [Silva et al., 2020].
The review revealed different formats of group work in this domain, with a
higher focus on collaborative code development. The studies mostly focused
on the development of tools to allow student collaboration, such as collabora-
tive programming editors and chat features. This approach seems to stem from
the assumption that offering collaborative tools is sufficient for structuring col-
laborative learning. However, this perspective contradicts recent studies in col-
laborative learning, which indicate that students often struggle with organizing
learning strategies and regulating their learning collaboratively, which can im-
pact their academic performance in group work [Lu and Smiles, 2022]. Few stud-
ies offered scaffoldings to stimulate cognitive, metacognitive, motivational, or be-
havioral processes, which limits the understanding of how this can be achieved
in collaborative programming activities.
134

Discussion
Another limitation identified in the analyzed studies is the limited theoretical
discussion on aspects of collaborative learning processes. When viewed from
a self-regulation perspective, only one study mentions the theory, despite its im-
portance for research in collaborative learning [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011]. These
observations align with the findings of Loksa et al. [2022], which pointed out that
many studies prioritize developing technological resources over integrating edu-
cational theories into their pedagogical designs. This lack of theoretical ground-
ing could potentially hinder the effectiveness of these interventions [Azevedo
and Hadwin, 2005].
The findings point to research opportunities, particularly in the development of
scaffoldings that can assist in orchestrating the collaborative process in program-
ming. These resources are necessary, in particular, due to the consistent body of
literature pointing out that regulatory difficulties at both individual and collec-
tive levels negatively impact collaborative learning [Hadwin et al., 2017], and the
use of scaffoldings has the potential to alleviate this problem [Zheng, 2016b]. The
challenge lies in understanding how these scaffoldings can be designed, which
is an expected contribution of this doctoral work and will be discussed in the
following research question.
6.3 RQ3) How can a CSCL be designed to foster regu-
lation of learning among programming students?
The regulatory scaffolding propositions of this doctoral thesis contemplate differ-
ent formats and areas of stimuli, which seems to be a necessity in the program-
ming area that involves a multifaceted set of mental processes [Dayimani and Pa-
dayachee, 2023]. The design of these scaffoldings demanded a deeper exploration
of learning regulation theory to comprehend its application in programming ed-
ucation. In this regard, it was necessary to understand the mechanisms behind
programming learning and align them with regulatory constructs. For example,
programming students often struggle with code creation due to inadequate plan-
ning [Loksa and Ko, 2016]. At the same time, the influence of students’ regulatory
abilities on planning is well-documented in self-regulated learning literature in
other educational domains [Eilam and Aharon, 2003]. Thus, understanding how
the SRL takes part in the planning process helps in the design of scaffoldings
to stimulate cognitive and metacognitive processes relevant to this scope in pro-
gramming education. This example helps illustrate how the SRL could be used
as a basis for supporting programming learning, which was materialized into the
code planner scaffolding.
The design of programming-specific scaffoldings was particularly challenging
because it required a comprehensive understanding of the various mental pro-
cesses involved in the different stages of the programming learning process. Zim-
merman’s model was used as a reference to organize the learning process into
phases, assisting the investigations carried out to understand how programming
learning occurs. This investigation involved reviewing current programming lit-
erature regarding cognitive, metacognitive, motivational, and behavioral pro-
135

Chapter 6
cesses involved in planning, executing, monitoring, and evaluating program-
ming tasks. Frequently, the research also referred to self-regulation literature in
other domains to understand how it could be applied to programming education.
To illustrate, error correction is an action that is typical to programming learning
[Robins, 2019], and the literature points out that through this action students of-
ten experience motivational and emotional issues [Coto et al., 2021]. Moreover,
recognizing and correcting errors also requires the student to have the ability to
recognize areas in which they need to improve [Denny et al., 2020]. These psycho-
logical processes are part of the SRL framework and represented as the constructs
of motivational and metacognitive regulation. Thus, aspects related to the regu-
latory behaviors were used to design resources to assist students in overcoming
their limitations during error correction (e.g., the error analysis scaffolding).
Social regulatory scaffolding design was even more challenging due to its relative
novelty in programming education. Moreover, the literature related to this type
of scaffolding in other domains was relatively scarce compared to individual reg-
ulatory scaffoldings, limiting a broader understanding of the types of stimuli that
could be explored.
The scaffoldings proposed in this doctoral research aimed to help future inves-
tigators interested in the use of the regulation of learning theory. The findings
in this research question provide an understanding of the resources that could
be explored to foster regulatory behavior. Moreover, the theoretical discussion
can give researchers a richer understanding of how learning regulation integrates
within programming education.
6.4 RQ4) How do students use and evaluate the RE-
SPE’s regulatory scaffoldings?
There is a notable gap in the existing literature regarding the evaluation of stu-
dents’ perceptions of regulatory scaffolding usage [Silva et al., 2021b]. This knowl-
edge is important as it reveals whether the student is capable of understanding
what the scaffolding proposes for them to do, and measures the acceptance of
this resource. For instance, if a student struggles to use scaffolding or fails to
recognize its value, their potential impact on learning is diminished [Al-Adwan,
2020].
This research question sought to provide an initial evaluation of how students use
and perceive the adequacy of RESPE’s scaffoldings during programming learn-
ing. A multimodal data collection approach was performed, combining data
from student interactions with the provided scaffolding and qualitative feedback
on their experiences. Analysis of students’ scaffolding usage revealed three dis-
tinct patterns. Firstly, some students showed resistance, responding to prompts
with random characters. Secondly, others faced difficulties using the scaffoldings,
highlighting an unforeseen situation where a resource meant to foster the realiza-
tion of certain mental processes was not able to be used because demanded other
processes. Lastly, a group of students initially used the scaffoldings effectively
136

Discussion
but gradually disengaged. This behavior could indicate a loss of interest or chal-
lenges in maintaining consistent usage.
The qualitative data provided insights into the behaviors observed. Most stu-
dents positively evaluated their experience, acknowledging that the scaffoldings
prompted actions they would not have done spontaneously. However, some ex-
pressed confusion about how to perform certain actions or the relevance of these
actions to their learning process. For example, some students found using the
code planner unproductive, preferring to code without prior planning. This be-
havior has been previously reported [Loksa and Ko, 2016] and highlights a chal-
lenge that researchers and educators interested in the promotion of regulatory
scaffoldings have to face.
Based on the collected data, some recommendations and modifications were made
to the scaffoldings. It is necessary to consider the mental processes required for
the use of a particular scaffolding, and anticipate eventual necessities and de-
sign resources to mitigate difficulties in their use. For example, some students
reported difficulties in understanding and answering the question prompts in
the code planner. To cope with this limitation, this scaffolding was modified and
examples of how the prompts could be answered were provided. Moreover, stu-
dents might have different needs and competencies to use the scaffoldings. For
example, students with prior programming knowledge may have different needs
than novices, and present a higher ability to use programming-specific scaffold-
ings. In this regard, further research is needed to explore the use and challenges
of these scaffoldings.
How students perceive the gains of scaffolding usage can also influence the ac-
ceptance of these resources [Al-Adwan, 2020]. For example, some students re-
ported not being accustomed to planning their code before programming, and
the code planner altered this behavior. Some students recognized the benefits of
this approach, but others were uncertain. Data from students’ interactions also
revealed that simply making the scaffoldings available is insufficient to guaran-
tee their usage, as they avoided some scaffoldings by writing random characters.
These findings highlight the need to think into strategies to encourage students’
usage of the available resources. It is essential to demonstrate how scaffoldings
can enhance study strategies, and training programs could be a strategy to in-
crease awareness of the importance of self-regulation [Volet and Lund, 1994].
Finally, personalizing scaffolding usage could help address the students’ difficul-
ties and perceptions regarding this support [Azevedo and Hadwin, 2005]. Due
to time constraints and scope, this doctorate barely explored personalization, be-
ing a topic for future work. The adoption of scaffolding personalization has its
own set of challenges. For example, how do we constantly measure students’
competencies in performing certain regulatory strategies so that the scaffolding
can fade out? the inclusion or removal of the scaffolding was beneficial? These
aspects deserve investigation.
137

Chapter 6
6.5 RQ5) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit
higher performance in individual programming
exercises than students who did not?
RESPE’s effectiveness in supporting learning was assessed in an experiment com-
prised of three experimental conditions, named ExpSRL (received the support of
regulatory scaffoldings focused on individual regulation), ExpSrlSocialReg (re-
ceived both the support from individual and social regulatory scaffoldings), and
a control group, who did not receive support from regulatory scaffoldings. RE-
SPES’s evaluation in this research question focused on the performance in indi-
vidual tasks that was measured based on the course grade.
The results indicate that students who received regulatory support, whether it
was solely individual or both individual and social, outperformed those in the
control group. This result offers empirical evidence of the effectiveness of regula-
tory scaffolding as a teaching tool for programming education. Given the scarcity
studies in this thematic [Loksa et al., 2022; Silva et al., 2021b], these findings can
help researchers and educators evaluate the adequacy of regulatory scaffoldings.
Another key contribution of this doctoral research is the summarizing of the exist-
ing literature on regulatory scaffoldings in programming education. Besides the
literature review discussed in Section 4.1.1, a meta-analysis was also performed.
The Forest plot presented in Figure 5.1 shows the overall effect size from vari-
ous studies utilizing regulatory scaffoldings. The average effect size was 0.58,
and the effect size for RESPE was 0.85, both representing a significant value in
educational research [Kotrlik et al., 2011].
Evaluating this result requires comparing effect sizes with other interventions.
Table 6.1 presents the average effect size of different interventions obtained by
several meta-analyses in programming education. Although this list is not an
exhaustive compilation, the data serves as an initial benchmark for comparing
various categories of strategies employed to facilitate programming learning.
Table 6.1 demonstrates that regulatory scaffolding is a promising strategy in pro-
gramming education. Its effect size is comparable to other more extensively re-
searched interventions, such as pair programming and project-based learning.
Given that the concept of learning regulation is relatively novel in the context of
programming education [Loksa et al., 2022], there is potential for future research
to enhance the effectiveness of scaffoldings. The design process of RESPE’s scaf-
folding is expected to leverage the theoretical discussion of how regulation of
learning aligns with programming learning and improve the quality of future
scaffoldings.
138

Discussion

6.6 RQ6) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit
higher performance in collaborative programming
exercises than students who did not?
This research question delved into analyzing students’ performance in collabo-
rative activities. Separating the performance analysis of individual and collab-
orative activities was necessary due to their differing dynamics and the distinct
mental processes they require [Stahl, 2004]. Collaborative activities involve peer
interactions that influence cognitive, metacognitive, affective, and motivational
processes in ways that differ from individual learning [Barujel and Groba, 2014;
Panadero et al., 2015].
This research question aimed to understand how different types of regulatory
support (individual vs. individual and social) affected performance in collabora-
tive tasks. The research was driven by the hypothesis that social regulatory scaf-
folding support could enhance performance in collaborative activities [Panadero
and Järvelä, 2015]. This hypothesis is based on research suggesting that collab-
orative activities necessitate regulatory processes of a social nature, distinct, in
part, from self-regulation of learning [Hadwin and Oshige, 2011; Järvelä et al.,
2016; Malmberg et al., 2017; Panadero et al., 2015]. Providing specific support
for these social regulatory processes could be considered a means to orchestrate
collaborative learning [Järvelä et al., 2014].
The findings indicated that students receiving social regulatory support (Exp-
SRLSocialReg) outperformed those who received only SRL scaffolding support
(ExpSRL) and those without support (control group). These findings support the
above hypothesis and highlight the importance of providing regulatory support
for students during collaborative tasks, which is a finding also reported by previ-
ous authors [Liu et al., 2023]. Nonetheless, it is necessary to ponder that existing
evidence is still fragile due to the few studies that have explored this theme. In
139

Chapter 6
addition, it is also necessary to discuss the ways in which stimuli to social regu-
latory processes will be offered, whether in a more structured and directed way
as in this work, or in a less guided format regarding the strategic planning of the
code, as proposed by [Liu et al., 2023].
The results also raise questions about the limitations of individual regulatory
scaffoldings in collaborative tasks. Even though the ExpSRL group scored higher
in individual tasks than both groups, their performance in collaborative tasks
was no different than the control group. Nonetheless, the data does not clar-
ify whether the control group’s performance improved or the ExpSRL group’s
declined. This ambiguity raises a question such as whether students can trans-
fer benefits from individual regulatory scaffoldings to collaborative tasks, which
warrants further investigation.
Finally, future research could focus on how individual and social regulatory pro-
cesses interact during collaborative activities, as SRL is understood as a precursor
to co-regulation and social regulation [Järvelä and Hadwin, 2013]. For social reg-
ulation to be effective, regulatory processes at the individual level (SRL) must
first be established [Hadwin et al., 2017]. Understanding these dynamics in the
context of programming education is essential for comprehending how collabo-
rative learning unfolds.
6.7 RQ7) Do students who used RESPE demonstrate
higher regulatory skills development than students
who did not?
In addition to the analysis of improvement in academic outcomes, research on
the use of regulatory scaffoldings often focuses on improving regulatory skills
[Zheng, 2016a]. This aspect was investigated in this research question. The reg-
ulatory abilities were assessed using the Motivational Strategies for Learning
Questionnaire (MSLQ) and the Self-regulation of Learning Programming Strate-
gies Questionnaire (SPSQ).
The results indicated that none of the groups that participated in the experiment
had improved their scores in these instruments after the experiment. This find-
ing, alongside previous literature reviews [Silva et al., 2021b], suggests that while
regulatory scaffoldings can support learning achievements, their impact on the
development of regulatory skills is less clear. This result raises questions about
whether the scaffoldings were ineffective in supporting SRL development or if
such development requires more prolonged exposure.
Research by Kollar and Fischer [2006] indicates that students respond differently
to regulatory scaffoldings. This variation could be due to personal factors that
influence regulatory skill development, such as metacognitive experiences [Efk-
lides, 2009], interpersonal relationships [Balapumi et al., 2016], parental influence
[Perry, 2019], and cultural factors [Jaramillo et al., 2017]. Many of these influenc-
ing factors are beyond the control of the scaffoldings used in this doctoral work.
140

Discussion
Nonetheless, other aspects, like the duration of exposure to the scaffoldings and
the types of scaffolding used, are within the researchers’ control and can impact
SRL skill development [Devolder et al., 2012]. In this regard, future research is
necessary to explore these factors.
In programming education, the optimal length of exposure to scaffoldings and
the most effective types of scaffoldings remain unclear [Silva et al., 2021b]. To
address these uncertainties, a meta-analysis could be conducted to consolidate
findings from previous studies, using the above-mentioned factors as moderators
in the development of SRL. This approach would help researchers and educators
situate into the factors influencing SRL development.
6.8 RQ8) What is the association between SRL and
programming learning?
Self-regulation of learning has become a pivotal theory in education, backed by a
substantial body of research underscoring its importance in the learning process
[Schunk and Zimmerman, 2023]. There is a well-established link between reg-
ulatory skills and learning across various knowledge domains [Theobald, 2021].
However, in programming education, this understanding remains limited [Loksa
et al., 2022].
Expanding the knowledge about the relationship between SRL abilities and pro-
gramming learning is essential to comprehend their interrelationship. SRL is
considered to have context-specific characteristics that may alter self-regulation
dynamics [Boekaerts, 2002]. This research aimed to investigate the relationship
between SRL and programming education using a mixed-method approach. This
process involved our own data and a combination with the findings from pub-
lished literature.
As discussed in RQ7, participants of the RESPE experiment answered the MSLQ
and SPSQ questionnaires. The analysis revealed only a weak correlation be-
tween regulatory skills and programming learning outcomes, challenging the ex-
pected stronger association seen in other educational domains [Zimmerman and
Schunk, 2011]. However, in another study involving Brazilian and Macanese un-
dergraduate programming students, a higher association was found [Silva et al.,
2021a], suggesting that SRL might exert variable impacts across different edu-
cational settings. Nonetheless, the findings seemed consistent across the under-
graduate settings. At the same time, these findings also hint at the potential need
for further psychometric validation to ensure these instruments’ reliability across
various learning environments.
MSLQ’s suitability across different contexts has also been questioned [Hamid and
Singaram, 2016; Jackson, 2018]. Despite its wide use in programming education
[Loksa et al., 2022], the adequacy of this instrument in this educational context
remains unclear due to the lack of psychometric validation. To illustrate, in a pre-
vious systematic literature review, Silva et al. [2021a] found that the MSLQ cog-
nitive strategies scale showed low correlations with programming performance
141

Chapter 6
[Souza et al., 2017]. This finding contradicts a body of literature that points to the
opposite, regarding the importance of cognitive strategies to programming learn-
ing [Robins, 2019]. Thus, one hypothesis is that the MSLQ cognitive strategy scale
may not effectively capture the most relevant strategies to programming educa-
tion. The SPSQ usage raises higher concerns due to the lack of psychometric val-
idation, including the authors’ original publication. Although validation efforts
were examined in this doctorate (Section 6.10), further validations are necessary.
Pondering the instruments’ limitations due to lack of psychometric validity, the
findings from the review suggest that some regulatory constructs are associated
with programming learning outcomes [Silva et al., 2021a]. For example, metacog-
nition and self-efficacy showed higher association, including consistent results
across studies in different educational settings. On the contrary, the cognitive
strategies measured by the MSLQ exhibited a lower correlation. A possible ex-
planation for this outcome is that programming learning requires specific cogni-
tive strategies that the general ones measured by the MSLQ do not adequately
capture.
One emerging approach to studying SRL is using multimodal data, which is ade-
quate to capture the complexities of the SRL process [Järvelä et al., 2021]. Section
6.9 presents a contribution in this regard, in which students’ self-report data was
combined with analysis of their interactions within the RESPE environment. The
results offer a different perspective on the effectiveness of regulatory scaffoldings,
as they point out that their usage correlated with higher performance [Silva et al.,
2024a]. This evidence, combined with the data from experiments, indicates the
relevance of the regulation of the learning theory in supporting the designing of
effective pedagogical resources for programming education [Silva et al., 2021b].
Although a distinction exists between SRL aptitudes (e.g., measured by ques-
tionnaires) and SRL scaffoldings, multiple evidence are presented in this doctoral
work and in the literature, pointing out that this learning theory can have a signif-
icant contribution to designing educational software for learning programming.
We argue that this data partially contributes to understanding the importance of
SRL to programming education.
6.9 RQ9) What regulatory strategies are used by pro-
gramming students?
Studying the learning strategies employed by students is a vital aspect of re-
search in the field of self-regulation of learning [Bown, 2009]. It provides insights
into how learning occurs and the regulatory strategies utilized [Zimmerman and
Schunk, 2011]. This research question aligns with these goals, aiming to compre-
hend the learning strategies students employ at different stages of programming
education.
The findings revealed that learning programming is a multifaceted process in-
volving numerous psychological processes. Students often employ a mix of reg-
ulatory strategies at various stages of their learning, highlighting the complex
142

Discussion
nature of regulation in this domain. For example, cognitive resources were typ-
ically mentioned to understand the problem and plan the code structure, while
metacognitive strategies focus on self-review and error identification. Motiva-
tional regulation was also observed, such as knowing how to deal with challenges
and acknowledging successes.
The study presents evidence that programming learning involves a combination
of general-use and programming-specific regulatory strategies, supporting the
observations of Falkner et al. [2014]. For example, in the planning phase, stu-
dents often use general strategies like re-reading the problem and breaking it
down, forming the foundation for using programming-specific strategies like de-
termining necessary programming resources. Compared to previous literature,
this doctorate reported a set of new regulatory strategies, helping gain a deeper
understanding of SRL in this domain.
Comparing the strategies externalized by low and high performers suggests that
this latter group have a higher emphasis on programming-specific strategies,
aligning with the results reported by Falkner et al. [2015]. This finding indicates
the potential significance of programming-specific strategies in learning, but fur-
ther research remains necessary to determine the most effective strategies. Ad-
ditional evidence on the impact of programming-specific strategies on learning
outcomes is also crucial. Furthermore, it is also necessary to investigate the de-
pendency that programming-specific strategies could have on general-purpose
regulatory abilities.
Analysis of student-reported strategies shows parallels with prior research. Falkner
et al. [2015] also categorized strategies into programming-specific and general-
purpose, although slight variations in the specific strategies exist. These differ-
ences could stem from the inherent complexity of SRL research. Factors such
as the learning environment, student characteristics, foundation SRL model or
methodological procedures could influence these differences across studies [Schunk
and Zimmerman, 2013].
There is also variability in the level of detail of how the regulatory strategies are
described. For instance, Prather et al. [2022] identified the categories of regu-
latory strategies without detailing specific actions (e.g., time management). On
the contrary, Falkner et al. [2015], and this doctorate provided more granular de-
scriptions, including actions like task prioritization under time management. Re-
searchers exploring the application of learning regulation theory in programming
education should consider these methodological nuances as they can affect stu-
dent responses and the subsequent identification of strategies.
The work presented by Ferreira and Campos [2023] also helps to situate the find-
ings of this research. They also identified that a significant portion of students
have a small repertoire of regulation strategies, lacking in particular specific pro-
gramming strategies. Their results of are also important in offering an overview
of the strategies used for co-regulation, expanding knowledge on this topic.
Finally, a contribution of this doctorate was the proposition of a conceptual frame-
work to categorize the identified regulatory strategies. This framework helps or-
ganize the understanding regarding the multiple strategies present during learn-
143

Chapter 6
ing and their usage by high and low performers.
6.10 RQ10) What is the psychometric validity of self-
regulation measurement instruments in the con-
text of programming education?
Measuring regulatory skills is a critical aspect of research based on the theory
of the regulation of learning [Hilpert et al., 2013]. Questionnaires are typically
used for this purpose, but their use requires thorough psychometric validation to
ensure its validity [Roth et al., 2015]. In this sense, the Self-regulation of Learn-
ing Programming Strategies Questionnaire (SPSQ) [Lu et al., 2017] has potential
usage in the field due to the lack of context-specific instruments, but the lack of
psychometric validation hinders its usage.
In this research question, a psychometric evaluation of the SPSQ was performed.
The findings revealed that the instrument has psychometric issues but had im-
portant results, in particular regarding its internal consistency and validity re-
garding the comparison with other regulatory questionnaires, such as the MSLQ
[Silva et al., 2022]. Although both instruments have comparable outcomes, the
SPSQ focuses on programming-specific strategies, which may be more suitable
for research in this area. It allows for identifying gaps in students’ programming
abilities, such as planning code development. This data can be used as a moni-
toring tool for researchers and educators.
The instrument can also serve as a diagnostic tool to assess students’ regulatory
capabilities in classroom contexts. However, it is important to consider the psy-
chometric issues and the fact that the SPSQ lacks constructs related to motivation
and self-efficacy. A more recent alternative is the use of the Characterize Learn-
ing Strategies in Computer Programming (CEAPC) questionnaire proposed by
Torres Jiménez et al. [2023]. This instrument is also based on the constructs of
the self-regulation of learning theory and comprises a wider range of regulatory
constructs than the SPSQ.
144

Chapter 7
Conclusion
This doctoral thesis intersects three distinct yet complementary areas: program-
ming education, the use of computers in education, and self-regulation of learn-
ing (SRL). Programming education is a frequently researched topic due to the dif-
ficulties students often face [Qian and Lehman, 2017]. Among the many existing
strategies to support learning in this subject, the use of technology is a recognized
approach due to its effectiveness [Chen et al., 2018]. However, a fundamental el-
ement to the success of technologies in education is their pedagogical theoretical
foundation, and self-regulation theory meets this aspect by providing a basis for
understanding learning processes and design scaffoldings through a regulatory
perspective [Greene and Azevedo, 2011].
The self-regulation of learning theory gained prominence in the late 1980s and
highlighted the importance of students’ active role for their academic success
[Zimmerman and Schunk, 2011]. This theory has since been influential to study
the learning processes across several subjects, and also to provide a pedagogical
foundation for the design of computational learning environments [Azevedo and
Aleven, 2013]. In this sense, regulatory scaffoldings are used through technolog-
ical resources to foster learners’ independence and support their metacognitive,
cognitive, motivational, and emotional regulation.
Incorporating self-regulation into the design of educational technology environ-
ments to support programming learning is not a straightforward task [Garcia
et al., 2018a]. It requires an understanding of how regulatory processes occur
within a specific educational domain, which involves the intersection of different
theories and pedagogical practices that need to be aligned [Azevedo and Had-
win, 2005]. This process extends beyond the technology development itself and
includes conceptualization and theoretical grounding to establish its pedagogical
bases, encompassing an understanding of regulatory processes. These elements
inform the design of regulatory scaffoldings.
During this doctoral work, an effort was made to build a theoretical foundation
concerning learning regulation and programming learning processes. This was
achieved not only through summarizations of the current state of literature but
also by producing our own data. New evidence is necessary to expand the ex-
isting, but limited body of knowledge in the existing literature on self-regulation
145

Chapter 7
and programming learning. The collected knowledge supported the develop-
ment of a set of regulatory scaffoldings, which are one of the contributions of this
work. From a practical perspective, these scaffoldings have a direct usage by re-
searchers and educators interested in developing interventions grounded in the
regulation of learning theory. Moreover, the body of knowledge originated from
this thesis and materialized through the systematic literature reviews, guidelines,
framework, and statistical data presented in this work are expected to support fu-
ture research on this topic.
7.1 Summary of findings
The answers to the research questions that guided this work are discussed in
detail in Section 6. This section provides an overview of the main findings dis-
covered through this doctorate.
Research questions RQ1) Which regulatory scaffoldings are used in programming ed-
ucation? (Section 4.1.1) and RQ2) How to structure collaborative activities in pro-
gramming? (Section 4.1.2) aimed to understand the current state of the art related
to topics of interest to this doctorate: the use of self-regulation in programming
education and collaborative learning. The findings indicate that the use of SRL
theory in programming education is still in its earlier stages, which can be ob-
served in the lower number of identified studies, and in the limited discussion
of how this theory aligns with the programming learning processes. Research
in collaborative learning in programming education is more mature, but still a
scarcity of studies exploring the social regulation of learning processes.
The limited knowledge regarding the use of SRL theory, and social regulation, in
programming education research limits the understanding of how to design ed-
ucational software to foster regulatory processes in the programming domain.
This finding motivated the development of RQ3) How can a CSCL be designed
to foster regulation of learning among programming students? , presented in Section
4.1.3. The set of proposed regulatory scaffoldings were incorporated into an
computer-supported collaborative learning (CSCL) named Regulatory Support
for Programming Education (RESPE) .
Scaffolding design proved challenging to understand how the SRL constructs
could support the diverse learning situations experienced in programming learn-
ing. An alignment between several learning theories, related to SRL and pro-
gramming education, was necessary and served as a basis in the development
of the proposed regulatory scaffoldings. The knowledge built in the design de-
cisions that motivated the scaffolding development (Section 4) is expected to aid
educators and researchers interested in this theme.
Some concerns arose during the development process of these scaffoldings. At
this stage of the investigation, there was only the hypothesis that the support
offered through these scaffoldings would be helpful in learning. However, there
were uncertainties about students’ capabilities to use the provided resources and
their perceptions of these scaffoldings. An initial evaluation was necessary to
146

Conclusion
validate these concerns, in particular before the conduction of the experiment.
Thus, the research question RQ4) How do students use and evaluate the RESPE’s
regulatory scaffoldings? guided this evaluative process as a pilot study (Section
4.2.1).
The findings indicated that while scaffoldings were recognized as valuable, there
were challenges in their adoption. Some students encountered difficulties in us-
ing the resources, whereas others failed to see their potential benefits. These ob-
servations suggested that modifications to the scaffoldings were necessary to ad-
dress certain students’ usage challenges, and highlighted that the mere availabil-
ity of support does not ensure its effective utilization. Resistance to adopting new
learning strategies was noted among some students, who might prefer familiar
approaches. Therefore, it is crucial to educate learners about the significance of
self-regulation and how the offered scaffolding can enhance their learning expe-
rience, emphasizing the importance of embracing these supports for educational
success.
Modifications in the software were performed and RESPE’s empirical evaluation
was performed and described in RQ5) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit
higher performance in individual programming exercises than students who did not?
(Section 4.3.2). This research question focused on an analysis of students’ individ-
ual performance, whereas the RQ6) Do students who used the RESPE exhibit higher
performance in collaborative programming exercises than students who did not? (Sec-
tion 4.3.3) assessed the performance in collaborative exercises. The findings from
both research questions highlight the potential usage of regulatory scaffoldings
to enhance learning programming, corroborating the importance of SRL theory
as a pedagogical support to design computational scaffoldings.
Section 4.3.4 presents the results of RQ7) Do students who used RESPE demonstrate
higher regulatory skills development than students who did not? , which sought to in-
vestigate students’ development of regulatory skills through the RESPE usage.
The findings indicate that regulatory scaffoldings are limited in fostering SRL
abilities development. Comparing the findings with others from the program-
ming literature and also in other educational domains, it is observed that the
development of these skills through interventions is still not a well-understood
process.
A significant part of this doctorate sought to build and summarize the evidence
in existing literature regarding the association between SRL and programming
learning, which is an important step in assessing the importance of this theory
[Zimmerman and Martinez-Pons, 1988]. Section 5.1 presents the findings of the
RQ8) What is the association between SRL and programming learning? . The results
indicate that certain constructs from self-regulation theory correlate with pro-
gramming learning, and that offering regulatory scaffoldings can enhance this
learning. However, the limited number of studies constrains broader conclusions.
Additional research is needed to evaluate constructs that did not show an associ-
ation with learning outcomes, ensuring a more comprehensive understanding of
the impact of self-regulation in programming education.
Part of the understanding of how SRL theory aligns with programming learning
147

Chapter 7
involves investigating the learning strategies used by students. Understanding
these strategies helps in comprehending the resources students utilize for learn-
ing programming. In this sense, RQ9) What regulatory strategies are used by pro-
gramming students? was proposed and detailed in Section 5.2. The findings high-
light that learning programming entails a diverse set of mental processes and
reveal distinctions in how students with varying performance levels manifest
these strategies. Although this research area is nascent, initial data hint at the
possibility of discerning more effective from less effective strategies, suggesting
a promising avenue for further exploration and understanding of the strategic
approaches students employ in programming learning.
Finally, adopting SRL theory in programming education research depends on the
availability of instruments to measure SRL skills [Zimmerman and Pons, 1986].
The Self-regulation of Learning Programming Strategies Questionnaire (SPSQ) is
an initiative in this direction [Lu et al., 2017]. However, the lack of psychometric
validation weakened its use, and RQ10) What is the psychometric validity of self-
regulation measurement instruments in the context of programming education? aimed
to mitigate this issue (Section 5.3). The findings partially supported the adequacy
of this instrument but revealed inconsistencies that can impact its usage.
7.2 Threats to validity
The correct interpretation of the results of this doctoral dissertation should ac-
knowledge the potential validity threats that could affect the findings. Various
biases might arise from the chosen research methodologies and the inherent fea-
tures of the study settings. This section aims to identify these possible biases, out-
lining how they might influence the outcomes and underlining the importance of
considering these factors when evaluating the research conclusions.
The scaffoldings evaluated in this research were tested in an experimental en-
vironment that might not fully replicate real-world scenarios. The inherent ex-
perimental design of the study introduces potential validity threats, though ef-
forts were made to mitigate these concerns. Utilizing a control group offered a
comparative baseline to more effectively discern the impact of the independent
variable, which in this case is the usage of scaffoldings. Moreover, implementing
randomization aimed to minimize selection bias, enhancing the study’s reliability
in gauging the actual influence of the scaffoldings on learning outcomes.
The study’s sample may also affect the validity of the results. It is important to
consider the characteristics of the participants and the number required for suffi-
cient statistical power [Ewert and Sibthorp, 2009]. Although measures were taken
to secure an adequate sample size to support statistical analysis, the sample size
in this doctoral research may constrain the broader applicability of the results.
The instruments used for measuring self-regulation skills could also introduce
bias [Souza et al., 2017]. Both the MSLQ and SPSQ, as examined in this dis-
sertation, have acknowledged psychometric limitations. To draw more precise
conclusions, additional validations of these tools are warranted. Moreover, the
148

Conclusion
impact of using translated versions of these instruments on their psychometric
properties warrants careful consideration [Souza et al., 2017]. Initial evaluations
conducted within this thesis suggest that certain metrics, like internal consistency,
align with those of the original instruments, providing some level of confidence
in their use.
7.3 Future work
This section delves into areas with potential for further investigation in the short
and long term.
7.3.1 Further research on the use of self-regulated learning scaf-
foldings in programming education
The literature reviews identified fewer number of studies using SRL as a foun-
dation theory in the programming education field. This also extends to related
areas, such as collaborative learning, where there has also been little theoretical
representation regarding social regulation. Consequently, gaps were observed
related to how to design scaffoldings to stimulate regulatory processes, both in-
dividually and at a social level, and their impact on programming learning.
There is a need to explore regulatory support that can be offered during code
creation. This doctorate unveils several possibilities, predominantly focusing on
cognitive and metacognitive processes. However, the field needs further explo-
ration, encompassing a broader array of scaffolding formats and delving into ad-
ditional stimulus areas, including motivational and emotional regulation sup-
ports.
7.3.2 Understanding how students use the scaffoldings
This doctorate explored how students used scaffoldings only to a limited extent.
It is necessary to investigate students’ perceptions of the use of these resources,
potential limiting factors, and strategies to mitigate difficulties. Future research
should delve deeper into this, as the results can inform improvements in the ped-
agogical design of these tools.
7.3.3 Investigate scaffolding usage from the lens of the cognitive
load theory
Integrating regulatory scaffoldings into the code development process was chal-
lenging because programming requires multiple mental resources that often oc-
curs simultaneously [Prather et al., 2020]. Therefore, the impact of incorporating
149

Chapter 7
regulatory scaffoldings and students’ capabilities to process them during code de-
velopment is still little understood. One possibility is to study this phenomenon
through the lens of Cognitive Load Theory [Berssanette and de Francisco, 2022],
and further research is needed to understand how regulatory support impacts
cognitive load.
7.3.4 Investigating strategies to incentives scaffolding usage
Some students did not perceive a need for regulatory support, highlighting that
providing scaffolding alone does not guarantee its adoption. Further research is
needed to understand the factors influencing students’ engagement with regula-
tory scaffoldings, and strategies to incentivize their usage.
7.3.5 Assessing the individual contribution of each scaffolding
to learning
Evidence supports that regulatory scaffoldings can be an effective resource to
support programming learning. However, most studies, including this doctor-
ate, use a combination of several scaffoldings, which makes it difficult to discern
individual impacts. Therefore, the individual contribution of each scaffolding to
learning is unclear. Future research should isolate scaffolding usage to evaluate
them individually.
7.3.6 Investigating the regulatory strategies that emerge during
co-regulation and socially shared regulation of learning
This doctorate presented significant contributions into how programming stu-
dents regulate themselves. However, social interaction that takes place during
co-regulation and shared regulation of learning triggers a unique set of regula-
tory strategies [Malmberg et al., 2017]. Therefore, further research is needed to
understand how these processes unfold in the field of programming, and the
works by [Prather et al., 2022] and [Ferreira and Campos, 2023] offer initial paths
for this understanding.
7.3.7 Identifying the most and least beneficial regulatory strate-
gies for programming learning
This doctoral study identified a range of regulatory strategies students exter-
nalized during programming learning. However, few studies explored this as-
pect and there is a need to expand this knowledge and help determine which
strategies are most effective. The conceptual framework presented in Section 5.2
presents an opportunity for researchers interested in this topic.
150

Conclusion
7.3.8 Investigating the factors that contribute to the development
of regulatory skills
The students who participated in the study did not improve their regulatory
skills. Current literature presents different results in this regard, with some re-
searchers reporting improvements in self-regulation after exposure to scaffold-
ings, while others did not report gains [Silva et al., 2021b]. Thus, it is necessary
to investigate the factors that lead to improvements in the development of self-
regulated learning. Potential factors includes exposure duration to scaffolding,
resources used (e.g., question-based, graphics), and stimulated mental processes
(e.g., cognitive, metacognitive), but data is necessary.
7.3.9 Investigating the influence of SRL on Social Regulation
Research indicates the importance of SRL processes for engaging in collaborative
activities [Lin et al., 2016]. However, the influence of SRL on social regulation in
collaborative programming education remains underexplored. Thus, investigat-
ing the regulatory processes that emerge during collaborative learning is vital,
such as the work of [Prather et al., 2022].
7.3.10 Scaffolding personalization
The data on students’ perceptions of the scaffolding usage indicate that some of
them experienced different levels of difficulty and acceptance of these resources.
Scaffolding personalization represents an opportunity to mitigate these issues
[Azevedo and Aleven, 2013], in which regulatory support can be omitted in cer-
tain situations, such as when the learner has assimilated its realization, or pro-
vided when needed. However, some challenges exist in this process and call
for further research, such as how to balance the provision of scaffolding with its
timely removal, which demands real-time monitoring of students’ regulatory ca-
pabilities. The use of artificial intelligence and machine learning techniques can
be used in such scenarios [Carreira et al., 2022; Wu, 2023].
7.3.11 Long-term effects of the provision of regulatory scaffold-
ings
The provision of regulatory scaffoldings was beneficial to students. However,
whether this effect persists when the scaffoldings are no longer available is un-
known. In this sense, a longitudinal evaluation is important to assess student’s
performance after some time has passed since the scaffolding removal.
151

Chapter 7
7.3.12 Validation and construction of instruments to measure self-
regulated learning
Existing instruments currently have psychometric issues that may compromise
their use in the programming education domain. Therefore, future validations
of the MSLQ and SPSQ are necessary as this is a fundamental step to ensure
their use. Constructing a new instrument that measures specific regulatory skills
in programming education is also desirable to cope with the SPSQ limitations.
Section 6.10 presents some factors that this instrument should consider.
7.3.13 Use of multimodal data collection procedures
SRL embodies a multifaceted construct that is manifested in different ways [Zim-
merman and Schunk, 2011]. Traditional self-report questionnaires, such as ques-
tionnaires, capture students’ perceptions of their regulatory practices rather than
their actual behaviors [Roth et al., 2015]. To enrich the understanding of SRL, it
is advantageous to employ diverse data collection methodologies that integrate
self-reports [Järvelä et al., 2021] with additional measures capturing different di-
mensions of regulation, akin to the approach in this dissertation which tracked
students’ engagement with regulatory scaffolds.
Given the earlier exploration of SRL and social regulation within programming
education, adopting a multimodal data collection strategy opens avenues to deepen
our comprehension of these critical learning processes.
7.3.14 Research on motivational and emotional regulation
Research on self-regulation often focuses on the cognitive and metacognitive pro-
cesses [Ergen and Kanadli, 2017; Silva et al., 2021b]. However, a strong body of
knowledge points out that motivation is a critical resource for learners, and for
SRL to occur, as they must be engaged to nurture the intention to self-regulate
[Duckworth et al., 2009; Silva et al., 2023a]. Research can investigate motivational
and emotional regulation in programming activities, and explore scaffoldings to
foster their realization. Additionally, as pointed out by Bosch et al. [2013], the use
of scaffoldings not directly created for nurturing motivation can have an impact
on students’ motivation. This aspect deserves further research, and also how the
impact of scaffolding fading out can have on motivation.
7.4 Final considerations
Building computational learning environments with regulatory scaffoldings to
support programming learning has proven to be a challenging process. The lim-
ited data on the learning regulation process in programming education, and the
scarce number of previous studies highlight a field in need of further research.
152

Conclusion
The work performed in this doctorate sought to extrapolate beyond the techno-
logical issues often present in educational software development, to incorporate
a deeper study of the theoretical aspects of self-regulation, social regulation, and
programming learning. This theoretical foundation was a basis for the construc-
tion of regulatory scaffoldings presented in this doctorate. We argue that it is not
possible to dissociate the construction of educational software from the body of
educational knowledge required for such a task.
The journey taken in this doctoral work highlights that the development of ed-
ucational technologies intersects with the fields of education and cognitive psy-
chology. Given the complexities involved, this thesis aims to offer contributions
in multiple areas, not only to the computing field but also to help understand
the self-regulation of the learning process in general. Finally, as a final contribu-
tion, the RESPE’s source code is available for researchers and educators interested
in their use in the following repository: https://github.com/lsoaresesilva/
respe.

Appendices
189

Appendix A
Course syllabus
191

Appendix A
Instructional content Learning goals
1. First steps in the
programming world
Understand what a program is and how it is devel-
oped;
2. Introduction to
computer algorithms
Comprehend what an algorithm is, its parts, and ex-
amples in our daily lives;
3. Storing data in our
software
Understand what a variable is and the different data
type that it can store;
4. Using variables in
Python
Recognize the variable syntax declaration in Python;
Perform mathematical and String operations in variables;
5. Reading and pre-
senting data to users
Know how to use the instructions for reading and pre-
senting data.
6. Programming er-
rors
Comprehend what a programming error is; interpret
Python’s errors message and how to solve them.
7. Introduction to
conditionals
Understand the concept of conditionals; the situations
in which this resource should be used, and the if syn-
tax.
8. Else instruction Understand the cases in which the else is necessary
and its syntax.
9. Elif instruction Comprehend the cases in which the elif is necessary;
recognize the differences between if, elif and else;
know how to use the elif syntax.
10. Logical operators Understand what logical operators are, when they
should be used, and their syntax.
11. Introduction to
loops
Understand the concept of loops and the situations in
which this resource should be used.
12 Creating loops
with for
Know how to use the for instruction; understand the
concept of iterable and the use of the range function.
13. Creating loops
with while
Know how to use the while instruction and its syntax.
14. Introduction to
functions
Understand the concept of functions in programming;
Know the Python ready-to-use functions and the parts
that make up the execution of a function.
15. Function creation Know how to identify the cases that demand a func-
tion and the syntax for their creation.
16. Using arguments
in functions
Understand what an argument is; Know the multi-
ple types of arguments in Python; Create and execute
functions with arguments.
17. Returning data
from functions
Understand what a return is; Know the differences
between functions with and without return; Compre-
hend the variable scope in Python and its relationship
with return.
192

2. Quando estou criando um programa, certifico-me de concentrar na progra-
mação e não pensar em outras coisas
3. Quando estou criando um programa, eu realmente não me certifico de focar
na programação e não pensar em outras coisas (R)

6. Antes de criar um programa, eu planejo quais as entradas e saídas para ele

8. Ao criar um programa, verifico se o que implementei se adequa ao que
havia planejado antes
9. Ao criar um programa, verifico o que me foi pedido para determinar se o
que implementei está relacionado à essas demandas
10. Após criar uma parte do programa, leio o código para determinar se o que
implementei estava bom
11. No final da criação de um programa, eu não volto para ver se tudo estava
bem. (R)
